intervals such that one of the following two conditions hold
10
10

10

For two intervals
I_{1}=\langle\!\langle i,d_{1}\rangle\!\rangle
and
I_{2}=\langle\!\langle i,d_{2}\rangle\!\rangle
with
d_{1}>d_{2}>0
(i. e.
I_{2}
is a
nonempty prefix of
I_{1}
), we will write
I_{1}\setminus I_{2}
to denote the interval
\langle\!\langle i+d_{2},d_{1}-d_{2}\rangle\!\rangle
.
, call this pair of conditions (
*
):

1.

q[L^{*}],q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]\leq t
; or

2.

q[L^{*}],q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]\geq t
.

If the first condition holds, we conclude the proof as follows. Since
p[I]=p[L]+p[R]
and

p[I]=q[I^{*}]\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)\geq t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
,
it must be that either
p[L]\geq\frac{1}{2}t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
or

p[R]\geq\frac{1}{2}t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
. In the first case,
L
satisfies the
required conditions. In the second case, since
q[R^{*}]\leq q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]\leq t
,
we conclude that
R
satisfies the required conditions and we are done.

If the second condition holds (which in particular implies that
L^{*},I^{*}\setminus L^{*}

are nonempty), note that since
p[I]=p[L]+p[R]
and

q[I^{*}]=q[L^{*}]+q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]
, we have

\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)=\frac{p[I]}{q[I^{*}]}=\frac{p[L]+p[R]}{q[L^{*}]+q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]}\leq\max\left\{\frac{p[L]}{q[L^{*}]},\frac{p[R]}{q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]}\right\}\,.

If
\frac{p[L]}{q[L^{*}]}\geq\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
, then since
q[L^{*}]\geq t
,
we conclude that
L
contradicts the minimality of
I
. Therefore we must have

\frac{p[R]}{q[R^{*}]}\geq\frac{p[R]}{q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]}\geq\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
.
Now, if
q[R^{*}]\leq t
, then
R
satisfies the required conditions, since we have

p[R]\geq q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}]\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)\geq t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
.
Otherwise, if
q[R^{*}]>t
, then
R
contradicts the minimality of
I
.
This completes the proof in the first case.

Case 2.
In the second case, we have that every partition
I=L\cup R
with nonempty
L
and
R
fails
both of the conditions in (
*
). Write
I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle
where, as previously remarked,
d\geq 2
. Consider the sequences of circular intervals
L_{1},\dotsc,L_{d-1}
given by
L_{j}\vcentcolon=\langle\!\langle i,j\rangle\!\rangle
, and write
R_{j}\vcentcolon=\langle\!\langle i+j,d-j\rangle\!\rangle=I\setminus L_{i}
, so
that
L^{*}_{j}=\langle\!\langle i,j-1\rangle\!\rangle
and
R^{*}_{j}=\langle\!\langle i+j,d-j-1\rangle\!\rangle
. Then each
L_{j}\cup R_{j}
for
j\in[d-1]
is a partition of
I
with nonempty

L_{j}
and
R_{j}
, which therefore must fail the two conditions in (
*
).

Now observe that
q[L^{*}_{j}]
is non-decreasing with
j
and
q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{j}],q[R^{*}_{j}]

are non-increasing with
j
, while
q[L^{*}_{1}]=q[\emptyset]=0
and hence

q[L^{*}_{1}]\leq q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{1}]
. Fix the maximum index
j\in[d-1]
satisfying

q[L^{*}_{j}]\leq q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{j}]
. We claim that
q[L^{*}_{j}],q[R^{*}_{j}]\leq t
.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
q[L^{*}_{j}]>t
. By the selection of
j
, we have

q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{j}]>t
. But then the partition
I=L_{j}\cup R_{j}
satisfies the second
condition in (
*
), a contradiction. So we have verified that
q[L^{*}_{j}]\leq t
.

Now assume for the sake of contradiction that
q[R^{*}_{j}]>t
. Then
R^{*}_{j}
is nonempty, implying
that
j<d-1
, and one can verify that
R^{*}_{j}=I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{j+1}
. Therefore

q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{j+1}]>t
. If
q[L^{*}_{j+1}]\geq t
, then the partition

I=L_{j+1}\cup R_{j+1}
satisfies the second condition in (
*
), again a contradiction.
We may therefore assume that
q[L^{*}_{j+1}]<t
. But this implies that

q[L^{*}_{j+1}]<q[I^{*}\setminus L^{*}_{j+1}]
, contradicting the maximality of our choice of
j
.
Therefore we have verified that
q[R^{*}_{j}]\leq t
.

Finally, recall that
p[L_{j}]+p[R_{j}]=p[I]\geq q[I^{*}]\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)\geq t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
, and therefore either

p[L_{j}]\geq\frac{1}{2}t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
or

p[R_{j}]\geq\frac{1}{2}t\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)
.
Since
q[L^{*}_{j}],q[R^{*}_{j}]\leq t
, it follows that either
L_{j}
or
R_{j}
satisfies the
required conditions.
∎

3
Testing Uniformity in the Confused Collector Model

Following the setup from
Section

2
, we consider the task of
testing uniformity of an unknown distribution
p
over the vertices
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
of a base graph

G=(\mathbb{Z}_{n},E)
in the confused collector model. Here,
G
is the path or the cycle and every
edge
(i,i+1)\in E
has weight
w(e)=\eta
, where
\eta
is the resolution parameter.

Our analysis will treat the cases of the cycle and the path in a unified presentation. In the case
of the cycle,
E
contains all
n
edges connecting each vertex
i
to
i+1
(mod
n
), and the
set of relevant intervals is
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{cycle}}
. We will write
\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{cycle}}
for the
corresponding join matrix.
In the case of the path,
E
does not contain an edge between
0
and
n-1
, the set of relevant
intervals is
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{path}}
, and the join matrix is
\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{path}}
. When a
result depends on the choice of domain, we will explicitly state the domain under consideration.

The tester is
Algorithm

1
, and consists of two steps:

1.

Concentration test: checks whether any count in the sample is too large;
this case corresponds to highly concentrated distributions, which can be rejected.

2.

Collision-based test: accept or reject depending on whether the test statistic
\bm{Y}
is
below a certain threshold.

Algorithm 1

Uniformity tester in the confused collector model.

Set
m\leftarrow c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\log^{2}n}{\eta^{3/2}}
.

Constants:

\alpha,\beta,L,c>0
to be defined later.

Requires:

\eta\geq\frac{L\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}
.

1:

procedure

UniformityTester-ConfusedCollector
(
p,n,\epsilon,\eta
)

2:

Let
X=(X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n})
be the variables defined in

Section

2

for a sample of size
\mathsf{Poi}(m)
from
p
.

3:

If

\max_{i}X_{i}\geq\alpha\log n
then
reject
.

4:

Y\leftarrow\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i}X_{i}(X_{i}-1)
.

5:

If

Y\geq\frac{m}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}+\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta

then
reject
.

6:

Accept
.

Remark on the optimality of the collision-based tester.

Considering that we give a
Poissonized tester whose main statistic
\bm{Y}
is equivalent to the collision-based statistic of

[
GR00
]
when
\eta=1
, it may seem surprising that we claim a sample complexity of

\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2}\right)
— as opposed to

O\left(\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{4}\right)
— when it is known that, for an analysis based on
bounding the variance of
\bm{Y}
and applying Chebyshev’s inequality, establishing the optimal
sample complexity is only possible with a different test statistic (e. g. the modified chi-squared
statistic
[
CDVV14
,
DKN15b
,
VV17a
]
) or a careful analysis of the non-Poissonized
tester
[
DGPP19
]
(see also the Remark in Section 2 therein). Our analysis implicitly avoids this
issue via our relative concentration test, which upper bounds
\|p\|_{\infty}
,
but another way to resolve the apparent conflict is to notice that dropping this extra test and
specializing our proof to the case
\eta=1
would only incur a dependence on
1/\epsilon^{4}
, rather
than
\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{4}
; and since our analysis only handles the case
\epsilon\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{-1/4}\right)
, the term
\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2}
dominates
1/\epsilon^{4}
.

3.1
Easy Case: Highly Concentrated Distributions

We would like to call distribution
p
“highly concentrated” if it contains too much mass in a
small contiguous range of the vertices
V
.
The tester will detect the highly concentrated distributions and reject,
while non-highly concentrated distributions are well-behaved in our analysis of the variance of the
main test statistic.
Concretely, we define highly concentrated distributions by imposing
a threshold on the relative concentration
\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;w)
introduced in

Section

2
, where
w
is the constant vector of edge weights given
by
w(e)=\eta
.

Definition 3.1

(Highly concentrated distributions)
.

Given a constant
C>0
, positive integer
m
, resolution parameter
\eta
, and probability
distribution
p
over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, we say that
p
is
C
-highly concentrated (under resolution

\eta
with respect to
m
)
if
\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;w)\geq\frac{C\log^{2}n}{m}
, where

t=\frac{1}{\log n}
.

One may think of this definition as follows: the average size of a bucket (connected component)
with resolution
\eta
is
\approx 1/\eta
. In such an interval
I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle
with

I^{*}=\langle\!\langle i,d-1\rangle\!\rangle
, we obtain
w[I^{*}]\approx 1
. If
p[I]/w[I^{*}]\geq\poly\log(n)/m
,
then the sample
\mathsf{Poi}(mp[I])
ought to produce a large entry
\bm{X}_{i}
, so the
algorithm should be able to reject such distributions. On the other hand, for intervals
I
that are
very small, the only way to ensure that the algorithm will likely reject is if
p[I]
is still large
regardless of how small
w[I^{*}]
is, which motivates the choice of
t
in the definition.

Remark 3.2
.

If
p
is not
C
-highly concentrated, then in particular
\|p\|_{\infty}<\frac{C\log n}{m}
,
as can be seen by taking intervals
I=\langle\!\langle i,1\rangle\!\rangle
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

We now show that the first step of the tester correctly accepts the uniform distribution and
rejects highly concentrated distributions with good probability. Therefore, we will be able
to assume that
p
is not highly concentrated when analyzing the second step of the tester.
We will need the following auxiliary result.

Proposition 3.3

(Buckets are almost always small)
.

Let
K\geq 2
be a constant, and
suppose
n\geq 3
. Then the buckets
\bm{\Gamma}=(\bm{\Gamma}_{i},\dotsc,\bm{\Gamma}_{\bm{b}})

induced by
\bm{H}
satisfy

\left\lvert\bm{\Gamma}_{i}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}\qquad\forall i\in[\bm{b}]

except with probability at most
1/n^{K}
.

Proof.

Let
d
be the smallest integer satisfying
d\geq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}
, and fix any interval

I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle
. The probability that all vertices in this interval are joined is

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(I)=1\right]=(1-\eta)^{d-1}\leq(1-\eta)^{\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}-1}\leq(1-\eta)^{\frac{(2K-1)\log n}{\eta}}\leq e^{-(2K-1)\log n}=1/n^{2K-1}\leq 1/n^{K+1}\,,

where we used the facts that
n\geq 3\implies\frac{\log n}{\eta}\geq 1
and that

K\geq 2\implies 2K-1\geq K+1
. Now, if any bucket has size at least
d
, then some interval

I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle
satisfies
J(I)=1
. Since there are at most

n
such intervals, the probability of this event is at most
1/n^{K}
by the union bound.
∎

We will need the following tail bounds for the Poisson distribution, as stated in
[
Can17
]
.

Fact 3.4
.

Let
\bm{X}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(\lambda)
for some
\lambda>0
. Then for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}\leq\lambda-t\right],\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}\geq\lambda+t\right]\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(\lambda+t)}}\,.

The result below makes the assumption that
m\leq n\eta
, which simplifies the analysis
and intuitively corresponds to the sublinear sample complexity regime in the standard uniformity
testing model. This assumption turns out to hold for the range of parameters we consider,
but not necessarily in more extreme regimes (see
Remark

3.24
).

Lemma 3.5
.

For sufficiently large constant
\alpha>0
and all sufficiently large
n
, the following holds.
Suppose
m\leq n\eta
. Then for any distribution
p
over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, we have:

1.

If
p
is uniform, the first step of the tester only rejects with probability
at most
1/100
; and

2.

If
p
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated, the first step of the tester rejects
with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

Completeness.
Suppose
p
is the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
From
Proposition

3.3
, we obtain that every bucket has size at most

\frac{4\log n}{\eta}
except with probability
o(1)
. Assume that this is the case, and fix some
particular bucket
\Gamma_{i}
. The number of elements sampled from this bucket is distributed as

\bm{X}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Poi}\left(mp\left[\Gamma_{i}\right]\right)=\mathsf{Poi}\left(m|\Gamma_{i}|/n\right)
.
Then, using
3.4
and for
\alpha>16
, the probability that

\bm{X}_{i}
is so large that the tester rejects is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}_{i}\geq\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}\left(m\cdot\frac{4\log n}{\eta}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\right)\geq\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(4\log n)\geq\alpha\log n\right]\qquad

(Since
m\leq n\eta
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(4\log n)-4\log n\geq(\alpha-4)\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{(\alpha-4)^{2}\log^{2}n}{2(4\log n+(\alpha-4)\log n)}}\leq e^{-\frac{(\alpha/2)^{2}\log n}{2\alpha}}\leq e^{-2\log n}=1/n^{2}\,.

Hence, the probability that this happens for any
\bm{X}_{i}
is at most
1/n=o(1)
.

Soundness.
Suppose
p
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated.
Using
Lemma

2.14
and the definition of high concentration,
we may choose some interval
I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle\in\mathcal{I}
satisfying

1.

(d-1)\eta=w[\langle\!\langle i,d-1\rangle\!\rangle]\leq\frac{1}{\log n}
,
and thus
|I|=d\leq 1+\frac{1}{\eta\log n}
; and

2.

p[I]\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\log n}\cdot\frac{4\alpha\log^{2}n}{m}=\frac{2\alpha\log n}{m}
.

We first claim that all the elements in
I
will be joined with high probability, i. e. every
edge in
I^{*}
will be sampled into
\bm{H}
. Indeed, by the union bound, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[J(I)=0\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\exists e\in I^{*}:e\not\in\bm{H}\right]\leq(|I|-1)\cdot\eta\leq\frac{1}{\eta\log n}\cdot\eta=\frac{1}{\log n}=o(1)\,.

Now, suppose every element in
I
belongs to the same bucket, say
\Gamma_{i}
. Recall that the
random variable
\bm{X}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Poi}\left(mp\left[\Gamma_{i}\right]\right)
represents the number
of elements drawn from this bucket, and by our assumption on
I
, we have

mp\left[\Gamma_{i}\right]\geq m\cdot\frac{2\alpha\log n}{m}=2\alpha\log n\,.

We now claim that, with high probability,
\bm{X}_{i}>\alpha\log n
and hence the tester will
reject. Indeed, using
3.4
, the probability that this does not
occur is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}_{i}\leq\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}\left(2\alpha\log n\right)\leq\alpha\log n\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}\left(2\alpha\log n\right)\leq 2\alpha\log n-\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}\log^{2}n}{2(2\alpha\log n+\alpha\log n)}}=e^{-\frac{\alpha\log n}{6}}=o(1)\,.\qed

We now proceed to the second step of the tester, and analyze the test statistic
\bm{Y}
.

3.2
Expected Value of the Test Statistic

Notation.

Let
\mu
denote the uniform distribution
over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. We will write
\bm{\Phi}
for the random join matrix and
\phi
for its expectation when
statements hold for both the path and the cycle. Otherwise, we will specify
\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{path}}
or

\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{cycle}}
.

Start by recalling that, as shown in
Proposition

2.6
, we may write the
expected value of
\bm{Y}
as

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=mp^{\top}\phi p\,.

(3)

When
G
is the path, the expected join matrix
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{path}}\right]
has a simple
formulation in terms of
\eta
.
It will be useful to name the quantity
1-\eta
, i. e. the probability of including each edge
in
\bm{H}
:

\nu\vcentcolon=1-\eta\,.

Proposition 3.6
.

The matrix
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}
is given by

\phi^{\mathsf{path}}_{i,j}=\nu^{\lvert i-j\rvert}

for each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

Proof.

Here, the relevant intervals are
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{path}}
. Hence,
for any
i<j
, we have that
i
and
j
are in the same bucket if and only if every edge
between them is in
\bm{H}
:

\phi^{\mathsf{path}}_{i,j}=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{path}}_{i,j}=1\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\forall e\in\langle\!\langle i,j-i+1\rangle\!\rangle^{*}:e\in\bm{H}\right]=\nu^{j-i}\,.\qed

When
G
is the cycle, so that the expected join matrix is
\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{cycle}}\right]
,
we need to account for the small and large intervals (in the notation of

Section

2.5.1
) connecting
i
and
j
, as follows.

Proposition 3.7
.

The matrix
\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}
is given by

\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}_{i,j}=\nu^{\lvert i-j\rvert}+\nu^{n-\lvert i-j\rvert}-\nu^{n}\,.

Proof.

The sets
\smallinterval(i,j)^{*}
and
\largeinterval(i,j)^{*}
have sizes

\min\{\lvert i-j\rvert,n-\lvert i-j\rvert\}
and
\max\{\lvert i-j\rvert,n-\lvert i-j\rvert\}
, respectively
(recall they partition the edges of the cycle). By the principle of inclusion-exclusion,

\displaystyle\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{\Phi}^{\mathsf{cycle}}_{i,j}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(\smallinterval(i,j))=1\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(\largeinterval(i,j))=1\right]-\mathbb{P}\left[\forall e\in E:e\in\bm{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\nu^{\lvert i-j\rvert}+\nu^{n-\lvert i-j\rvert}-\nu^{n}\,.\qed

We would like to show that
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]
is large when
p
is far from uniform.
Write
p=\mu+z
where
z\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
. As shown in
Proposition

2.7
,
we may decompose the expectation as

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=m\mu^{\top}\phi\mu+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z+mz^{\top}\phi z\,.

(4)

Letting
\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}
denote the test statistic when
p
is the uniform
distribution, we notice that the first term above is precisely the baseline against which

Algorithm

1
thresholds the test statistic:

Proposition 3.8

(Expectation of
\bm{Y}
in the uniform case)
.

When
p=\mu
,
\bm{Y}=\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]=m\mu^{\top}\phi\mu=\frac{m}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}\,.

Proof.

The claim follows from (
3
) and the
assumption that
p=\mu=\vec{1}/n
.
∎

Therefore, our strategy will be to show that 1) the minimum eigenvalue of
\phi
is large, and hence
so is
z^{\top}\phi z
when
\|z\|_{2}^{2}
is large; and 2) the term
\mu^{\top}\phi z
is small in
absolute value (in fact zero when
G
is the cycle),
so it does not affect the sum too much. We start with the first goal. Both
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}

and
\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}
enjoy nice properties (they are a Toeplitz and a circulant matrix, respectively),
and we bound the minimum eigenvalue of each in turn. Let

{\lambda_{\min}}(\cdot),{\lambda_{\max}}(\cdot)

denote the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of a (real symmetric) matrix, respectively.

Lemma 3.9

(Minimum eigenvalue of
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}
)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1]
. Then
\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{path}})>\eta/2
.

Proof.

The matrix
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}
is a symmetric Toeplitz matrix,
and its inverse may be found as in
[
Sra
]
.
Recall that
\nu=1-\eta
, so that
0\leq\nu<1
and
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}_{i,j}=\nu^{\lvert i-j\rvert}
by
Proposition

3.6
.
Then the inverse of
\phi^{\mathsf{path}}
(written
\phi^{-1}
for short)
is the following tridiagonal matrix:

\phi^{-1}=\frac{1}{1-\nu^{2}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1&-\nu&0&0&\dotsm&0\\
-\nu&1+\nu^{2}&-\nu&0&\dotsm&0\\
0&-\nu&1+\nu^{2}&-\nu&\dotsm&0\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
0&\dotsc&\dotsc&-\nu&1+\nu^{2}&-\nu\\
0&\dotsc&\dotsc&0&-\nu&1\\
\end{bmatrix}\,.

Now, we may upper bound the maximum eigenvalue of
\phi^{-1}

using the Gershgorin circle theorem:

\lambda_{\max}\left(\phi^{-1}\right)\leq\max_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\left\{\phi^{-1}_{i,i}+\sum_{j\neq i}\left\lvert\phi^{-1}_{i,j}\right\rvert\right\}\leq\left(\frac{1}{1-\nu^{2}}\right)\cdot\left(1+\nu^{2}+2\nu\right)=\frac{1+\nu}{1-\nu}\,.

Hence we obtain

\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{path}})=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\max}\left(\phi^{-1}\right)}\geq\frac{1-\nu}{1+\nu}>\frac{1-\nu}{2}=\eta/2\,.\qed

When
G
is the cycle, it is convenient to work with a simplified close approximation for

\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}
. Essentially, we wish to ignore the large intervals and instead work with the
matrix
\phi^{\smallinterval}
given by

\phi^{\smallinterval}_{i,j}\vcentcolon=\nu^{\left\lvert\smallinterval(i,j)\right\rvert-1}\,.

We will need the observation that
\zeta(\mathcal{I})
is negligibly small in our range of parameter
\eta
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Suppose
\eta\geq\Omega(n^{-1/5})
, and let
K>0
be any constant.
Then for all sufficiently large
n
,

\zeta(\mathcal{I})\leq\nu^{n/2}=o(n^{-K})\,.

Proof.

The first inequality is
Proposition

2.5
. The second one is easy to check:

\nu^{n/2}=(1-\eta)^{n/2}\leq e^{-\eta\cdot n/2}\leq e^{-\Omega(n^{-1/5}\cdot n)}=e^{-\Omega(n^{4/5})}=o(n^{-K})\,.\qed

We are now ready to lower bound the eigenvalues of
\phi^{\smallinterval}
and

\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}
. We first lower bound
\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\smallinterval})
, and then show that the
approximation error is negligible.

Fact 3.11

(Eigenvalues of circulant matrices; see
[
Gra06
]
)
.

Let
c_{0},c_{1},\dotsc,c_{n-1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the matrix

M=\begin{bmatrix}c_{0}&c_{1}&c_{2}&\cdots&c_{n-1}\\
c_{n-1}&c_{0}&c_{1}&\cdots&c_{n-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
c_{2}&c_{3}&c_{4}&\cdots&c_{1}\\
c_{1}&c_{2}&c_{3}&\cdots&c_{0}\end{bmatrix}

given by
M_{j,k}=c_{(k-j)\mod n}
has eigenvalues

\lambda_{\ell}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}c_{k}\omega^{\ell k}\qquad\ell=0,1,\dotsc,n-1\,,

where
\omega=e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{n}}
is a primitive
n
-th root of unity.

Lemma 3.12

(Minimum eigenvalue of
\phi^{\smallinterval}
)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1]
satisfy
\eta\geq\Omega(n^{-1/5})
. Then for all sufficiently large
n
,

\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\smallinterval})>\eta/3
.

Proof.

First assume
n
is odd. For each
k=0,1,\dotsc,n-1
, let

c_{k}\vcentcolon=\phi^{\smallinterval}_{0,k}
, so that
\phi^{\smallinterval}
is a symmetric
circulant matrix of the form stated in
3.11
. In particular,
letting
h\vcentcolon=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
for convenience, we have

c_{k}=\begin{cases}\nu^{k}&\text{if }k\leq h\\
\nu^{n-k}&\text{if }k>h\,.\end{cases}

Therefore for each
\ell=0,1,\dotsc,n-1
, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\ell}
is

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}c_{k}\omega^{\ell k}=c_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{h}\nu^{k}\omega^{\ell k}+\sum_{k=h+1}^{n-1}\nu^{n-k}\omega^{\ell k}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{h}\nu^{k}\omega^{\ell k}+\sum_{k=1}^{h}\nu^{n-(n-k)}\omega^{\ell(n-k)}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=1}^{h}\nu^{k}\omega^{\ell k}+\sum_{k=1}^{h}\nu^{k}\omega^{-\ell k}=1+\frac{\nu\omega^{\ell}-\nu^{h+1}\omega^{\ell(h+1)}}{1-\nu\omega^{\ell}}+\frac{\nu\omega^{-\ell}-\nu^{h+1}\omega^{-\ell(h+1)}}{1-\nu\omega^{-\ell}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\nu(\omega^{\ell}+\omega^{-\ell})+\nu^{2}+\nu\omega^{\ell}-\nu^{2}-\nu^{h+1}\omega^{\ell(h+1)}+\nu^{h+2}\omega^{\ell h}+\nu\omega^{-\ell}-\nu^{2}-\nu^{h+1}\omega^{-\ell(h+1)}+\nu^{h+2}\omega^{-\ell h}}{1-\nu(\omega^{\ell}+\omega^{-\ell})+\nu^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\nu^{2}-\nu^{h+1}(\omega^{\ell(h+1)}+\omega^{-\ell(h+1)})+\nu^{h+2}(\omega^{\ell h}+\omega^{-\ell h})}{1-\nu(\omega^{\ell}+\omega^{-\ell})+\nu^{2}}=\frac{(1-\nu)(1+\nu)\pm O(\nu^{n/2})}{1-2\nu\cos(2\pi\ell/n)+\nu^{2}}\,,

where we used the identity
e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta}=2\cos(\theta)
in the last step.
Thus, recalling that
\nu=1-\eta\in[0,1)
, we conclude that
\lambda_{\ell}
is lower bounded
by

\frac{(1-\nu)(1+\nu)\pm O(\nu^{n/2})}{1-2\nu\cos(2\pi\ell/n)+\nu^{2}}\geq\frac{(1-\nu)(1+\nu)}{(1+\nu)^{2}}-\frac{O(\nu^{n/2})}{(1-\nu)^{2}}\geq\frac{\eta}{2}-\frac{O(\nu^{n/2})}{(1-\nu)^{2}}\,.

Then, using
Proposition

3.10
,

\frac{O(\nu^{n/2})}{(1-\nu)^{2}}\leq\frac{o(n^{-3/5})}{\eta^{2}}\leq o(n^{-3/5}\cdot n^{2/5})=o(n^{-1/5})=o(\eta)\,,

and thus
\lambda_{\ell}>\eta/3
. When
n
is even, the same argument applies with an extra term
of order
O(\nu^{n/2})
, which leaves the asymptotic analysis unaffected.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1]
satisfy
\eta\geq\Omega(n^{-1/5})
. Then for all sufficiently large
n
,

\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}})>\eta/4
.

Proof.

Let
\phi^{\mathsf{err}}\vcentcolon=\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}-\phi^{\smallinterval}
. It is standard to check that

\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}})\geq\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\smallinterval})+\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{err}})
.
Since
\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\smallinterval})>\eta/3
by

Lemma

3.12
, it suffices to show that

\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{err}})>-o(\eta)
.
Since
\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}_{i,j}=\nu^{\lvert\smallinterval(i,j)\rvert-1}+\nu^{\lvert\largeinterval(i,j)\rvert-1}-\nu^{n}
by

Proposition

3.7
, we obtain

\phi^{\mathsf{err}}_{i,j}=\nu^{\lvert\largeinterval(i,j)\rvert-1}-\nu^{n}

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. By definition of
\zeta(\mathcal{I})
and recalling

Proposition

2.5
, we conclude that

\|\phi^{\mathsf{err}}\|_{\infty}\leq\zeta(\mathcal{I})\leq\nu^{n/2}\,.

By the Gershgorin circle theorem and
Proposition

3.10
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\phi^{\mathsf{err}})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\Big\{\phi^{\mathsf{err}}_{i,i}-\sum_{j\neq i}\left\lvert\phi^{\mathsf{err}}_{i,j}\right\rvert\Big\}>-n\cdot\nu^{n/2}\geq-n\cdot o(n^{-6/5})=-o(n^{-1/5})\geq-o(\eta)\,.\qed

We use the minimum eigenvalue of
\phi
to show that, if
\|p-\mu\|_{2}^{2}
is large, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]
is large. The following intermediate formulation of the expected value will
be useful.

Proposition 3.14
.

For all sufficiently large
n
, the following holds.
Let
p
be a distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
such that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(p,\mu)>\epsilon
, and write

p=\mu+z
. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]>\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+m\frac{\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{m}{2n}\epsilon^{2}\eta+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z\,.

Proof.

First, combine (
4
) and

Proposition

3.8
, along with the fact that

x^{\top}Mx\geq\lambda_{\min}(M)\|x\|_{2}^{2}
for any symmetric matrix
M
and vector
x

and the eigenvalue bounds
Lemmas

3.9
and
3.13

to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=m\mu^{\top}\phi\mu+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z+mz^{\top}\phi z

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+m\lambda_{\min}(\phi)\|z\|_{2}^{2}+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+m\frac{\eta}{4}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+m\frac{\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+m\frac{\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z\,.

Then, since
\|z\|_{1}=2\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(p,\mu)>2\epsilon
, we have

\|z\|_{2}^{2}>\left(\frac{2\epsilon}{n}\right)^{2}\cdot n=4\epsilon^{2}/n
, concluding the
proof.
∎

Now we show that the cross term
\mu^{\top}\phi z
is small. When
G
is the cycle, this term will
in fact be zero; when
G
is the path, the cross term is relevant due to the asymmetry between the
vertices closer to the endpoints or to the middle. However, this will not be a problem as long as

\|z\|_{\infty}
is not too large, which indeed holds when
p
is not highly concentrated.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1]
and let
\phi=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{\Phi}\right]
be the corresponding expected join matrix.
Let
\delta>0
be any positive real number. Then for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying

1.

\sum_{i}z_{i}=0
; and

2.

\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\delta
,

it is the case that

\left\lvert\mu^{\top}\phi z\right\rvert\leq\frac{2\delta}{n\eta^{2}}\,.

Proof.

When
\phi=\phi^{\mathsf{cycle}}
, we have that
\mu^{\top}\phi
is a constant vector (this is true for any
circulant matrix), and hence
\mu^{\top}\phi z=0
(since
\sum_{i}z_{i}=0
). Therefore we may
now assume that
\phi=\phi^{\mathsf{path}}
.

Note that, by symmetry between
z
and
-z
in the LHS, it suffices upper bound
\mu^{\top}\phi z
.
We expand this expression as follows:

\mu^{\top}\phi z=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\mu_{i}z_{j}\phi_{i,j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi_{i,j}\right)\,.

Hence our goal is to show

\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}S_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{?}}}}{{\leq}}\frac{2\delta}{\eta^{2}}\,,

(5)

where
S_{j}\vcentcolon=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi_{i,j}\right)
is the sum of the entries in the
j
-th
column of
\phi
. Note that
(S_{j})_{j=0,\dotsc,n-1}
is a symmetric unimodal sequence (first
increasing, then decreasing) with strict inequalities everywhere except for indices

\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor
and
\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil
when
n
is even.
We will use a “rearrangement and saturation” argument
to construct a vector
z^{*}
that upper bounds the LHS of (
5
) (hereafter called
the
objective value
).

Let
z^{\prime}
be a vector satisfying the conditions from the statement (hereafter called a

feasible solution
) that maximizes the objective value. Let
\sigma
be a permutation of

\{0,\dotsc,n-1\}
that puts the sequence of column sums in non-decreasing order:

S_{\sigma(0)}\leq\dotsm\leq S_{\sigma(n-1)}
. Then we can also assume that
z^{\prime}
respects this
order:
z^{\prime}_{\sigma(0)}\leq\dotsm\leq z^{\prime}_{\sigma(n-1)}
, since otherwise rearranging the entries
of
z^{\prime}
would yield another feasible solution with equal or larger objective value.

We now argue that we may assume that, among all nonzero entries of
z^{\prime}
, all have absolute
value equal to
\delta
(which we call
saturated entries
) except for at most one positive
entry and one negative entry. Indeed, if two consecutive (under
\sigma)
nonzero entries with
the same sign are not saturated, i. e. they satisfy

\left\lvert z^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}\right\rvert,\left\lvert z^{\prime}_{\sigma(i+1)}\right\rvert<\delta
, then we can obtain another feasible
solution with equal or larger objective value by “saturating” this pair of entries, i. e. making
z^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}
smaller and
z^{\prime}_{\sigma(i+1)}
larger until either of them reaches
a value in
\{-\delta,0,\delta\}
.

We claim that we may also assume that the multiset of values of the positive entries of
z^{\prime}
is
equal to the multiset of absolute values of the negative entries of
z^{\prime}
. Suppose
z^{\prime}
has
N^{+}

entries equal to
\delta
,
N^{-}
entries equal to
-\delta
,
M^{+}\in\{0,1\}
entries in the
interval
(0,\delta)
, and
M^{-}\in\{0,1\}
entries in the interval
(-\delta,0)
. If
N^{+}=N^{-}
, then since
\sum_{j}z^{\prime}_{j}=0
, we must also have
M^{+}=M^{-}
and, if this value is
1
, then
the corresponding entries of
z^{\prime}
must have the same absolute value so that they add to zero. On the
other hand, if
N^{+}\neq N^{-}
, say
N^{+}>N^{-}
without loss of generality, then
\sum_{i}z^{\prime}_{i}>0

since the sum of the saturated values of
z^{\prime}
is at least
\delta
while the sum of the
non-saturated values must be in
(-\delta,\delta)
. This contradicts the fact that
z^{\prime}
is a
feasible solution.

Now we construct
z^{*}
by saturating the remaining (zero or two) entries of
z^{\prime}
:

z^{*}_{i}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\delta,&\text{if $z^{\prime}_{i}>0$}\\
-\delta,&\text{if $z^{\prime}_{i}<0$}\\
0,&\text{if $z^{\prime}_{i}=0$.}\end{cases}

Then by the same arguments as above,
z^{*}
has equal or larger objective value as
z^{\prime}
.
We now upper bound this objective value by the RHS of (
5
), which will
conclude the argument.

Let
N
be the number of positive entries of
z^{*}
. By construction, we have

N=|\{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}:z^{*}_{i}=\delta\}|=|\{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}:z^{*}_{i}=-\delta\}|\,.

Then our objective value is

\sum_{j}z^{*}_{j}S_{j}=\delta\left[-\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}S_{\sigma(i)}+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}S_{\sigma(n-1-i)}\right]\,.

(6)

Let
h\vcentcolon=(n-1)/2
.
Since
(S_{j})_{j=0,\dotsc,n-1}
is a symmetric unimodal sequence attaining its maximum in the
middle, we may say without loss of generality that the indices
\sigma(i)
in the first
summation term in the RHS of (
6
) are

\{0,\dotsc,\lceil N/2\rceil-1\}\cup\{n-1,\dotsc,n-\lfloor N/2\rfloor\}
.
As for the indices
\sigma(n-1-i)
, an exact account depends on the parity of
n
,
but we can only make the objective value larger by simply using the indices

\{\lceil h\rceil,\dotsc,\lceil h\rceil+\lceil N/2\rceil-1\}\cup\{\lfloor h\rfloor,\dotsc,\lfloor h\rfloor-\lfloor N/2\rfloor+1\}
. Note that when
n
is odd, this choice
slightly overestimates the objective value by using the maximum value
S_{h}
twice,
but this looser bound suffices for our purposes.

Therefore, we may finally express and compute our upper bound on the objective value of
any feasible vector
z
. Recall that
\phi_{i,j}=\nu^{\lvert i-j\rvert}
.
In the edge case when
\eta=1
and thus
\nu=0
, we have that
\phi
is the identity matrix
and hence
S_{j}=1
for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}S_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}=0\,,

which satisfies (
5
) and we are done. Now, suppose
0<\nu<1
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}S_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left[-\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}S_{\sigma(i)}+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}S_{\sigma(n-1-i)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta\left[-\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil N/2\rceil-1}S_{i}-\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor-1}S_{n-1-i}+\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil N/2\rceil-1}S_{\lceil h\rceil+i}+\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor-1}S_{\lfloor h\rfloor-i}\right]\leq\frac{2\delta}{\eta^{2}}\,,

where we defer the tedious geometric sum calculations for the last inequality to

Proposition

B.1
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
C>0
, and let
p
be a distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
that is not
C
-highly concentrated.
Then as long as
\frac{8C\log n}{\eta^{3}\epsilon^{2}}\leq m\leq Cn\log n
,
the following holds:

\left\lvert 2m\mu^{\top}\phi z\right\rvert\leq\frac{m}{2n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\,.

Proof.

Write
p=\mu+z
. Since
\|p\|_{1}=\|\mu\|_{1}=1
, it follows that
\sum_{i}z_{i}=0
,
satisfying the first condition of
Proposition

3.15
. We will show that
z
also
satisfies the second condition with
\delta\vcentcolon=\frac{C\log n}{m}
.

Indeed, since
0\leq\frac{1}{n}+z_{i}\leq\frac{C\log n}{m}
(the second inequality
by
Remark

3.2
) we get, on the one hand,

z_{i}\geq-\frac{1}{n}\geq-\frac{C\log n}{m}\,,

where we used the assumption that
m\leq Cn\log n
, and on the other hand,

z_{i}\leq\frac{C\log n}{m}-\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{C\log n}{m}\,,

and thus
\|z\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{C\log n}{m}
as desired.
Proposition

3.15

implies that

\left\lvert\mu^{\top}\phi z\right\rvert\leq\frac{2C\log n}{mn\eta^{2}}\,.

Finally, it suffices to combine this inequality with our assumed lower bound
on
m
. We obtain

\left\lvert 2m\mu^{\top}\phi z\right\rvert\leq 2m\cdot\frac{2C\log n}{mn\eta^{2}}=\frac{8C\log n}{\eta^{3}\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}\eta}{2n}\leq\frac{m}{2n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\,.\qed

We combine the previous results to show the desired separation in the expected value of
\bm{Y}
:

Lemma 3.17

(Separation in the expected value of
\bm{Y}
)
.

Let
C,c>0
be constants, and let

m=c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n
.
Then for all sufficiently large
n
and all
\epsilon,\eta\in(0,1]
satisfying

\eta\geq(c/C)^{2/3}\frac{\log^{2/3}n}{n^{1/3}\epsilon^{4/3}}
, the following holds.
Suppose
p
is a distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
that is not
C
-highly concentrated such that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(p,\mu)>\epsilon
. Write
p=\mu+z
. Then the test statistic
\bm{Y}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]>\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.14
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]>\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{m}{2n}\epsilon^{2}\eta+2m\mu^{\top}\phi z\,.

Hence, we will be done if we can show that

2m\mu^{\top}\phi z\geq-\frac{m}{2n}\epsilon^{2}\eta
. This will follow immediately from

Lemma

3.16
as long as we can verify the preconditions on
m
. We first
check the lower bound:

m\geq\frac{8C\log n}{\eta^{3}\epsilon^{2}}\iff c\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\log^{2}n}{\eta^{3/2}}\geq\frac{8C\log n}{\eta^{3}\epsilon^{2}}\iff\eta\geq(8C/c)^{2/3}\frac{1}{n^{1/3}\log^{2/3}n}\,,

which holds for all sufficiently large
n
by our assumption on
\eta
. As for the upper bound,

m\leq Cn\log n\iff c\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\log^{2}n}{\eta^{3/2}}\leq Cn\log n\iff\eta\geq(c/C)^{2/3}\frac{\log^{2/3}n}{n^{1/3}\epsilon^{4/3}}\,,

which holds by assumption. Hence
Lemma

3.16
applies and we are done.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The condition
\eta=\Omega\left(\frac{\log^{2/3}n}{n^{1/3}\epsilon^{4/3}}\right)
in
the statement above will hold in the range of parameters considered by the present argument.
Concretely, when
\eta=\Omega\left(\frac{\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}\right)
and

\epsilon=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}\right)
, the condition holds because

\frac{\left(\frac{\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}\right)}{\left(\frac{\log^{2/3}n}{n^{1/3}\epsilon^{4/3}}\right)}=(\log n)^{2/15}n^{2/15}\epsilon^{8/15}\geq(\log n)^{2/15}n^{2/15}\Omega(n^{-2/15})=\omega(1)\,.

3.3
Concentration of the Test Statistic

We apply the general results presented in
Section

2.5.3
to upper bound the
variance of
\bm{Y}
.

Lemma 3.19

(First component of the variance)
.

Let
C>0
be a constant, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large and suppose
p
is a
probability distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
that is not
C
-highly concentrated.
Suppose that
m\leq\poly(n)
and
\eta\geq\Omega(n^{-1/5})
. Then

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O\left(\log^{4}n\right)\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.9
, for some constant
c>0
we have

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq 5m^{2}\zeta(\mathcal{I})\|p\|_{1}^{4}+cm^{2}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle\in\mathcal{I}\\
1\leq d\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}p[I]^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(I)\right]\,.

We start with the second component of the RHS.
Recall that for any
I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(I)\right]\leq\nu^{d-1}
(this value
may be zero if
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{path}}
and
I
crosses the edge between vertices
0
and
n-1
).
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle\in\mathcal{I}\\
1\leq d\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}p[I]^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(I)\right]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=1}^{n}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}p[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}\nu^{d-1}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=1}^{n}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}\nu^{d-1}\left[\frac{C\log^{2}n}{m}\cdot\max\left\{\eta(d-1),\frac{1}{\log n}\right\}\right]^{2}

(
p
is not
C
-highly-concentrated)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=1}^{n}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}\nu^{d-1}\left[\frac{C^{2}\log^{4}n}{m^{2}}\cdot\left(\eta^{2}d^{2}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{C^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\left(\eta^{2}\log^{2}(n)\sum_{d=1}^{n}d^{2}\nu^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}+\sum_{d=1}^{n}\nu^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C^{2}\|p\|_{2}^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\left(\eta^{2}\log^{2}(n)\sum_{d=1}^{n}d^{2}\nu^{d-1}+\sum_{d=1}^{n}\nu^{d-1}\right)

(by Cauchy-Schwarz)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C^{2}\|p\|_{2}^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\left(\eta^{2}\log^{2}(n)\frac{1+\nu}{\eta^{3}}+\frac{1}{\eta}\right)

(since
\eta=1-\nu
)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{3C^{2}\|p\|_{2}^{2}\log^{4}n}{m^{2}\eta}

(since
\nu<1
)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\cdot\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O\left(\log^{4}n\right)\,,

as desired. Then, it suffices to show that the term
5m^{2}\zeta(\mathcal{I})\|p\|_{1}^{4}
is
O(1/n)
,
since
\|p\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|\mu\|_{2}^{2}=1/n
. Let
K>0
be a constant such that

m\leq n^{K}
for sufficiently large
n
, as per the assumption that
m\leq\poly(n)
.
Then we have
\|p\|_{1}^{4}=1
and

m^{2}\zeta(\mathcal{I})=o(m^{2}/n^{2K+1})\leq o(1/n)

by
Proposition

3.10
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 3.20

(Second component of the variance)
.

Let
C>0
be a constant, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large and suppose
p
is a
probability distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
that is not
C
-highly concentrated.
Suppose
m\leq\poly(n)
. Then

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O(\log^{4}n)\,.

Proof.

Let
K\geq 2
be some constant such that
m\leq O(n^{K-1})
.
Recall that, from
Proposition

3.3
, the buckets

\bm{\Gamma}=(\bm{\Gamma}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{\Gamma}_{n})
induced by
\bm{H}
are such that

\left\lvert\bm{\Gamma}_{i}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}
for all
i
,
except with probability at most
1/n^{K}
. We show that the variance is small when this condition
holds, and that the low-probability case where the condition fails does not contribute too much
to the expectation.

Case 1.
Suppose
H
is such that its induced buckets

\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\dotsc,\Gamma_{b})
satisfy

\left\lvert\Gamma_{i}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}

for every
i\in[n]
. We wish to show that
\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]

satisfies the upper bound from the statement.
We start with the result from

Lemma

2.12
:

\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]=2\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+4m\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}\,.

We start by bounding the first term in the RHS. For each bucket
\Gamma_{i}
, we have

\left(p_{|\Gamma}\right)_{i}^{2}=\left(\sum_{j\in\Gamma_{i}}p_{j}\right)^{2}=\sum_{j,k\in\Gamma_{i}}p_{j}p_{k}\leq\sum_{j,k\in\Gamma_{i}}\frac{p_{j}^{2}+p_{k}^{2}}{2}=\left\lvert\Gamma_{i}\right\rvert\sum_{j\in\Gamma_{i}}p_{j}^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\log n}{\eta}\right)\sum_{j\in\Gamma_{i}}p_{j}^{2}\,.

Hence, we obtain

\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{b}\left(p_{|\Gamma}\right)_{i}^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\log n}{\eta}\right)\|p\|_{2}^{2}\,,

as desired. Moving on to the second term, first note that

\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}=\sum_{i}p[\Gamma_{i}]^{3}\leq\sum_{i}(\max_{j}p[\Gamma_{j}])p[\Gamma_{i}]^{2}=\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{\infty}\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}
. We claim that

\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{\infty}\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)
.
Fix any
i\in[b]
and consider entry

\left(p_{|\Gamma}\right)_{i}=p\left[\Gamma_{i}\right]
. The anticoncentration of
p
yields

p\left[\Gamma_{i}\right]\leq\frac{C\log^{2}n}{m}\cdot\max\left\{\eta\left(\left\lvert\Gamma_{i}\right\rvert-1\right),\frac{1}{\log n}\right\}\,.

Combining with the assumption that
\left\lvert\Gamma_{i}\right\rvert\leq O\left(\frac{\log n}{\eta}\right)
,
we get

p\left[\Gamma_{i}\right]\leq\frac{C\log^{2}n}{m}\cdot O(\log n)\,,

which establishes the claim. We have already shown that

\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O(\log n)
, and thus

m\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}\leq m\cdot O\left(\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)\cdot\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O(\log n)=\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O(\log^{4}n)\,,

which concludes Case 1.

Case 2.
In the rare event that
\bm{H}=H
is a subgraph that
fails the small-buckets condition of Case 1, we will fall back to a looser upper bound for
the conditional variance that holds for every
H
. We once again start with the result
from
Lemma

2.12
:

\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]=2\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+4m\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}\,.

Using
\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{1}=1
along with the monotonicity of
\ell^{p}
norms gives

\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]\leq 2\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{1}^{2}+4m\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{1}^{3}\leq 6m=O(n^{K-1})\,.

Concluding the argument.
We now combine both cases to upper bound the expected
variance. Using
Proposition

3.3
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{H}\text{ satisfies }\|\bm{\Gamma}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}\right]\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\text{ satisfies }\|\bm{\Gamma}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}\right.\right]\\
+\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{H}\text{ does not satisfy }\|\bm{\Gamma}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2K\log n}{\eta}\right]\max_{H}\left\{\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]\right\}\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta}\cdot O(\log^{4}n)+\frac{1}{n^{K}}\cdot O(n^{K-1})\,.

Since
\|p\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|\mu\|_{2}^{2}=1/n
, the first term dominates the second, concluding the
proof.
∎

We conclude that
\bm{Y}
satisfies the following concentration bound:

Lemma 3.21

(Concentration of the Test Statistic)
.

Let
C>0
be a constant, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large and suppose
p
is a
probability distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
that is not
C
-highly concentrated.
Suppose
\eta\geq\Omega(n^{-1/5})
and
m\leq\poly(n)
. Then for all
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]\right\rvert\geq t\right]\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta t^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{4}n)\,.

Proof.

Combine

Lemmas

3.19
and
3.20
,
via the law of total variance, and Chebyshev’s inequality.
∎

3.4
Correctness of the Tester

Combining our separation and concentration results above, we can show that
\bm{Y}
is concentrated
on the correct side of the tester’s threshold.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\alpha,L>0
be constants. Then there exist constants
\beta>0
, and

c=c_{\alpha,\beta}>0
such that
the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
.
Let
\epsilon,\eta\in(0,1]
satisfy
\eta\geq\frac{L\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}
.
Suppose
p
is a probability distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
that is not
4\alpha
-highly concentrated
with respect to
m=c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n
.
Let
T\vcentcolon=\frac{m}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}+\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta
be the
threshold used by the second step of
Algorithm

1
.
Then the test statistic
\bm{Y}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Completeness) If
p=\mu
, then
\bm{Y}<T
with probability at least
99/100
;

2.

(Soundness) If
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(p,\mu)>\epsilon
, then
\bm{Y}>T
with probability at
least
99/100
.

Proof.

Completeness.
Suppose
p=\mu
.
By
Proposition

3.8
,
\bm{Y}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=\frac{m}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}
.
Hence for any fixed
\beta
(to be chosen below),
it suffices to show that
\bm{Y}<\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]+\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta
with good
probability. By
Lemma

3.21
and using the fact that

\|\mu\|_{2}^{2}=1/n
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]+\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]\right\rvert\geq\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\right]\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta\left(\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\right)^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{4}n)=\frac{O(n\log^{4}n)}{\beta^{2}m^{2}\epsilon^{4}\eta^{3}}\,,

and we have

\frac{O(n\log^{4}n)}{\beta^{2}m^{2}\epsilon^{4}\eta^{3}}\leq 1/100\iff m\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\cdot O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n\right)\,,

(7)

as desired. Thus, there exists constant

c^{(1)}=c^{(1)}_{\alpha,\beta}
such that if

m\geq c^{(1)}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n

then, for all sufficiently large
n
,
\bm{Y}<T
with probability at least
99/100
.

Soundness.
We proceed similarly. By

Lemmas

3.17
and
3.8
,
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]>\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{m}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}+\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Therefore it suffices to show that, for appropriately chosen
\beta
, we have

\bm{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{?}}}}{{>}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\,.

Recall that, when
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(p,\mu)>\epsilon
, we have
\|z\|_{1}>2\epsilon
, which implies

\|z\|_{2}^{2}>4\epsilon^{2}/n
and hence

\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}>\frac{m}{2n}\epsilon^{2}\eta\,,

so that

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+\beta\frac{m}{n}\epsilon^{2}\eta<\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}+2\beta\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-(1-2\beta)\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Thus, for
\beta\leq 1/3
, we have
1-2\beta\geq\beta
and it suffices to show that the
following holds with probability at least
99/100
:

\bm{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{?}}}}{{>}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\beta\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\,.

We apply
Lemma

3.21
again, along with

\|p\|_{2}^{2}=\|\mu\|_{2}^{2}+\|z\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}+\|z\|_{2}^{2}
and

\|z\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{4\epsilon^{2}}{n}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\beta\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]\right\rvert\geq\beta\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{\|p\|_{2}^{2}}{\eta\left(\beta\frac{m\eta}{8}\|z\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{4}n)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\frac{1}{n}+\|z\|_{2}^{2})O(\log^{4}n)}{\beta^{2}m^{2}\eta^{3}\|z\|_{2}^{4}}\leq\frac{\frac{1}{n}O(\log^{4}n)}{\beta^{2}m^{2}\eta^{3}(\epsilon^{2}/n)^{2}}+\frac{O(\log^{4}n)}{\beta^{2}m^{2}\eta^{3}(\epsilon^{2}/n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{O(n\log^{4}n)}{\beta^{2}m^{2}\eta^{3}\epsilon^{4}}\,.

This failure probability is asymptotically the same as that obtained in the completeness case.
Thus there exists a constant
c^{(2)}=c^{(2)}_{\alpha,\beta}>0
such that, for

m\geq c^{(2)}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n

and all sufficiently large
n
,
\bm{Y}>T
with probability at least
99/100
.
Setting
c=\max\left\{c^{(1)}_{\alpha,\beta},c^{(2)}_{\alpha,\beta}\right\}

concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, we establish correctness by combining our results for the two steps of the tester:

Theorem 3.23

(Refinement of
Theorem

1.2
)
.

There exist constants
\alpha>0
,
\beta>0
,
c=c_{\alpha,\beta}>0
,
and
L=L_{c}>0
such that
the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
. Suppose
\epsilon,\eta\in(0,1]
satisfy

\eta\geq\frac{L\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}
. Let
G
be either the cycle or the
path on vertices
V=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and let
p
be a probability distribution
over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
Then
Algorithm

1
instantiated with constants

\alpha,\beta,L\text{ and }c
has sample complexity

\Theta\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n\right)

and its output on
p
satisfies

1.

(Completeness) If
p
is the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, then the algorithm
accepts with probability at least
9/10
;

2.

(Soundness) If
p
is
\epsilon
-far from the uniform distribution in TV distance,
then the algorithm rejects with probability at least
9/10
.

Proof.

We start by instantiating
\alpha>0
large enough as per

Lemma

3.5
. That lemma also requires that
m\leq n\eta
, which we
now verify. Fix any constant
c>0
and suppose

m=c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\eta^{3/2}}\log^{2}n
. Then

m\leq n\eta\iff c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\log^{2}n}{\eta^{3/2}}\leq n\eta\iff\eta^{5/2}\geq c\cdot\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/2}\epsilon^{2}}\iff\eta\geq\frac{c^{2/5}\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}\,.

Therefore, for any choice of
c
, setting
L=L_{c}\geq c^{2/5}
ensures that
m\leq n\eta
.

Thus, instantiate
\beta,c>0
as provided by

Lemma

3.22
, and the corresponding
L_{c}
as above.
Now, we can use
Lemmas

3.5
and
3.22

to establish overall correctness of the tester. (Note that the sample complexity claim follows
from the specification of the algorithm.)

Completeness.
By
Lemma

3.5
, the first step of the tester
rejects only with probability at most
1/100
. Likewise, by

Lemma

3.22
, the second step of the tester rejects
only with probability at most
1/100.
Hence the total rejection probability is at most

2/100<1/10
.

Soundness.
There are two cases depending on the concentration of
p
. First, suppose

p
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated. Then the first step of the tester rejects with
probability at least
99/100
by
Lemma

3.5
. On the other hand, if

p
is not
4\alpha
-highly concentrated, then the second step of the tester rejects with
probability at least
99/100
by
Lemma

3.22
. Either
way, the tester rejects with probability at least
99/100>9/10
.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

In the introduction (see
Theorem

1.2
), we stated that our sample
complexity upper bound would apply to the regime where

\epsilon\geq\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-1/4}\right)
and

\eta\geq\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-1/5}\epsilon^{-4/5}\right)
. Although the condition on

\epsilon
is not explicitly stated above, it is a consequence of the condition on
\eta

and the fact that
\eta\leq 1
in our definition of the problem:

\frac{L\log^{4/5}n}{n^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}\leq\eta\leq 1\implies\epsilon\geq\frac{L^{5/4}\log n}{n^{1/4}}\,.

An interesting question is whether it is possible to handle an ever wider range of parameters;
in particular, our analysis uses the inequality
m\leq n\eta
, but if we allow arbitrarily
small
\epsilon
, then necessarily
m\gg n
. Also note that it is not possible to
handle the
full
range of parameters: for sufficiently small
\epsilon
and (say)

\eta=1/10
, one may place the deviation from uniformity on two adjacent vertices,
and with probability at least
9/10
this deviation will be imperceptible to the tester.

4
Testing Uniformity in the Parity Trace Model

In this section we state the upper bound portion of our main
Theorem

1.1
,
stated formally here:

Theorem 4.1
.

Fix domain
[2n]
. Let
\Pi
contain only the uniform distribution. Then the sample complexity
of
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi))
-distribution testing under the parity trace is
\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
.

Following the setup from
Section

2
, we consider the task of testing
uniformity of an unknown distribution
\pi=\pi(p,q)
in the parity trace model. Recall that we
stitch the ends of the trace into a necklace and study the resulting circular trace.
Therefore our base graph
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{n},E)
is the cycle and we think of
p
as a partial distribution
over the vertices, whereas
q
will determine the weights of the edges:
if
e\in E
connects vertices
i
and
i+1
(mod
n
), then
w(e)=1-e^{-mq_{i}}
,
and this edge is sampled into
\bm{H}
with probability
1-w(e)=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(mq_{i})=0\right]
.

The testing algorithm has two cases: when
\epsilon
is very small, in which case we may reduce to
the standard uniformity testing algorithm (handled in
Section

4.1
); and when

\epsilon
is not too small, in which case our main analysis applies. In our main analysis, the
tester performs 3 steps:

1.

Bias test: check whether the counts of 1- and 0-valued symbols in the trace are too
unbalanced, in which case the distribution must be far from uniform;

2.

Concentration test: check whether any run-length (of either 1- or 0-valued symbols)
is too large. We will show that this case can also be safely rejected.

3.

Collision-based test: accept or reject depending on whether the test statistic
\bm{Y}
is
below a certain threshold. This step will require the most technical work.

Formally, the algorithm is
Algorithm

2
.
It is parameterized by absolute constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,K
, which will be
defined later, and requires that
\epsilon\geq\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
; note that this
condition, combined with the fact that the algorithm sets
m=O\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n\right)
, implies
m=O\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)=o(n)
. We will use this fact throughout the analysis.

Algorithm 2

Uniformity tester for the case when

\epsilon\geq\frac{K_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\log^{3}}{n^{1/4}}
.

Set
m\leftarrow\Theta_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n\right)
.

Constants:

\alpha,\beta,\gamma>0
and
K=K_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}>1
, to be defined later.

Input:
For
\pi=\pi(p,q)
on domain
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
,
receive
\mathsf{trace}(S)
for sample
S\leftarrow\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)

Requires:

\epsilon\geq\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
.

1:

procedure

UniformityTester-ParityTrace
(
\mathsf{trace}(S)
)

2:

Construct the circular trace from
\mathsf{trace}(S)
.

3:

for

b\in\{0,1\}

do

4:

Let
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}
be the “
b
” run-lengths defined in

Section

2.3
.

5:

N\leftarrow\sum_{i}X_{i}
.

6:

Y\leftarrow\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i}X_{i}(X_{i}-1)
.

7:

If

\frac{N}{m}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}

then
reject
.

8:

If

\max_{i}X_{i}\geq\alpha\log n
then
reject
.

9:

If

Y\geq\frac{m}{4n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi^{(\mu)}_{i,j}+\beta\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}
then
reject
.

10:

Accept
.

We will write
\mu
for the partial distribution such that
\pi(\mu,\mu)
is the uniform distribution,
i. e.
\mu_{i}=1/2n
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. As discussed in

Section

2.3
, we analyze only the statistics for the 1s in the trace (i. e. the case

b=1
in
Algorithm

2
), as the case for the 0s is symmetric.

Section

4.2
will show that the first two steps of

Algorithm

2
are correct.

Section

4.3
will show that
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]
is small when the input distribution
is uniform, and large when it is far from uniform.

Section

4.4
will give a bound on the variance of
\bm{Y}
.

Section

4.5
combines these results to prove correctness of

Algorithm

2
. Together with the algorithm for the small
\epsilon
case
in
Section

4.1
, this will prove the upper bound of
Theorem

4.1
.

4.1
The Small
\epsilon
Case

Fix any constant
K>0
. In the case
\epsilon<\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
, the tester will
simulate the standard uniformity tester (see e. g.
[
VV17a
,
DGPP19
]
). The tester under the parity
trace will use a sample of size
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}+n\log n\right)=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
. The simulation is possible because, using
O(n\log n)

samples, the tester either receives a sample from every domain element (therefore gaining the
ability to correctly distinguish all elements of the support), or it can safely reject. This proof
is not particularly insightful and we defer it to
Section

C.1
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
\epsilon<\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
. Then there is a distribution tester under the
parity trace, with sample complexity
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
,
such that on input distribution
\pi=\pi(p,q)
:

1.

If
p=q=\mu
, the algorithm will accept with probability at least
2/3
; and,

2.

If
\pi
is
\epsilon
-far from uniform, then the algorithm will reject with probability at
least
2/3
.

4.2
Easy Cases: Unbalanced and Highly-Concentrated Distributions

We now proceed to the main analysis, where
\epsilon\geq\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
, where
K
is
some constant. In this section we handle the two “easy” rejection cases of the tester, which
detect whether the total probability masses on the 1-valued elements and 0-valued elements are
significantly unbalanced, or whether one of the partial distributions
p
or
q
is highly
concentrated relative to the other.
First, we consider the case where the partial distributions are unbalanced:

Proposition 4.3
.

For sufficiently large absolute constant
\gamma>0
and sufficiently large
n
, the tester
satisfies the following:

•

When
\pi(p,q)
is uniform,
7
rejects with probability
at most
1/100
.

•

When
\|p\|_{1}\not\in\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}

(equivalently,
\|q\|_{1}\not\in\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
),

7
rejects with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

Fix the iteration of the tester with
b=1
, so that
\bm{N}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(m\|p\|_{1})
is the number
of “1” symbols observed in the trace. First suppose
\pi(p,q)
is uniform, so that in
particular
\|p\|_{1}=1/2
. Then by
3.4
, the probability that
the test in this iteration rejects is

\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\bm{N}}{m}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}\geq m/2+\gamma\sqrt{m}\right]\leq e^{-\frac{m\gamma^{2}}{2(m/2+\gamma\sqrt{m})}}\leq e^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\leq 1/200\,.

Now suppose
\|p\|_{1}>\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
. Then the probability that the
test in this iteration
fails
to reject is

\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\bm{N}}{m}<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}\leq m\|p\|_{1}-\gamma\sqrt{m}\right]\leq e^{-\frac{m\gamma^{2}}{2(m\|p\|_{1}+\gamma\sqrt{m})}}\leq e^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}\leq 1/200\,.

By symmetry, the same holds for the iteration
b=0
with respect to
\|q\|_{1}
. Hence this
step correctly accepts/rejects except with probability at most
1/100
.
∎

Next, we handle the case where
p
or
q
is highly concentrated relative to the other, which we
define as follows.

Definition 4.4

(Highly concentrated partial distributions)
.

Given a constant
C>0
, positive integer
m
, and partial distribution
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, we say
that
p
is
C
-highly concentrated
11
11

11

We would adjust this definition appropriately to
test the statistic for the 0s.

relative to
q
(with respect to
m
)
if

\rho_{t}(p\;\|\;q)\geq C\log^{2}n
, where
t=\frac{1}{m\log n}
.

Remark 4.5
.

If
p
is not
C
-highly concentrated relative to
q
, then in particular

\|p\|_{\infty}<\frac{C\log n}{m}
,
as can be seen by taking intervals
I=\langle\!\langle i,1\rangle\!\rangle
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

We can now combine this definition with
Lemma

2.14
to show
that the second step of the tester behaves as intended:

Proposition 4.6
.

For sufficiently large absolute constant
\alpha>0
and sufficiently large
n
, the tester
satisfies the following:

1.

When
\pi(p,q)
is uniform,
8
rejects with
probability at most
1/100
.

2.

When at least one of
p,q
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated relative to the other
with respect to
m
,
8
rejects with probability at
least
99/100
.

Proof.

Completeness.

Suppose
\pi(p,q)
is uniform and fix iteration
b=1
,
so that the tester is looking for long runs of “1” symbols. Therefore, we have
buckets
\bm{\Gamma}=(\bm{\Gamma}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{\Gamma}_{\bm{b}})

corresponding to the connected components of
\bm{H}
, and each bucket
\bm{\Gamma}_{i}

contributes a run length
\bm{X}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Poi}\left(mp\left[\bm{\Gamma}_{i}\right]\right)
.

First, we claim that
\max_{i}q\left[\bm{\Gamma}_{i}^{*}\right]\leq\frac{2\log n}{m}
with high
probability. For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, let
I_{i}
be the minimal circular interval

I_{i}=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle
satisfying
q[I^{*}_{i}]>\frac{2\log n}{m}
.
If
\max_{i}q\left[\bm{\Gamma}_{i}^{*}\right]>\frac{2\log n}{m}
, then at least one of these
intervals was joined, i. e.
\bm{J}(I_{i})=1
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Recall that each edge

e=(i,i+1)
appears in
\bm{H}
with probability

1-w(e)=e^{-mq_{i}}=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(mq_{i})=0\right]
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(I_{i})=1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\forall e\in I_{i}^{*}:e\in\bm{H}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(mq[I^{*}_{i}])=0\right]

\displaystyle<\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}\left(m\cdot\frac{2\log n}{m}\right)=0\right]=e^{-2\log n}=1/n^{2}\,.

By the union bound,

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{i}q\left[\bm{\Gamma}_{i}^{*}\right]>\frac{2\log n}{m}\right]<1/n=o(1)
.

Now, suppose
\max_{i}q\left[\bm{\Gamma}^{*}_{i}\right]\leq\frac{2\log n}{m}
. Since
\pi
is
uniform and therefore
p=q
, it follows that

\max_{i}p\left[\bm{\Gamma}_{i}\right]\leq\frac{4\log n}{m}
(the factor of
2
accounts for
the fact that
\Gamma_{i}
is in general one element larger than
\Gamma_{i}^{*}
, and if
the latter is empty and
\Gamma_{i}
has size 1, then the claim follows from the fact that

\pi
is the uniform distribution).
Thus, we use
3.4
to upper bound the probability that any fixed
bucket
\Gamma_{i}
produces a run length that
8
would
reject: for sufficiently large
\alpha
,

\displaystyle\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{X}_{i}\geq\alpha\log n\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{\Gamma}_{i}=\Gamma_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}\left(mp[\Gamma_{i}]\right)\geq\alpha\log n\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(4\log n)\geq\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(4\log n)-4\log n\geq(\alpha-4)\log n\right]\leq e^{-\frac{(\alpha-4)^{2}\log^{2}n}{2\left((\alpha-4)\log n+4\log n\right)}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{(\alpha/2)^{2}\log n}{2\alpha}}\leq e^{-2\log n}\leq 1/n^{2}\,.

Hence, the probability that this event occurs for any bucket is at most
1/n=o(1)
,
and by symmetry the same is true for the iteration of the tester with
b=0
.
Therefore, when
\pi(p,q)
is uniform
8
rejects only
with
o(1)
probability.

Soundness.

Suppose without loss of generality that
p
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated relative to
q
.
Using
Lemma

2.14
, let
I
be a circular
interval satisfying

1.

q[I^{*}]\leq\frac{1}{m\log n}
; and

2.

p[I]\geq 2\alpha\frac{\log n}{m}
.

First, we claim that with high probability
\bm{J}(I)=1
. Indeed we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(I)=0\right]

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left[\forall e\in I^{*}:e\in\bm{H}\right]=1-\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(mq[I^{*}])=0\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(mq[I^{*}])>0\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}\left(\frac{1}{\log n}\right)>0\right]=1-e^{-\frac{1}{\log n}}=o(1)\,.

Therefore any “1” symbols sampled from the vertices in
I
will belong to the
same run, and this run will contain at least
\mathsf{Poi}(mp[I])
symbols where

mp[I]\geq 2\alpha\log n
. Thus the probability that this run length fails to exceed the
rejection threshold is at most

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(mp[I])<\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(2\alpha\log n)\leq\alpha\log n\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}(2\alpha\log n)\leq 2\alpha\log n-\alpha\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}\log^{2}n}{2(\alpha\log n+2\alpha\log n)}}=e^{-\frac{\alpha\log n}{6}}=o(1)\,.

Hence
8
rejects except with
o(1)
probability, completing
the proof.
∎

These two steps will allow us to assume, when useful, that
1)
\|p\|_{1},\|q\|_{1}\in\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
; and 2) neither
p
nor
q
is

4\alpha
-highly concentrated relative to the other with respect to
m
.
We are now ready to analyze our main test statistic
\bm{Y}
.

4.3
Expected Value of the Test Statistic

Our first goal is to show that
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]
is well-separated between the case when
\pi(p,q)=\pi(\mu,\mu)
and when
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi(p,q),\pi(\mu,\mu))>\epsilon
. Note that the latter case
implies that
\|p-\mu\|_{1}>\epsilon
or
\|q-\mu\|_{1}>\epsilon
and that our tester is
symmetric with respect to the 0- and 1-valued symbols, so it is safe to assume without loss of
generality that
\|q-\mu\|_{1}>\epsilon
.

Recall our formulation of the expected value of
\bm{Y}
from
Proposition

2.6
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=mp^{\top}\phi p\,.

(8)

We first show that, when
\pi(p,q)=(\mu,\mu)
this value is precisely the baseline against which

Algorithm

2
thresholds the test statistic. Let

\phi^{(\mu)}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{\Phi}^{(\mu)}\right]
, where
\bm{\Phi}^{(\mu)}=\bm{\Phi}(\mu)
is the random
join matrix produced by the uniform partial distribution
q=\mu
.
Let random variable
\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}
denote the value of the test statistic when
\pi(p,q)

is the uniform distribution. Then we have:

Proposition 4.7

(Expectation of
\bm{Y}
in the uniform case)
.

When
\pi(p,q)
is the uniform distribution, the statistic
\bm{Y}=\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]=\frac{m}{4n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi^{(\mu)}_{i,j}\,.

Proof.

Since
p=q=\mu=\vec{1}/2n
, (
8
) yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu})\right]=m\mu^{\top}\phi^{(\mu)}\mu=\frac{m}{4n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi^{(\mu)}_{i,j}\,.\qed

We wish to show that when
q
is far from
\mu
, the quadratic form
p^{\top}\phi p
is large
regardless of the choice of
p
(assuming our conditions on the relative concentration and the
bias). Our strategy is to show that even a “worst-case” partial distribution
\overline{p}=\overline{p}(q)
, tailored to make
\bm{Y}
as small as possible, would still incur a large gap
compared to the uniform case; and then argue that if
p
deviates from
\overline{p}
, this can only
make the testing task easier.

We call this worst-case partial distribution the
uniform conjugate
of
q
, and denote it by

\overline{p}
(since it takes the role of
p
). Formally, we say a partial distribution
\overline{p}
is a
\tau
-uniform conjugate of
q
if

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\overline{p}_{i}\right)+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}q_{i}\right)=1\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\phi\overline{p}=\tau\cdot\vec{1}\,.

Note that the
i
-th entry of
\phi p
is

\left(\phi p\right)_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\phi_{i,j}p_{j}=\underset{\bm{\Phi}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\bm{\Phi}_{i,j}p_{j}\right]\,,

which is the expected sum
p[\bm{\Gamma}_{\bm{\gamma}(i)}]
of the bucket containing element
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Therefore, when
\overline{p}
is a
\tau
-uniform conjugate of
q
, this expected sum is the
same for every bucket. This is the “worst-case” because, in expectation, the distribution over the
odd elements sampled from
\overline{p}
will be uniform.

If we allow
\overline{p}
to have negative entries, one could show that every
q
has a
uniform conjugate for some value
\tau
, because
\phi
is positive semidefinite
12
12

12

If all
entries of
q
are non-zero, then as observed in
4.10

\phi
is positive
definite and therefore invertible, so
\overline{p}
exists for appropriate
\tau
. If
q
has some
zero entries, one can first reduce the domain by eliminating such entries, solve the inverse
problem, and then arbitrarily distribute the density in each range of
\overline{p}
separated by
zero
q
-density.
(
4.10
). But we require an explicit
\tau
(and
non-negative entries). We will give a closed-form solution for an
approximate
uniform
conjugate:

Definition 4.8

(Approximate uniform conjugate)
.

For a partial distribution
q
over
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
such that
\|q\|_{1}>0

and sample-size parameter
m
, with expected join matrix

\phi=\phi(q)
, let
\xi(m,q)\vcentcolon=\frac{e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}}{(1-e^{-m\|q\|_{1}})^{2}}
.
We say that
\widetilde{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}_{\geq 0}
is an
approximate uniform
conjugate
of
q
if
\|\widetilde{p}\|_{1}=1-\|q\|_{1}
, and, for
\tau=\tau(m,q)\vcentcolon=\frac{1-\|q\|_{1}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}
, it holds that

\max_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\left\lvert(\phi\widetilde{p})_{i}-\tau\right\rvert\leq 4n\cdot\xi(m,q)\,.

Going forward, write
\xi\vcentcolon=\xi(m,q)
for convenience.
We write the expected value of our test statistic in terms of an approximate uniform conjugate:

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose
\widetilde{p}
is an approximate uniform conjugate of
q
, and
write
p=\widetilde{p}+z
. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=m\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}+mz^{\top}\phi z\pm 8mn^{2}\xi

and

\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}=\|p\|_{1}(\tau\pm 4n\xi)\,.

Moreover, if
z=\vec{0}
, then we simply have

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=m\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}\,.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=mp^{\top}\phi p=m(\widetilde{p}+z)^{\top}\phi(\widetilde{p}+z)=m\left(\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}+2z^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}+z^{\top}\phi z\right)

\displaystyle=m\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}+mz^{\top}\phi z+2m\sum_{i}z_{i}(\phi\widetilde{p})_{i}=m\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}+mz^{\top}\phi z+2m\sum_{i}z_{i}(\tau\pm 4n\xi)\,.

Since
\sum_{j}z_{j}=0
(because
\|p\|_{1}=\|\widetilde{p}\|_{1}=1-\|q\|_{1}
)
and
|z_{i}|\leq 1
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, the term
2m\sum_{i}z_{i}(\tau\pm 4n\xi)

is bounded by
8mn^{2}\xi
in absolute value, which gives the first conclusion.
Inspecting the case when
z=\vec{0}
also gives the last conclusion.
For the second statement, observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}=\widetilde{p}^{\top}(\phi\widetilde{p})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{i}(\tau\pm 4n\xi)\,.

Since
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{i}=\|\widetilde{p}\|_{1}=\|p\|_{1}=1-\|q\|_{1}
, this value
is bounded from above by
(\tau+4n\xi)\|p\|_{1}
, and from below by
(\tau-4n\xi)\|p\|_{1}
.
∎

The positive semidefiniteness of
\phi
(shown below) already gives that
z^{\top}\phi z
is
non-negative, and we will also show that the approximation error term
8mn^{2}\xi
is negligible.
Therefore, our task is to show that
\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}=\|p\|_{1}(\tau\pm 4n\xi)

is large, i. e. to show that
\tau
is large when
q
is far from uniform.

Claim 4.10
.

For any partial distribution
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, the matrix
\phi=\phi(q)
is positive semidefinite.
If
q_{i}>0
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, then
\phi
is positive definite.

Proof.

For any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
, we have

u^{\top}\phi u=\mathbb{E}\left[u^{\top}\bm{\Phi}u\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\bm{\Phi}_{i,j}u_{i}u_{j}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in\bm{\Gamma}_{i}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j\in\bm{\Gamma}_{i}}u_{j}\right)^{2}\right]\,.

This is non-negative, so we conclude that
\phi
is positive semi-definite. If
q_{i}>0

for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, then
w(e)>0
for every edge
e
, so with positive probability

\kappa>0
the subgraph
\bm{H}
will be an independent set, in which case

\bm{\Phi}=\Phi
will have singleton buckets
\Gamma_{i}=(i,1)
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and

u^{\top}\phi u\geq\kappa\cdot u^{\top}\Phi u=\kappa\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{i}}u_{j}u_{j^{\prime}}\right)^{2}=\kappa\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}u_{i}^{2}>0\,,

whenever
u\neq\vec{0}
. So
\phi
is positive definite.
∎

Before continuing to make use of the nice properties of approximate uniform conjugates, we show
that such an object does exist:

Lemma 4.11
.

For any partial distribution
q
such that
\|q\|_{1}>0

and sample-size parameter
m
, there exists an approximate uniform
conjugate
\widetilde{p}
of
q
. If
q=\mu
, we may take
\widetilde{p}=\mu
.

Proof.

Recall that we identify the vertices of the cycle with the integers modulo
n
, i. e.
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

Let
E
be the event that
\bm{H}=G
, i. e. that every edge was sampled into
\bm{H}
.
Define
\varepsilon\vcentcolon=\mathbb{P}\left[E\right]
and note that
\varepsilon=e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}
, since each
edge
e=(i,i+1)
is sampled with probability
1-w(e)=e^{-mq_{i}}
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
, which we view as a candidate for
\widetilde{p}
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, define three random variables:

•

\bm{N^{R}}_{i}
is the number of vertices joined with
i
in
\bm{H}
in the clockwise
direction (including
i
itself). Formally,

\bm{N^{R}}\vcentcolon=\sum_{d=1}^{n}\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle)
.

Then define
\bm{R}_{i}\vcentcolon=u[\langle\!\langle i,\bm{N^{R}}_{i}\rangle\!\rangle]=\sum_{t=0}^{\bm{N^{R}}_{i}-1}u_{i+t}
.

•

\bm{N^{L}}_{i}
is the number of vertices joined with
i
in
\bm{H}
in the
counterclockwise direction (including
i
itself), unless all edges were sampled into

\bm{H}
(i. e. event
E
occurs), in which case we define
\bm{N^{L}}_{i}=1
.
Formally,
\bm{N^{L}}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathds{1}\left[E\right]+(1-\mathds{1}\left[E\right])\sum_{d=1}^{n}\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,-d\rangle\!\rangle)
.

Then define
\bm{L}_{i}\vcentcolon=u[\langle\!\langle i,-\bm{N^{L}}_{i}\rangle\!\rangle]=\sum_{t=0}^{\bm{N^{L}}_{i}-1}u_{i-t}
.

•

Define
\bm{D}_{i}\vcentcolon=u[\bm{\Gamma}_{\bm{\gamma}(i)}]=u[\langle\!\langle i-\bm{N^{L}}+1,\bm{N^{L}}+\bm{N^{R}}-1\rangle\!\rangle]
.

Note that
\bm{D}_{i}=\bm{L}_{i}+\bm{R}_{i}-u_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
; when
E
does not occur,
this is true because
\bm{N^{L}}_{i}
and
\bm{N^{R}}_{i}
count the number of joined elements to the left
and right of
i
and, since both encounter a non-joined element somewhere, only
i
itself is
counted twice. On the other hand, when
E
does occur, then this is true by construction, since
we get
\bm{D}_{i}=\bm{R}_{i}=\vec{1}^{\top}u
, whereas
\bm{L}_{i}=u_{i}
.

A perfect uniform conjugate
u
would make
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}_{i}\right]
equal everywhere. Observe that the random
variables
\bm{R}_{i}
and
\bm{L}_{i}
are not mutually independent, and
\bm{N^{R}}_{i},\bm{N^{L}}_{i}
are
bounded, which complicates the analysis. We relax the problem by defining random variables that are
independent and asking for an
approximate
uniform conjugate.

We introduce independent random variables
\bm{N^{R^{\prime}}}_{i}
and
\bm{N^{L^{\prime}}}_{i}
which are generated by
a Markov process, and define new variables
\bm{L}^{\prime}_{i}
,
\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}
, and
\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}
that depend on

\bm{N^{R^{\prime}}}_{i}
and
\bm{N^{L^{\prime}}}_{i}
in the same way as before:

•

\bm{N^{R^{\prime}}}_{i}
is generated as follows. Initialize
\bm{N^{R^{\prime}}}_{i}
to 1. For each
t\geq 0

in increasing order, sample
\bm{P}_{t}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(mq_{i+t})
. If
\bm{P}_{t}>0
, stop; otherwise
increment
\bm{N^{R^{\prime}}}_{i}
.

Define
\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}\vcentcolon=u[\langle\!\langle i,\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}\rangle\!\rangle]=\sum_{t=0}^{\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}-1}u_{i+t}
.

•

\bm{N^{L^{\prime}}}_{i}
is generated as follows. Initialize
\bm{N^{L^{\prime}}}_{i}
to 1. For each
t\geq 1

in increasing order, sample
\bm{P}_{t}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(mq_{i-t})
. If
\bm{P}_{t}>0
, stop; otherwise
increment
\bm{N^{L^{\prime}}}_{i}
.

Define
\bm{L}^{\prime}_{i}\vcentcolon=u[\langle\!\langle i,-\bm{N}^{\bm{L}^{\prime}}_{i}\rangle\!\rangle]=\sum_{t=0}^{\bm{N}^{\bm{L}^{\prime}}_{i}-1}u_{i-t}
.

•

Define
\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\vcentcolon=\bm{L}^{\prime}_{i}+\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}-u_{i}
, as in the original process above.

Recall that
\langle\!\langle i,k\rangle\!\rangle
is a multiset, so that
u[\langle\!\langle i,k\rangle\!\rangle]
can count
an element
u_{t}
more than once. Let
f\vcentcolon=1-\|q\|_{1}
.
Recalling
Definition

4.8
, we would like
u
to satisfy three
requirements: 1)
u_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
; 2)
\sum_{i}u_{i}=f
; and 3)
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\right]=\tau=\frac{1-\|q\|_{1}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. If we obtain such
u
and show that
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}_{i}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\right]\pm 4n\xi
, we will have found our approximate uniform conjugate
\widetilde{p}
.

We give an explicit solution and then verify it. Set

u_{i}\vcentcolon=\tau\left(\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}-1\right)

for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. It is clear that
u_{i}\geq 0
, satisfying the first requirement.
The second requirement is also satisfied:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\tau\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}-1\right]=\tau\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left[\frac{2}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}-1\right]=\tau\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left[\frac{1-e^{-mq_{i}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{f}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh(mq_{i}/2)}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh(mq_{i}/2)=f\,.

We now verify the third requirement. For convenience of notation, define

r^{\prime}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}\right]
,
l^{\prime}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{L}^{\prime}_{i}\right]
, and

d^{\prime}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\right]
. Let
\overline{R}
be the
n\times n
matrix given by

\overline{R}_{i,i+d}=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N^{R^{\prime}}}_{i}>d\right]=e^{-mq[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]}

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and
0\leq d\leq n-1
.
Then
R_{i,i+d}
is the probability that the Markov process
generating
\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}
counts
u_{i+d}
at least once.
Note that
\varepsilon=\mathbb{P}\left[E\right]=e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}
is the probability that the process
loops back to the same element
i
once.
Then using the Markov property, the expectation of
\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}
is

\displaystyle r^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\varepsilon^{t}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}=\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}+\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\varepsilon^{t}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}+\varepsilon\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\varepsilon^{t}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}=\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}+\varepsilon r^{\prime}_{i}\,,

and thus

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)r^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}u_{i+d}=\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\cdot\tau\left(\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d}}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d-1}}}-1\right)
