\displaystyle=\tau\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\frac{\overline{R}_{i,i+d}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d}}}-\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\right)+\tau\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\frac{\overline{R}_{i,i+d}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d-1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tau\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+d}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d}}}\right)+\tau\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d-1}}}\right)\,.

We now observe that, for
1\leq d\leq n-1
,

\overline{R}_{i,i+d}=e^{-mq_{i+d-1}}\overline{R}_{i,i+d-1}
. Also note that

\overline{R}_{i,i}=1
. Along with a change of variables in the second sum above, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\tau}r^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-2}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+d}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d}}}\right)-\overline{R}_{i,i+n-1}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\overline{R}_{i,i}\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}+\left(\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}e^{-mq_{i+d-1}}R_{i,i+d-1}\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d-1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-2}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+d}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d}}}\right)-\overline{R}_{i,i+n-1}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}+\left(\sum_{d=0}^{n-2}\overline{R}_{i,i+d}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+d}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+d}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\overline{R}_{i,i+n-1}\frac{e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}{1+e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}\,.

Also note that
\varepsilon=e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}\overline{R}_{i,i+n-1}
, and

q_{i-1}=q_{i+n-1}
, and therefore

\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\tau}r^{\prime}_{i}=-\frac{\varepsilon}{1+e^{-mq_{i+n-1}}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}=(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}\,.

Since
\varepsilon<1
(because
\|q\|>0
),
We conclude that

r^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{\tau}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}\,.

An identical analysis for the symmetrical process determining
\bm{L}^{\prime}_{i}
yields

l^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{\tau}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}\,.

We now verify the third requirement: for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\right]=d^{\prime}_{i}=l^{\prime}_{i}+r^{\prime}_{i}-u_{i}=\frac{\tau}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}+\frac{\tau}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}-\tau\left(\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i}}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{-mq_{i-1}}}-1\right)=\tau\,,

as needed.

It remains to show that
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}_{i}\right]
does not differ from
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\right]=\tau
by
more than
4n\xi
.
Write
l_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{L}_{i}\right]
and
r_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{R}_{i}\right]
. We will show that

\lvert r_{i}-r^{\prime}_{i}\rvert
and
\lvert l_{i}-l^{\prime}_{i}\rvert
are small.

Fix some
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Recall that
\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}
counts how many states the Markov process
for
\bm{R}^{\prime}_{i}
visited before stopping, meaning that the process sampled
\bm{P}_{t}=0
and
advanced to the next state (vertex) exactly
\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}-1
consecutive times before
stopping. Let
\bm{K}\vcentcolon=\lfloor\frac{\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}-1}{n}\rfloor
, so that

\bm{K}
is the number of times the process “looped back” and reached vertex
i
again.

Then, recalling the definition of event
E
, note that

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{K}\geq 1\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[E\right]=\varepsilon=e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}
.
More generally, we have
\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{K}\geq k\right]\leq e^{-km\|q\|_{1}}
for every non-negative
integer
k
by the Markov property.

Now, we may bound
\lvert r_{i}-r^{\prime}_{i}\rvert
as follows. First, note that
r_{i}\leq r^{\prime}_{i}
, since

u
is a non-negative vector and, although the join (or transition) probabilities are the same
for both processes, the Markov process may continue even after visiting
n
elements.
In fact, we have
\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N^{R}}_{i}=t\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}=t\right]
for every

1\leq t\leq n-1
, and thus
\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N^{R}}_{i}=n\right]\geq\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}=n\right]
.
Now, it suffices to upper bound
r^{\prime}_{i}-r_{i}
, which we do as follows:

\displaystyle r^{\prime}_{i}-r_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}=t\right]\sum_{d=0}^{t-1}u_{i+d}-\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N^{R}}_{i}=t\right]\sum_{d=0}^{t-1}u_{i+d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}=t\right]-\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N^{R}}_{i}=t\right]\right)\sum_{d=0}^{t-1}u_{i+d}+\sum_{t=n+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}=t\right]\sum_{d=0}^{t-1}u_{i+d}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}=kn+t\right]\sum_{d=0}^{kn+t-1}u_{i+d}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}n\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{N}^{\bm{R}^{\prime}}_{i}\geq kn+1\right](k+1)f=nf\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{K}\geq k\right](k+1)

\displaystyle\leq nf\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(k+1)e^{-km\|q\|_{1}}\leq nf\cdot 2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}ke^{-km\|q\|_{1}}\leq 2nf\cdot\frac{e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}}{(1-e^{-m\|q\|_{1}})^{2}}\,,

which is bounded by
2n\xi(m,q)
where
\xi
is defined as in

Definition

4.8
. A similar analysis shows that
\lvert l^{\prime}_{i}-l_{i}\rvert\leq 2n\xi(m,q)
. Therefore
\lvert d^{\prime}_{i}-d_{i}\rvert\leq 4n\xi(m,q)
, and hence
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}_{i}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{D}^{\prime}_{i}\right]\pm 4n\xi(m,q)=\tau\pm 4n\xi(m,q)
. Hence
\widetilde{p}=u
is an approximate uniform
conjugate with the desired parameters. Moreover, one can check that the solution
\widetilde{p}
we
obtained yields
\mu
when
q=\mu
.
∎

To analyze
\tau=\frac{1-\|q\|_{1}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}
,
we will require the following bounds on
\tanh(x)
.

Fact 4.12
.

For sufficiently small
x>0
,

\frac{x}{2}\leq\tanh(x)\leq 2x\,.

Proof.

This follows from the Taylor expansion
\tanh(x)=x-\frac{x^{3}}{3}+\frac{2x^{5}}{15}+O(x^{7})
.
∎

Notation

For vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
, we denote by
u^{+}
the vector given by

u^{+}_{i}=\max(0,u_{i})
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and by
u^{-}
the vector given by

u^{-}_{i}=-\min(0,u_{i})
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

Proposition 4.13

(Quadratic upper bound to
\tanh
from near zero to the right)
.

For all sufficiently small real numbers
r>0
and all
0\leq x\leq\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}
,
we have

\tanh(r+x)\leq\tanh(r)+(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x-\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x^{2}\,.

Proof.

Define functions
f,g:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
as the quantities on the two sides
of the desired inequality:

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\tanh(r+x)\,,

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\tanh(r)+(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x-\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x^{2}\,.

Thus we wish to show that, for sufficiently small
r
,
f(x)\leq g(x)
for all

0\leq x\leq\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}
.

Recall that
\tanh
is bounded between
0
and
1
in its non-negative domain, with

\tanh(r)=r\pm O(r^{3})
as
r\to 0
(this follows from its Taylor series) and

\tanh(y)\to 1
as
y\to\infty
.
Since
g
is a downward-facing parabola, we start by determining the point
x^{*}
at which

g
attains its maximum. We can determine this point by setting the derivative
g^{\prime}
to zero:

g^{\prime}(x^{*})=0\implies(1-\tanh^{2}(r))-2\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x^{*}=0\implies x^{*}=\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}\,.

Now, our strategy will be to define a “breakpoint”
c\log(1/r)
(for a sufficiently
small constant
c
to be specified) and show that
g(c\log(1/r))\to\infty
, thus dividing
the argument in two parts:
c\log(1/r)\leq x\leq x^{*}
, where
g
is increasing and hence the
result will follow immediately, and
0\leq x\leq c\log(1/r)
, which will require some more work.

We first show that for every
c>0
,
g(c\log(1/r))\to\infty
as
r\to 0
:

\displaystyle g(c\log(1/r))

\displaystyle\geq(1-\tanh^{2}(r))c\log(1/r)(1-\tanh(r)c\log(1/r))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}c\log(1/r)(1-2cr\log(1/r))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c\log(1/r)}{4}

\displaystyle=\omega(1)\,,

where we have used the fact that
r\log(1/r)\to 0
in the last inequality.

Note that
c\log\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\leq\frac{1}{4r}\leq\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}
for all
sufficiently small
r
. This means that
g(x)
is increasing on

\left[c\log(1/r),x^{*}\right]
and hence
g(x)>1
in this range.
Since
f(x)<1
for all
x
, we have shown that
f(x)\leq g(x)
when
c\log(1/r)\leq x\leq x^{*}
.

We now proceed to the range
0\leq x\leq c\log(1/r)
. By the mean-value form of Taylor’s
theorem, we have that

f(x)=f(0)+f^{\prime}(0)x+\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\eta)x^{2}

for some
0\leq\eta\leq x
. Substituting the definition of
f
, we obtain:

f(x)=\tanh(r)+(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x-\tanh(r+\eta)(1-\tanh^{2}(r+\eta))x^{2}\,.

Hence, to show that
f(x)\leq g(x)
, it suffices to show that

\tanh(y)(1-\tanh^{2}(y))\geq\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))

for all
r\leq y\leq r+x\leq r+c\log(1/r)
. We will show this for the larger interval

r\leq y\leq 2c\log(1/r)
.

Define
h:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
by
h(x)\vcentcolon=\tanh(x)(1-\tanh^{2}(x))
, so that we wish
to show
h(y)\geq h(r)
for
r\leq y\leq 2c\log(1/r)
. The derivative
h^{\prime}
satisfies the
following properties:

1.

h^{\prime}(x)=(1-3\tanh^{2}(x))(1-\tanh^{2}(x))
;

2.

h^{\prime}(0)=1
;

3.

h^{\prime}
has its only positive real root at
\nu\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}(2)=\frac{1}{2}\log(2+\sqrt{3})
; and

4.

h^{\prime}
is positive on
[0,\nu)
and negative on
(\nu,\infty)
.

It follows that
h
is increasing on
[r,\nu]
and decreasing on
[\nu,2c\log(1/r)]
.
Since
h(r)\geq h(r)
trivially, we obtain that
h(y)\geq h(r)
for
r\leq y\leq\nu
.
Therefore it suffices to show that
h(2c\log(1/r))\geq h(r)
as long as
r
is sufficiently
small. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle h(2c\log(1/r))

\displaystyle\geq h(r)

\displaystyle\iff\tanh(2c\log(1/r))(1-\tanh^{2}(2c\log(1/r)))

\displaystyle\geq\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\tanh(2c\log(1/r))}{\tanh(r)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1-\tanh^{2}(r)}{1-\tanh^{2}(2c\log(1/r))}

\displaystyle\impliedby\frac{1/2}{2r}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1-\left(\frac{1-e^{-4c\log(1/r)}}{1+e^{-4c\log(1/r)}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\impliedby\frac{1}{4r}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1-(1-e^{-4c\log(1/r)})}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{1}{4r}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{r^{4c}}

\displaystyle\iff r^{1-4c}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\,,

which holds for all sufficiently small
r
as long as
c<1/4
, since then

r^{1-4c}\to 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.14

(Quantitative Jensen’s inequality for
\tanh
near zero)
.

For all sufficiently small
r>0
, the following holds. Suppose
u\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
is a vector
satisfying
u_{i}\in\left[0,r+\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}\right]
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and whose
average is
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}u_{i}=r
. Then we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh(u_{i})\leq\tanh(r)\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\left(1-O(r^{2})\right)\|(u-r\cdot\vec{1})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

Proof.

Write
u_{i}=r+x_{i}
, so that
x_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}

and
\sum_{i}x_{i}=\sum_{i}(u_{i}-r)=0
.
Since
\tanh
is a concave function on its non-negative domain, the first-degree Taylor series
around
r
, namely
\tanh(r+y)\approx\tanh(r)+(1-\tanh^{2}(r))y
,
upper bounds
\tanh(u_{i})
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

Therefore, our strategy will be to upper bound the entries with
x_{i}<0
via the first-degree
series, and the entries with
x_{i}\geq 0
via
Proposition

4.13
. Then,
the first degree terms will cancel out and the second-degree terms will yield the desired bound.
Concretely, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh(u_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh(r+x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\Big[\tanh(r)+(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x_{i}\Big]-\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}:x_{i}\geq 0}\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))x_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=n\tanh(r)+(1-\tanh^{2}(r))\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\tanh(r)(1-\tanh^{2}(r))\sum_{i:x_{i}\geq 0}x_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\tanh(r)\Big(n-\left(1-O\left(r^{2}\right)\right)\|(u-r)^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)\,.\qed

We may now combine the results above to show a separation in
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]
as long as
q

is not highly concentrated relative to
p
:

Lemma 4.15

(Separation in the expected value of the test statistic)
.

Let
C,\gamma>0
be constants, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large,
let
\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}
, and let
m=m(n,\epsilon)
satisfy

\left(2^{8}\gamma^{2}+(2^{18}\gamma)^{2/5}\right)\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\leq m\leq\frac{4n}{3C\log n}\,.

Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, where
p,q
are partial distributions
satisfying
\|p\|_{1},\|q\|_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
,
\|q-\mu\|_{1}>\epsilon
, and such that
q
is not
C
-highly concentrated with respect to
p
.
Write
p=\widetilde{p}+z
where
\widetilde{p}
is an approximate uniform conjugate of
q
.
Then the expected value of the test statistic
\bm{Y}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)+mz^{\top}\phi z\,.

Proof.

From
Lemma

4.11
,
\mu
is its own approximate uniform conjugate.
Since we will reason about both
\mu
as its own approximate uniform conjugate and about

\widetilde{p}
as the approximate uniform conjugate of
q
, let

\xi\vcentcolon=\max(\xi(m,\mu),\xi(m,q))
. First, using
Proposition

4.9
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]=m\mu^{\top}\phi^{(\mu)}\mu=m\|\mu\|_{1}(\tau(m,\mu)\pm 4n\xi)=\frac{m\|\mu\|_{1}^{2}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)}\pm 4mn\|\mu\|_{1}\xi=\frac{m}{4n\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)}\pm 2mn\xi\,.

(9)

We now consider
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]
.
By the assumption that
\|p\|_{1}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
, we obtain

\|p\|_{1}^{2}\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\,.

Let
\tau\vcentcolon=\tau(m,q)
. Using
Proposition

4.9
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=m\|p\|_{1}(\tau\pm 4n\xi)+mz^{\top}\phi z\pm 8mn^{2}\xi

(10)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m\|p\|_{1}^{2}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}+mz^{\top}\phi z-4mn\xi\|p\|_{1}-8mn^{2}\xi

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m-8\gamma\sqrt{m}}{4\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}+mz^{\top}\phi z-12mn^{2}\xi\,.

We now write
q_{i}=\tfrac{1}{n}\|q\|_{1}+x_{i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, so

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}+\frac{mx_{i}}{2}\right)\,,

and
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}x_{i}=0
. Writing
r\vcentcolon=\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}
and

u_{i}\vcentcolon=\tfrac{mq_{i}}{2}
, we have
u=\frac{m}{2}q
and

0\leq u_{i}=r+\frac{mx_{i}}{2}\,.

Since
q
is not
C
-highly concentrated relative to
p
, then as observed in

Remark

4.5
it holds that

\|q\|_{\infty}<\frac{C\log n}{m}
, so we have

x_{i}<\frac{C\log n}{m}-\frac{1}{n}\|q\|_{1}<\frac{C\log n}{m}\,.

Moreover, since
\|q\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
, we have that
r
satisfies

r\leq\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}\leq\frac{3m}{8n}\implies\tanh(r)\leq\frac{3m}{4n}\,,

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
\sqrt{m}/n=o(m/n)
, and in the
last inequality we used
4.12
and the assumption that
m/n\leq\frac{4}{3C\log n}

and that
n
is sufficiently large. Then we obtain

x_{i}<\frac{C\log n}{m}=\frac{2}{m}\cdot\frac{C\log n}{2}\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot\frac{2n}{3m}=\frac{2}{m}\cdot\frac{1}{2\cdot 3m/(4n)}\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}\,,

where in the second inequality we used the assumption
m\leq\frac{4n}{3C\log n}
.
Thus
u,r
satisfy the conditions

0\leq u_{i}=r+\frac{mx_{i}}{2}\leq r+\frac{1}{2\tanh(r)}\,,\qquad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}u_{i}=\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}=r\,.

Let
q^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\tfrac{1}{n}\|q\|_{1}\cdot\vec{1}
, which is the partial distribution that is
uniform with total mass equal to the total mass of
q
. Then observing that

r\cdot\vec{1}=m\|q\|_{1}\cdot\mu=(m/2)\cdot q^{\prime}
, we apply
Lemma

4.14
, yielding

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh(u_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{n}(1-O(r^{2}))\|((m/2)q-(m/2)q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)\left(1-\frac{m^{2}}{8n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\,,

where the last inequality used the fact that
r^{2}=\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)^{2}=o(1)
.
We will also use the following upper bound on
\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}
:

\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|q\|_{2}^{2}<\left(\frac{C\log n}{m}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\left(\frac{C\log n}{m}\right)}=\frac{C\log n}{m}\,,

where we used the fact that
\|q\|_{\infty}<\frac{C\log n}{m}
by the
anticoncentration assumption, so that the maximum
\ell^{2}
-norm is achieved by concentrating
the partial distribution as much as possible given this constraint. We conclude that

\frac{m^{2}}{8n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}<\frac{Cm\log n}{8n}\leq\frac{1}{6}\,,

(11)

the latter since
m\leq\frac{4n}{3C\log n}
. Thus we use the inequality

\frac{1}{1-x}\geq 1+x
, valid for
x<1
, as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{m-8\gamma\sqrt{m}}{4\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m-8\gamma\sqrt{m}}{4n\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)\left(1-\frac{m^{2}}{8n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right)}

(12)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m-8\gamma\sqrt{m}}{4n\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)}\left(1+\frac{m^{2}}{8n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

From (
9
), (
10
), and (
12
), we now have

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]\geq\frac{m}{4n}\cdot F+G-H+mz^{\top}\phi z-14mn^{2}\xi\,,

(13)

where

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)}-\frac{1}{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)}\,,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{4n\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)}\cdot\frac{m^{2}}{8n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\frac{8\gamma\sqrt{m}}{4n\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)}\left(1+\frac{m^{2}}{8n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

We will show that
G
is large enough to give the desired separation

\Omega(\epsilon^{2}m^{2}/n^{2})
, while
F
and
H
are asymptotically small enough.
We first lower bound
F
. Using the fact that

\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\leq\frac{m}{2n}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right)=\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}
and
the upper bound
\tanh(r+x)\leq\tanh(r)+x(1-\tanh^{2}(r))
, which holds from the Taylor
expansion of
\tanh
when the arguments are all non-negative, we obtain

F\geq\frac{1}{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}\right)}-\frac{1}{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)}=\frac{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)-\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}\right)}{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}\right)}\geq-\frac{\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}\left(1-\tanh^{2}\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)\right)}{\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}}{n}\right)}\,.

For sufficiently large
n
and therefore sufficiently small
m/n
, we have

\tanh(m/4n+\gamma\sqrt{m}/n)>\tanh(m/4n)\geq m/8n
from
4.12
. We obtain

\frac{m}{4n}\cdot F\geq-\frac{m}{4n}\cdot\frac{\gamma\sqrt{m}/n}{(m/8n)^{2}}=-\frac{16\cdot\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\,.

We verify that this negative factor does not overwhelm the desired separation

\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}
as follows:

\frac{16\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2^{12}\cdot n^{2}}\iff m^{5/2}\geq\frac{2^{16}\cdot\gamma\cdot n^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\iff m\geq(2^{16}\cdot\gamma)^{2/5}(n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\,,

which holds by assumption.

As for
G
, we use the bound
\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)\leq\tanh(m/2n)\leq m/n

and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to obtain

G\geq\frac{m^{2}}{32n}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{m^{2}}{32n^{2}}\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{1}^{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{2^{7}\cdot n^{2}}\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}^{2}\,,

where the equality is because, since
\|q\|_{1}=\|q^{\prime}\|_{1}
, we have

\|(q-q^{\prime})^{+}\|_{1}=\|(q-q^{\prime})^{-}\|_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}
.

Therefore, our goal is to show that
\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}^{2}
is not much smaller than
\epsilon^{2}
.
Using the triangle inequality, we have

\epsilon<\|q-\mu\|_{1}\leq\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}+\|q^{\prime}-\mu\|_{1}=\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\lvert\frac{\|q\|_{1}}{n}-\frac{1}{2n}\right\rvert=\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}+\left\lvert\|q\|_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\leq\|q-q^{\prime}\|_{1}+\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\,,

so that, using
\epsilon\leq 2
which always holds,

G\geq\frac{m^{2}}{2^{7}\cdot n^{2}}\left(\epsilon-\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right)^{2}>\frac{m^{2}}{2^{7}\cdot n^{2}}\left(\epsilon^{2}-\frac{8\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right)\geq\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2^{8}\cdot n^{2}}\,,

where the last inequality is obtained as follows, using the assumption that

m\geq 2^{8}\cdot\gamma^{2}(n/\epsilon)^{4/5}
:

\displaystyle\frac{8\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\iff m\geq\frac{2^{8}\gamma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}}

\displaystyle\impliedby 2^{8}\gamma^{2}(n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\geq\frac{2^{8}\gamma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}}\iff\epsilon^{16/5}\geq\frac{1}{n^{4/5}}\iff\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}\,,

which is true by assumption.

We also show that
H
does not overwhelm this term. For sufficiently large
n
and therefore

m
, we have the inequality

\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\|q\|_{1}\right)\geq\tanh\left(\frac{m}{2n}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\right)\right)\geq\tanh\left(\frac{m}{8n}\right)\geq m/16n
.
Along with, (
11
), we conclude

H<\frac{8\gamma\sqrt{m}}{4n\cdot(m/16n)}\left(1+\frac{1}{6}\right)<\frac{2^{6}\cdot\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2^{12}\cdot n^{2}}\,,

where the last inequality holds since

\frac{2^{6}\cdot\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2^{12}\cdot n^{2}}\iff m^{5/2}\geq\frac{2^{18}\gamma n^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\iff m\geq(2^{18}\gamma)^{2/5}(n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\,.

Finally, we inspect the error term
-14mn^{2}\xi
. Recall that

\xi=\max(\xi(m,\mu),\xi(m,q))
, where

\xi(m,\mu)=\frac{e^{-m\|\mu\|_{1}}}{(1-e^{-m\|\mu\|_{1}})^{2}}=\frac{e^{-m/2}}{(1-e^{-m/2})^{2}}
and

\xi(m,q)=\frac{e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}}{(1-e^{-m\|q\|_{1}})^{2}}
. Using the bound

\|q\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}\geq\frac{1}{4}
as
n
and
m
grow,
we conclude that
\xi\leq 2e^{-m/4}
and therefore, using the (simplified) assumptions

\Omega(n^{4/5})\leq m\leq n
, we conclude that

mn^{2}\xi\leq n^{3}e^{-\Omega(n^{4/5})}=o(n^{-5/2})=o\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)=o\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)\,,

where we used the assumption
\epsilon\geq n^{-1/4}
in the penultimate step.
Returning to (
13
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m}{4n}\cdot F+G-H+mz^{\top}\phi z-14mn^{2}\xi\geq-3\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2^{12}n^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2^{8}\cdot n^{2}}+mz^{\top}\phi z

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)+mz^{\top}\phi z\,.\qed

4.4
Concentration of the Test Statistic

In this section, we start from the general results established in
Section

2.5.3

and conclude specific bounds for the variance of
\bm{Y}
in the current setting.

Lemma 4.16

(First component of the variance)
.

Let
C>0
be a constant, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large, and let
m
satisfy

m\leq\poly(n)
. Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, where
p,q
are partial distributions
satisfying
\|p\|_{1},\|q\|_{1}\geq 1/4
such that
p
is not
C
-highly concentrated relative
to
q
. Then

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\log^{6}n\right)\cdot p^{\top}\phi p+O(m^{2}e^{-m/8})\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.9
we have, for some absolute constant
c>0
,

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq 5m^{2}\zeta(\mathcal{I})\|p\|_{1}^{4}+cm^{2}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle\in\mathcal{I}\\
1\leq d\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}p[I]^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(I)\right]\,.

(14)

Our assumption that
p
is not highly concentrated relative to
q
gives the inequality

p[\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}\leq\left[C\log^{2}(n)\cdot\max\left\{q[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle],\frac{1}{m\log n}\right\}\right]^{2}\leq C^{2}\log^{4}(n)q[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}+\frac{C^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\,,

and therefore, with a small change of variables in
d
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle\in\mathcal{I}\\
1\leq d\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{i}p_{i+d-1}p[I]^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}[I]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]p[\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C^{2}\log^{4}(n)\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]q[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}+\frac{C^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]\,.

We show that both terms above satisfy our desired asymptotic bound. For the second term, note
that
\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)=1\implies\bm{\Phi}_{i,i+d}=1
, and therefore

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]\leq\phi_{i,i+d}
. Thus

\frac{C^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]\leq\frac{C^{2}\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}\phi_{i,i+d}=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\right)\cdot p^{\top}\phi p\,,

(15)

as desired. As for the first term, recall that

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\forall e\in\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle:e\in\bm{H}\right]=\prod_{e\in\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle}(1-w(e))=e^{-mq[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]}\,.

Let
K\geq 1
be a constant such that
m\leq n^{K}
for all sufficiently large
n
,
as per the assumption that
m\leq\poly(n)
.
Now, letting
x\vcentcolon=q[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]
, which is bounded between
0
and
1
,
we consider two cases. First, suppose
x\geq\frac{2(K+2)\log n}{m}
. Then we obtain

x^{2}e^{-mx}\leq 1\cdot e^{-2(K+2)\log n}=n^{-2K-4}\,.

On the other hand, if
x\leq\frac{2(K+2)\log n}{m}
, then

x^{2}e^{-mx}\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\right)e^{-mx}\,.

Therefore the first term is

\displaystyle C^{2}\log^{4}(n)\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}e^{-mq[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]}q[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq O\left(\log^{4}n\right)\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}\left(n^{-2K-4}+O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{m^{2}}\right)e^{-mq[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq O(n^{-2K-1})+O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m^{2}}\right)\cdot p^{\top}\phi p\,,

where again we used the inequality

e^{-mq[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]}=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{J}(\langle\!\langle i,d+1\rangle\!\rangle)\right]\leq\phi_{i,i+d}

in the last step.

To upper bound the term
O(n^{-2K-1})
, we recall that
m\leq n^{K}
and observe that,
since
\phi
is
1
on the diagonal and
\|p\|_{1}\geq 1/4
, we have

p^{\top}\phi p\geq\|p\|_{2}^{2}\geq\Omega(1/n)
. Therefore

n^{-2K-1}=\frac{1}{n^{2K}}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{1}{m^{2}}\cdot O(p^{\top}\phi p)\,.

It follows that

C^{2}\log^{4}(n)\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{d=0}^{n-1}p_{i}p_{i+d}e^{-mq[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]}q[\langle\!\langle i,d\rangle\!\rangle]^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m^{2}}\right)\cdot p^{\top}\phi p\,.

(16)

As for the error term
5m^{2}\zeta(\mathcal{I})\|p\|_{1}^{4}
, we upper bound
\|p\|_{1}
by
1

and recall that
\zeta(\mathcal{I})\leq e^{-\frac{m\|q\|_{1}}{2}}
by
Proposition

2.5
.
Along with the assumption that
\|q\|_{1}\geq 1/4
, we obtain

5m^{2}\zeta(\mathcal{I})\|p\|_{1}^{4}\leq O(m^{2}e^{-m/8})\,,

(17)

as needed.
Putting together (
15
),(
16
) and (
17
)
into (
14
), we conclude that

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O(\log^{6}n)p^{\top}\phi p+O(m^{2}e^{-m/8})\,.\qed

To make the result above useful, we need to upper bound the quadratic form
p^{\top}\phi p

by some quantity comparable to the separation shown in
Lemma

4.15
.
Recalling the breakdown in terms of an approximate uniform conjugate,
p=\widetilde{p}+z
,
our first task is to upper bound
\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
C>0
be a constant, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large, and suppose
m
satisfies

m=\omega(\log n)
,
m=o(n)
. Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, where
p
,
q
are partial distributions
satisfying
\|q\|_{1}\geq 1/4
such that
q
is not
C
-highly concentrated relative to
p
.
Let
\widetilde{p}
be an approximate uniform conjugate of
q
. Then

{\widetilde{p}}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}=O\left(\frac{\log n}{m}\right)\,.

Proof.

By
Proposition

4.9
,
{\widetilde{p}}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}=\|p\|(\tau\pm 4n\xi)
.
Our main task is to show that
\tau=O\left(\frac{\log n}{m}\right)
, but we first check
that
4n\xi
is small enough.
Indeed, from
Definition

4.8
and since
\|q\|_{1}\geq 1/4
, we have

n\xi=n\cdot\frac{e^{-m\|q\|_{1}}}{(1-e^{-m\|q\|_{1})^{2}}}\leq\frac{n\cdot e^{-\Omega(m)}}{(1-e^{-\Omega(m)})^{2}}=o(1/n)\leq o(1/m)\,,

the last two steps since
m=\omega(\log n)
,
m=o(n)
.
We now study
\tau
. Recall that

\tau=\frac{\|p\|_{1}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}\,.

Since
\tanh
is concave on the non-negative domain, our goal will be to upper bound
\tau
using
Jensen’s inequality. Write
q=\mu+y
. Let
S\vcentcolon=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}:y_{i}\geq 0\}
and

\overline{S}\vcentcolon=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}:y_{i}<0\}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}\setminus S
. Note that the vector
y^{+}

only takes non-zero entries in
S
, and
y^{-}
only takes non-zero entries in
\overline{S}
.
For each
i\in S
,
we have
\frac{1}{2n}\leq q_{i}\leq\frac{C\log n}{m}
, the upper bound since

\|q\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{C\log n}{m}
due to the anticoncentration assumption
(
Remark

4.5
). Then we may write

q_{i}=\lambda_{i}\cdot\frac{C\log n}{m}+(1-\lambda_{i})\cdot\frac{1}{2n}\,,\qquad\text{ where }\qquad\lambda_{i}=\frac{y_{i}}{\frac{C\log n}{m}-\frac{1}{2n}}\in[0,1]\,.

For
i\in\overline{S}
, we have
0\leq q_{i}<\frac{1}{2n}
, so we may
write

q_{i}=\lambda_{i}\cdot\frac{1}{2n}\,,\qquad\text{ where }\qquad\lambda_{i}=2nq_{i}\,.

Applying Jensen’s inequality, and using
\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)\geq\frac{m}{8n}
which
holds for sufficiently small
m/4n
(
4.12
),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S}\tanh\left(\lambda_{i}\cdot\frac{C\log n}{2}+(1-\lambda_{i})\cdot\frac{m}{4n}\right)+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\tanh\left(\lambda_{i}\cdot\frac{m}{4n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in S}\left[\lambda_{i}\tanh\left(\frac{C\log n}{2}\right)+(1-\lambda_{i})\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)\right]+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\lambda_{i}\tanh\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S}\lambda_{i}+\sum_{i\in S}(1-\lambda_{i})\cdot\frac{m}{8n}+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\lambda_{i}\cdot\frac{m}{8n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S}\lambda_{i}+\frac{m}{8n}\left(\sum_{i\in S}(1-\lambda_{i})+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\lambda_{i}\right)\,,

where we have used the fact that
\tanh\left(\frac{C\log n}{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}
for
sufficiently large
n
. We consider two cases. First assume that
\|y^{+}\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{8}
. Then

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S}\lambda_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{m}{C\log n-\frac{m}{2n}}\|y^{+}\|_{1}\geq\frac{m}{16C\log n}\,,

and therefore

\tau=\frac{\|p\|_{1}}{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)}\leq\frac{16C\log n}{m}\,,

as desired. Next assume that
\|y^{+}\|_{1}<\frac{1}{8}
. Observe that for
i\in S
,

\lambda_{i}=\frac{y_{i}}{\frac{C\log n}{m}-\frac{1}{2n}}=\frac{my_{i}}{C\log n-\frac{m}{2n}}\leq\frac{2my_{i}}{C\log n}\,,

since
\frac{m}{2n}\leq\frac{1}{2}C\log n
.
So

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S}(1-\lambda_{i})+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in S}\left(1-\frac{2my_{i}}{C\log n}\right)+2n\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}q_{i}=\sum_{i\in S}\left(1-\frac{2my_{i}}{C\log n}\right)+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}(1+2ny_{i})

\displaystyle=|S|-\frac{2m}{C\log n}\|y^{+}\|_{1}+|\overline{S}|-2n\|y^{-}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=n-\frac{2m}{C\log n}\|y^{+}\|_{1}-2n\|y^{-}\|_{1}\,.

Recalling the assumption that
\|q\|_{1}\geq 1/4
, we now observe that

\displaystyle\|q\|_{1}=\sum_{i\in S}\left(\frac{1}{2n}+|y_{i}|\right)+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\left(\frac{1}{2n}-|y_{i}|\right)=\frac{1}{2}+\|y^{+}\|_{1}-\|y^{-}\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{4}\,,

so
\|y^{-}\|_{1}\leq\|y^{+}\|_{1}+\frac{1}{4}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S}(1-\lambda_{i})+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle\geq n-\frac{2m}{C\log n}\|y^{+}\|_{1}-2n\|y^{-}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq n-\frac{2m}{C\log n}\|y^{+}\|_{1}-2n\left(\|y^{+}\|_{1}+\frac{1}{4}\right)

\displaystyle=n-\|y^{+}\|_{1}\left(\frac{2m}{C\log n}+2n\right)-\frac{n}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{2m}{C\log n}+2n\right)=\left(\frac{1}{4}-o(1)\right)n\,.

We conclude that

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\tanh\left(\frac{mq_{i}}{2}\right)\geq\frac{m}{8n}\left(\sum_{i\in S}(1-\lambda_{i})+\sum_{i\in\overline{S}}\lambda_{i}\right)\geq\frac{m}{8n}\left(\frac{1}{4}-o(1)\right)n\geq\frac{m}{33}\,,

for sufficiently large
n
, in which case
\tau\leq\frac{33}{m}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.18
.

Under the assumptions of
Lemma

4.16
and

Proposition

4.17
, and writing
p=\widetilde{p}+z
,
the first component of the variance satisfies

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{7}n}{m}\right)+O\left(\log^{6}n\right)z^{\top}\phi z\,.

Proof.

Note that
m^{2}e^{-m/8}=o(1/m)
since
m=\omega(\log n)=\omega(1)
.
Thus
Lemma

4.16
, along with the breakdown
p=\widetilde{p}+z
, yields

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\log^{6}n\right)\cdot(\widetilde{p}+z)^{\top}\phi(\widetilde{p}+z)+O(m^{2}e^{-m/8})

\displaystyle=O\left(\log^{6}n\right)\left(\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}+z^{\top}\phi z+2z^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}\right)+o(1/m)\,.

By a similar argument as in the proof of
Proposition

4.9
, we have

z^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}\leq 4n^{2}\xi=o(1/m)
, the last step as in
the proof of
Proposition

4.17
.
Applying
Proposition

4.17
to the term

\widetilde{p}^{\top}\phi\widetilde{p}
, we obtain

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{7}n}{m}\right)+O\left(\log^{6}n\right)z^{\top}\phi z\,.\qed

We now upper bound the second component of the variance. The key step is to show that, with
high probability, no bucket contains too much probability mass:

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
C,K>0
be constants.
Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
where
p,q
are partial distributions such that
p
is not
C
-highly
concentrated relative to
q
. Then the random bucketing

\bm{\Gamma}=(\bm{\Gamma}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{\Gamma}_{\bm{b}})
induced by
\bm{H}
satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j}p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]\geq 2(K+1)\frac{C\log^{3}n}{m}\right]<2/n^{K}\,.

Proof.

Fix any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and let
\bm{\Gamma}_{j}
be the (random) bucket containing
i
. We wish to
bound the probability that
p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]>2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}
.
Let
R=\langle\!\langle i,d_{R}\rangle\!\rangle
be the minimal circular interval in the clockwise direction
starting at
i
satisfying
p[R]\geq(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}
. Likewise, let

L=\langle\!\langle i,-d_{L}\rangle\!\rangle
be the minimal circular interval in the counterclockwise direction
starting at
i
satisfying
p[L]\geq(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}
. Observe that, if

p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]\geq 2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}
, then the bucket contains at least one of these
intervals:
\mathcal{E}(L)\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\bm{\Gamma}_{j})
or
\mathcal{E}(R)\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\bm{\Gamma}_{j})
,
and therefore
\bm{J}(L)=1
or
\bm{J}(R)=1
.

Since
p
is not
C
-highly concentrated relative to
q
, we have

(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\leq p[R]<C\log^{2}(n)\cdot\max\{q[R^{*}],\frac{1}{m\log n}\}
.
Therefore, it must be the case that

q[R^{*}]>\frac{p[R]}{C\log^{2}n}\geq(K+1)\frac{\log n}{m}\,.

Now

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(R)=1\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\forall z\in R^{*}:z\in\bm{H}\right]=\prod_{z\in R^{*}}(1-w(z))=\prod_{z\in R^{*}}e^{-mq_{z}}=e^{-mq[R^{*}]}<e^{-(K+1)\log n}=\frac{1}{n^{K+1}}\,.

The same holds for
\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{J}(L)=1\right]
. Then, by the union bound over
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j}p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]\geq 2(K+1)\frac{C\log^{3}n}{m}\right]<2/n^{K}\,.\qed

Lemma 4.20

(Second component of the variance)
.

Let
C>0
be a constant.
Let
n
be sufficiently large and suppose
m
satisfies
m\leq\poly(n)
.
Suppose
\pi=\pi(p,q)
where
p,q
are partial distributions such that
p
is not
C
-highly
concentrated relative to
q
. Then we have

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m}\right)\,.

Proof.

We start from the general result from
Lemma

2.12
:
for some absolute constant
c>0
, for every
H
in the support of
\bm{H}
with induced
buckets
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\dotsc,\Gamma_{b})
,

\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]\leq c\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+cm\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}\,.

Let
K>0
be a constant such that
m\leq n^{K/2}
for all sufficiently large
n
,
which exists by the assumption that
m\leq\poly(n)
.
First, suppose the subgraph
H
induces bucketing
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\dotsc,\Gamma_{b})
satisfying

\max_{j}p[\Gamma_{j}]\leq 2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}
. Since
\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{1}=\|p\|_{1}\leq 1
,
we can upper bound the values that
\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}
and
\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}
can take
by distributing
1
total weight in a maximally concentrated way, i. e. meeting the per-bucket
upper bound we have just assumed. Therefore, we obtain

\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left(2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\left(2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)}=O\left(\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)

and

\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}\leq\left(2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)^{3}\cdot\frac{1}{\left(2(K+1)C\frac{\log^{3}n}{m}\right)}=O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m^{2}}\right)\,.

Therefore, in this case, we have

\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m}\right)\,.

On the other hand, since
\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{1}\leq 1
, every
H
satisfies the simpler bound

\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}=H\right.\right]\leq c\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+cm\|p_{|\Gamma}\|_{3}^{3}=O(m)\,.

Using
Proposition

4.19
, we write

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j}p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]\leq\frac{2(K+1)C\log^{3}n}{m}\right]\cdot\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\;\;\left|\;\;\max_{j}p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]\leq\frac{2(K+1)C\log^{3}n}{m}\right.\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j}p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]>\frac{2(K+1)C\log n}{m}\right]\cdot\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\;\;\left|\;\;\max_{j}p[\bm{\Gamma}_{j}]>\frac{2(K+1)C\log^{3}n}{m}\right.\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m}\right)+O(m)\cdot\frac{2}{n^{K}}\,,

and since
m\leq n^{K/2}
, we have
O(m)\cdot\frac{2}{n^{K}}\leq O(1/n^{K/2})\leq O(1/m)
,
as needed.
∎

We can now use the law of total variance to combine these results into a concentration bound for
the test statistic:

Lemma 4.21

(Concentration of the Test Statistic)
.

Let
C>0
be a constant and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large. Suppose
m=m(n,\epsilon)

satisfies
m\leq\poly(n)
. Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, where
p,q
are partial distributions
satisfying
\|p\|_{1},\|q\|_{1}\geq 1/4
such that
p
is not
C
-highly concentrated
relative to
q
. Then for all
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]\right\rvert\geq t\right]\leq\frac{\frac{1}{m}+p^{\top}\phi p}{t^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{6}n)\,.

Moreover, suppose
q
is not
C
-highly concentrated relative to
p
and
m
satisfies

m=\omega(\log n)
,
m=o(n)
. Then writing
p=\widetilde{p}+z
where
\widetilde{p}

is an approximate uniform conjugate of
q
, we also have

\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]\right\rvert\geq t\right]\leq\frac{\frac{1}{m}+z^{\top}\phi z}{t^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\,.

Proof.

By the law of total variance,

\mathrm{Var}\left[\bm{Y}\right]=\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]+\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\,.

The first term is bounded by

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\log^{6}n\right)\cdot p^{\top}\phi p+O(m^{2}e^{-m/8})

by
Lemma

4.16
, and the second term is bounded by

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\frac{\log^{6}n}{m}\right)

by
Lemma

4.20
. Moreover, for any constant
c>0
,
the function
m\mapsto m^{3}e^{-cm}
has a global maximum of

\frac{3^{3}}{e^{3}c^{3}}
, and therefore

m^{2}e^{-m/8}=O(1/m)
. The first statement follows from Chebyshev’s inequality.

Making also the second set of assumptions,
Corollary

4.18
implies that

\underset{\bm{H}}{\mathrm{Var}}\left[\underset{\bm{T}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{Y}\;\;\left|\;\;\bm{H}\right.\right]\right]\leq O\left(\frac{1}{m}+z^{\top}\phi z\right)\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\,,

so the second statement follows again from Chebyshev’s inequality.
∎

4.5
Correctness of the Tester for Large
\epsilon

We can use our separation and concentration results above to show that
\bm{Y}
is concentrated
on the correct side of the tester’s threshold. Combining this with the easy cases of biased and
highly concentrated distributions will yield the correctness result.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
\alpha,\gamma>0
be constants. There exist constants

\beta=\beta_{\alpha,\gamma}>0
and
K=K_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}>1

such that the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
.
Suppose
\epsilon\geq\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
.
Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, where
p,q
are partial
distributions satisfying
\|p\|_{1},\|q\|_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}

and suppose that
p,q
are not
4\alpha
-highly concentrated relative to the other.

Let
T\vcentcolon=\frac{m}{4n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\phi^{(\mu)}_{i,j}+\beta\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}
be the threshold used by

Algorithm

2
.
Let
\bm{Y}^{(0)}
and
\bm{Y}^{(1)}
be random variables denoting the value of the test
statistic
\bm{Y}
in the iterations
b=0
and
b=1
of the algorithm, respectively.
Then when

m=\Theta_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n\right)
,
the following statements hold:

1.

(Completeness) If
\pi(p,q)
is the uniform distribution over
[2n]
, then

\max\{\bm{Y}^{(0)},\bm{Y}^{(1)}\}<T
with probability at least
99/100
; and

2.

(Soundness) If
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi(p,q),\pi(\mu,\mu))>\epsilon
,
then
\max\{\bm{Y}^{(0)},\bm{Y}^{(1)}\}>T
with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

Note that we can simply write
T=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+\beta\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}

by
Proposition

4.7
.

Completeness.

In this case,
\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(0)}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]
.
Moreover, in this case we can write
p=\widetilde{p}+z
for
\widetilde{p}=\mu
and

z=\vec{0}
since
\mu
is its own uniform conjugate by
Lemma

4.11
.
Hence
Lemma

4.21
gives

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\geq T\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]\right\rvert\geq\beta\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right]\leq\frac{\frac{1}{m}+z^{\top}\phi z}{\left(\beta\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)=\frac{n^{4}}{\beta^{2}\epsilon^{4}m^{5}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\,.

Thus for any constant
\beta
(to be chosen below), this probability is at most (say)
1/200

when
m=\Omega((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n)
.
By symmetry, the same is true for
\bm{Y}^{(0)}
, and hence the probability that

\max\{\bm{Y}^{(0)},\bm{Y}^{(1)}\}<T
fails to hold is at most
1/100
, as desired.

Soundness.

Without loss of generality, it suffices to consider the case when
\|q-\mu\|_{1}>\epsilon

and show that
\bm{Y}^{(1)}>T
with probability at least
99/100
.

Since
\epsilon\geq\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
and

m=\Theta_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n)
,
for any value of
\beta

we can ensure that
\frac{m}{n/\log n}
is smaller than any constant by making

K=K_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
sufficiently large. Indeed, for some constant

A=A_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}>0
and sufficiently large
n
, we have

m\leq A\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n\leq\frac{An\log^{7/5}n}{K^{4/5}\log^{12/5}n}=\frac{A}{K^{4/5}}\cdot\frac{n}{\log n}\,,

which can be made sufficiently small by making
K
sufficiently large.
Therefore the conditions of
Lemma

4.15
are satisfied and we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)+mz^{\top}\phi z\,.

For concreteness, let
L=L_{\alpha,\gamma}>0
be a constant such that,
for sufficiently large
n
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(\mu)}\right]+L\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}+mz^{\top}\phi z\,.

Then as long as
\beta<L/2
,
Lemma

4.21
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\leq T\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]\leq\beta\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}-L\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}-mz^{\top}\phi z\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]\right\rvert\geq(L-\beta)\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}+mz^{\top}\phi z\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\bm{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\right]\right\rvert\geq\frac{L}{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}+mz^{\top}\phi z\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\frac{1}{m}+z^{\top}\phi z}{\left(\frac{L}{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n^{2}}+mz^{\top}\phi z\right)^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\frac{1}{m}+z^{\top}\phi z}{\frac{L^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{4}m^{4}}{n^{4}}+m^{2}\left(z^{\top}\phi z\right)^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\,,

the last step since
z^{\top}\phi z\geq 0
due to the positive semidefiniteness of
\phi
.
We now consider two cases. First, suppose
z^{\top}\phi z\leq 1/m
. Then

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\leq T\right]\leq\frac{2/m}{\frac{L^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{4}m^{4}}{n^{4}}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)=\frac{n^{4}}{L^{2}\epsilon^{4}m^{5}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\,,

which is again at most
1/200
. On the other hand, suppose
z^{\top}\phi z\geq 1/m
. Then

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\leq T\right]\leq\frac{1/m}{\frac{L^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{4}m^{4}}{n^{4}}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)+\frac{z^{\top}\phi z}{m^{2}(z^{\top}\phi z)^{2}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\leq\frac{n^{4}}{L^{2}\epsilon^{4}m^{5}}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)+\frac{1}{m}\cdot O(\log^{7}n)\,.

We have already seen that the first term is at most
1/200
, and the second term is clearly

o(1)
. Hence
\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}^{(1)}\leq T\right]\leq 1/100
, concluding the proof.
∎

We may now combine the previous results to conclude the correctness of the tester:

Theorem 4.23
.

There exist constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma>0
and
K>1
such that the following holds
for all sufficiently large
n
.
Suppose
\epsilon\geq\frac{K\log^{3}n}{n^{1/4}}
.
Let
\pi=\pi(p,q)
, where
p,q
are partial distributions.

Then
Algorithm

2
instantiated with constants

\alpha,\beta,\text{and }\gamma

has sample complexity
\Theta\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\log^{7/5}n\right)

and satisfies the following:

1.

(Completeness) If
\pi(p,q)
is the uniform distribution over
[2n]
, the algorithm
accepts with probability at least
9/10
; and

2.

(Soundness) If
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi(p,q),\pi(\mu,\mu))>\epsilon
, the algorithm
rejects with probability at least
9/10
.

Proof.

We first instantiate sufficiently large
\alpha,\gamma>0
, sufficiently small
\beta>0
and
sufficiently large
K>1
(in this order) to satisfy the conditions of

Propositions

4.3
,
4.6
and
4.22
.
The sample complexity follows from the definition of the algorithm; we now show that it
correctly accepts/rejects.

Completeness.

By
Propositions

4.3
,
4.6
and
4.22
,
the algorithm rejects with probability at most

1/100+1/100+1/100<1/10
.

Soundness.

We consider three cases. First, suppose
\|p\|_{1}\not\in\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
.
Then by
Proposition

4.3
, the algorithm rejects with probability at least
99/100
.

Second, suppose
p
or
q
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated relative to the other. Then
by
Proposition

4.6
, the algorithm rejects with probability at least
99/100
.

Finally, suppose
\|p\|_{1},\|q\|_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{2\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}
and neither
p

nor
q
is
4\alpha
-highly concentrated. Then by
Lemma

4.22
,
the algorithm rejects with probability at least
99/100
, as desired.
∎

Combining
Theorems

4.23
and
4.2

establishes the upper bound portion of
Theorem

4.1
.

5
Lower Bound for Testing Uniformity in the Parity Trace Model

Notation

In this section, let
\mu
denote the partial distribution for domain
[2n]

with total mass
1/2
uniformly distributed over its support, so that
\pi(\mu,\mu)
is the uniform
distribution over
[2n]
.

We wish to prove the following result:

Theorem 5.1

(Lower bound portion of
Theorem

4.1
)
.

Let
\Pi_{1}
contain only the uniform distribution over
[2n]
, and let
\Pi_{2}
be the set of
distributions over
[2n]
that are
\epsilon
-far from uniform in total variation distance. Then

(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},51/100)
-testing under the parity trace requires sample complexity at least

\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
,
where the
\widetilde{\Omega}
notation only hides polylogarithmic factors in
n
.
Furthermore, this bound holds even if the input distribution

\pi
is guaranteed to have
1/2
mass uniformly distributed over the zero-valued (i. e. even)
coordinates.

We divide the analysis into two parts: a reduction from the standard uniformity testing model,
which establishes an
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
lower bound, and a more sophisticated argument
that applies when
\epsilon\geq n^{-1/4}
; fortunately, this is precisely the regime where

(n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\geq\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2}
. First, the easier bound:

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\Pi_{1}
contain only the uniform distribution over
[2n]
, and let
\Pi_{2}
be the set of
distributions over
[2n]
that are
\epsilon
-far from uniform in total variation distance. Then

(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},51/100)
-testing under the parity trace requires sample complexity at least

\Omega(\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.
Furthermore, this bound holds even if the input distribution

\pi
is guaranteed to have
1/2
mass uniformly distributed over the zero-valued (i. e. even)
coordinates.

Proof.

We reduce from testing uniformity of a distribution over
[n]
, for which there is a lower bound of

\Omega(\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})

[
Pan08
]
. For input distribution
\pi
over
[n]
, let
\pi^{\prime}
be
the distribution on
[2n]
defined by setting
\pi^{\prime}(2i-1)=\pi(i)/2
for each
i\in[n]
and

\pi(2i)=\tfrac{1}{2n}
for
i\in[n]
, so that
\pi^{\prime}
is uniform over the even elements.
Observe that we may simulate a sample from
\pi^{\prime}
by sampling
\bm{x}\sim\pi
and taking

2\bm{x}-1
with probability
1/2
, and otherwise taking a uniformly random even element of
[2n]
.
Then the following hold:

1.

If
\pi
is uniform over
[n]
then
\pi^{\prime}
is uniform over
[2n]
; and

2.

If
\pi
is
\epsilon
-far from uniform then
\pi^{\prime}
is
\epsilon/2
-far from uniform (with
respect to TV distance).

Therefore the tester for uniformity may simulate the parity trace tester with parameter

\epsilon/2
.
∎

We now give our main technical argument to show the

\widetilde{\Omega}\left((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}\right)
bound for the case
\epsilon\geq n^{-1/4}
.

5.1
Outline of the Argument

Our approach, inspired by
[
DK16
]
, is to construct distributions over YES and NO inputs
such that, when
\bm{Z}
is a random variable indicating the YES/NO case and
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
is the input
to the algorithm (a parity trace drawn from a YES or NO distribution),
the mutual information
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})

is small, so that no algorithm can predict
\bm{Z}
from
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
with good probability.
Concretely, we follow
[
DK16
]
and use the following simple consequence of Fano’s inequality:

Fact 5.3

(Fano’s inequality)
.

Suppose
\bm{Z}
is a uniform random bit,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
is a random variable, and there exists
a function
f
such that
f(\bm{\mathcal{T}})=\bm{Z}
with probability at least
51\%
.
Then
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
.

Therefore, our goal is to construct “distributions over distributions”
(hereby called
distributions
)

\mathcal{D}_{0}
(YES case) and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
(NO case), which are supported on distributions
\pi
over
[2n]
,
satisfying the following: let
m=m(n,\epsilon)
be the sample complexity of the tester, and
assume the
Poissonized setting (which will be convenient later). Then we want to satisfy the following:

1.

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is supported on a single element
\pi(\mu,\mu)
,
the uniform distribution over
[2n]
;

2.

Every
\pi
in the support of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
satisfies

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi(\mu,\mu))\geq\Omega(\epsilon)
; and

3.

Let
\bm{Z}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(1/2)
, and
\bm{\pi}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\bm{Z}}
.
Let
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
be distributed as follows: draw
\bm{S}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\bm{\pi},\mathsf{Poi}(m))

and let
\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\mathsf{trace}(\bm{S})
.
Then when
m=o\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\right)
,
we have
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})=o(1)
.

We now outline the main ingredients of our proof, and then present the full argument.
For simplicity, we will assume that
n
is even.

YES and NO distributions.

Recall that a probability distribution
\pi=\pi(p,q)
over
[2n]
consists of partial
distributions
p
over the 1-valued elements (odd indices) and
q
over the 0-valued elements
(even indices). We will partition the domain
[2n]
into
n/2
consecutive
length-4 intervals, called
dominoes
, such that the
i
-th domino determines the entries

(p_{j},q_{j},p_{j+1},q_{j+1})
, where
j=2i-1
, and contributes to the trace a string (called
a
subtrace
) distributed as

1^{\bm{A}_{j}}0^{\bm{B}_{j}}1^{\bm{A}_{j+1}}0^{\bm{B}_{j+1}}\,,

where
\bm{A}_{k}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(mp_{k}),\bm{B}_{k}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(mq_{k})
independently for each
k\in[n]
.

We will always set
q=\mu
, i. e. the partial distribution over the 0-valued elements is
uniform with total mass
1/2
. In the YES distribution
\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
p=\mu
as well.
In the NO distribution
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, we will set either

(p_{j},p_{j+1})=\left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{2n},\frac{1-\epsilon}{2n}\right)
or

(p_{j},p_{j+1})=\left(\frac{1-\epsilon}{2n},\frac{1+\epsilon}{2n}\right)
,
with equal probability and independently for each domino. Hence each domino is “balanced”
and the subtraces produced by different dominoes are independent conditional on
\bm{Z}
.
Moreover, we will show that sampling at most 2 symbols from a domino reveals no information about

\bm{Z}
, i. e. only 3-way or larger collisions are informative.

Partial fingerprints.

Since each domino is uninformative if at most 2 symbols are drawn from it, we will study the
distributional properties of those dominoes from which a larger number of symbols was
sampled—this is where information about
\bm{Z}
may be revealed to the algorithm.
Drawing inspiration from standard distribution testing theory, we will study the
partial
fingerprint
over the dominoes, which essentially measures how many information-revealing symbols
were sampled.

Roughly speaking, we will show that the probability of a partial fingerprint decreases exponentially
in the number of information-revealing sample elements (namely, those coming from dominoes
from which 3 or more symbols were drawn), which places an
upper bound on how much the algorithm can learn from these elements. We remark that the lower
bound argument of
[
DKN15a
]
for testing closeness of structured distributions uses the similar
idea of constructing a gadget from which up to two samples are distributed identically under YES
and NO conditions.

Partition of the domain and chain rule of mutual information.

Given the observations above, one might hope to conclude the argument by 1) upper bounding the
mutual information between
\bm{Z}
and the subtrace from each domino; and 2) adding up, by
the chain rule of mutual information, this quantity over all the dominoes.
(If random variables
\bm{T}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{T}_{k}
are independent conditional on
\bm{Z}
,
the chain rule of mutual information implies that

I(\bm{T}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{T}_{k}:\bm{Z})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}I(\bm{T}_{i}:\bm{Z})
.)
Unfortunately,
this strategy does not give a good bound; intuitively, it assumes that the
algorithm ‘‘knows’’ too much---namely the boundaries of all the dominoes in the trace it sees,
which, in reality, should be very difficult to predict.
13
13

13

Another interesting attempt is to
condition the analysis on the identities of the 0-valued symbols seen in the trace, and
then consider the distribution of the 1-valued symbols inside each range delimited by the zeroes.
This also seems to fail for a similar reason: by the birthday paradox, when one
draws
n^{4/5}
samples from
[2n]
, many of the intervals delimited by the 0-valued symbols
will be very small, which also amounts to “revealing” too much information.

As it turns out, one solution is to consider
\Theta(m)
contiguous ranges, each consisting
of
\Theta(n/m)
dominoes. Since we sample
\mathsf{Poi}(m)
symbols in total, the expected number of
symbols sampled from each such range is
\Theta(1)
, which makes the analysis tractable, and adding
up the contribution from each of these ranges to the mutual information gives the desired bound.

5.2
Construction of YES and NO Distributions

We now formally define dominoes, subtraces, and the YES and NO distributions.

Definition 5.4

(Dominoes)
.

For any integer
i\in[n/2]
, let
j=2i-1
and
j^{\prime}=4i-3
.
We call the range
\{j^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime}+2,j^{\prime}+3\}
of the domain
[2n]
, along with the probability
masses of
p
and
q
associated with these positions (namely
p_{j},q_{j},p_{j+1},q_{j+1}
)
the
i
-th domino
.

In particular, we categorize dominoes as one of three kinds according to the probability
masses of its
p
entries (which will be chosen differently under the YES and NO distributions):

1.

Unbiased
: when
p_{j}=p_{j+1}=\frac{1}{2n}
.

2.

Left
\epsilon
-biased
: when
p_{j}=\frac{1}{2n}(1+\epsilon)
and

p_{j+1}=\frac{1}{2n}(1-\epsilon)
.

3.

Right
\epsilon
-biased
: when
p_{j}=\frac{1}{2n}(1-\epsilon)
and

p_{j+1}=\frac{1}{2n}(1+\epsilon)
.

Definition 5.5

(Subtraces)
.

Given a probability distribution
\pi(p,q)
over
[2n]
, and for each
i\in[n/2]
, we say
that the
subtrace produced by the
i
-th domino
is the random binary string

\bm{t}_{i}\vcentcolon=1^{\bm{A}_{j}}0^{\bm{B}_{j}}1^{\bm{A}_{j+1}}0^{\bm{B}_{j+1}}\,,

where
j=2i-1
and
\bm{A}_{k}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(mp_{k}),\bm{B}_{k}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(mq_{k})
independently.

Given a contiguous range of
r
dominoes indexed by
\{i,i+1,\dotsc,i+r-1\}
, the subtrace
produced by this range of dominoes is

\bm{T}_{i,r}\vcentcolon=\bm{t}_{i}\circ\dotsc\circ\bm{t}_{i+r-1}\,,

where
\circ
stands for concatenation.

Observation 5.6
.

Recall that any domino has
q_{j}=q_{j+1}=1/2n
, i. e. the partial distribution over the
0-valued elements is uniform with total mass
1/2
. Therefore each domino satisfies

p_{j}+q_{j}+p_{j+1}+q_{j+1}=2/n\,,

and therefore the length of the subtrace produced by each domino is independently distributed
as
\mathsf{Poi}(2m/n)
regardless of the value of
\bm{Z}
.

Using the definitions above, we can see that the full trace
\mathcal{T}
is distributed as

\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\bm{t}_{1}\circ\dotsm\circ\bm{t}_{n/2}\,.

Alternatively, if we partition the set of all dominoes into contiguous ranges

\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{1}+r_{1}-1\},\dotsc,\{i_{k},\dotsc,i_{k}+r_{k}-1\}
, then

\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\bm{T}_{i_{1},r_{1}}\circ\dotsm\circ\bm{T}_{i_{k},r_{k}}\,.

We now define the YES and NO distributions.

Definition 5.7

(YES and NO distributions)
.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
The
YES
distribution

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a distribution supported on a single element

\pi(\mu,\mu)
, the uniform distribution over
[2n]
. The
NO
distribution

\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a distribution supported on distributions over
[2n]
drawn as follows: for each

i\in[n/2]
, make the
i
-th domino left
\epsilon
-biased or right
\epsilon
-biased, with
equal probability independently for each domino.

When we are thinking of the distribution
\pi(p,q)
as a random variable drawn from these
distributions, we will accordingly write
\bm{\pi}=\pi(\bm{p},\bm{q})
.

Observation 5.8
.

Every
\pi(p,q)
in the support of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
satisfies

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi(p,q),\pi(\mu,\mu))=\epsilon/4
.

Therefore, we seek to show the following result:

Claim 5.9
.

Let
\bm{Z}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(1/2)
, and let
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
be a parity trace of size
\mathsf{Poi}(m)
sampled from
distribution
\bm{\pi}
over
[2n]
, where
\bm{\pi}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\bm{Z}}
. Then if

m=o\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\right)
, it follows
that
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})=o(1)
.

5.3
Partial Fingerprints and their Probabilities

In the standard model of distribution testing, the
fingerprint
of a sample is a complete
description of the relevant information for testing symmetric properties of discrete
distributions
[
Bat01
]
. The fingerprint is the “histogram of the histogram”: for each positive
integer
k
, the number of elements that occurred exactly
k
times in the sample.

In our construction, we would like to analyze the fingerprint over the
dominoes
, as follows.
For each
i
, let
d_{i}
be the number of trace symbols produced from the
i
-th domino.
Then
d
is our histogram and the corresponding fingerprint counts, for each positive integer
k
,
how many trace symbols came from dominoes satisfying
d_{i}=k
.

Studying the fingerprint over the dominoes is useful because, as our analysis will show,
each domino is uninformative about
\bm{Z}
when at most 2 symbols are sampled it, and
when 3 or more symbols are sampled, the amount of information revealed grows according to the number
of symbols. This phenomenon suggests that we consider a
partial fingerprint
, which is
obtained from the fingerprint by collapsing the counts corresponding to all integers
k\geq 3

into a single category “
3^{+}
”. We give the following equivalent formulation, which is more
convenient for our analysis:

Definition 5.10

(
(h,k,s)
-collisions)
.

Consider the process of throwing
b
identical balls into
r
bins, each ball at a bin
selected independently uniformly at random. A vector
d=(d_{1},\dotsc,d_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}

such that
d_{i}
is the number of balls in the
i
-th bin, for each
i\in[r]
, is called
an
outcome
of this process.

We say that outcome
d
is an
(h,k,s)
-collision
if, among the
r
bins,
exactly
h
of them contain exactly two balls,
exactly
k
of them contain at least three balls and, moreover, the total number of balls in
those
k
bins is
k+s
(in other words,
s
is the number of “surplus” balls in the bins
with at least 3 balls). We define
\mathcal{C}_{r,b}(h,k,s)
, the

set of
(h,k,s)
-collision outcomes
, as

\mathcal{C}_{r,b}(h,k,s)\vcentcolon=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(d_{1},\dotsc,d_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}:\\
&\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}=b,\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}=2\right]=h,\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right]=k,\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right]\cdot(d_{i}-1)=s\end{aligned}\right\}\,.

Note that
\mathcal{C}_{r,b}(h,k,s)=\emptyset
whenever
s<2k
,
and similarly when
b<2h
or
b<k+s
.

Note that the random vector
\bm{d}
expressing the outcome of the random process described above
is distributed as
\bm{d}\sim\mathsf{Multinomial}(b,r,(1/r,\dotsc,1/r))
.
We show that, for appropriate range of values,
(h,k,s)
-collisions are exponentially
unlikely in
h
and
s
:

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
\bm{d}=(\bm{d}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{d}_{r})\sim\mathsf{Multinomial}(b,r,(1/r,\dotsc,1/r))
.
Then for each
h\geq 0
and
k,s>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}\in C_{r,b}(h,k,s)\right]\leq\left(\frac{(bh)^{2}}{r}\right)^{h}\left(\frac{(bk)^{3/2}}{r}\right)^{s}\,,

where
0^{0}
is interpreted as
1
.

Proof.

We can upper bound this probability by ranging over which bins will contain exactly two
balls, if any—call these “
2
-collisions”—and three or more balls—call
these “
3^{+}
-collisions”—, as well as which balls fall into those bins,
and then roughly upper bounding the combinatorial quantities determining each.
Let notation
\binom{[n]}{m}
denote the set of subsets of
[n]
of size
m
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}\in C_{r,b}(h,k,s)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{I_{2}\in\binom{[r]}{h}}\sum_{I_{3}\in\binom{[r]\setminus I_{2}}{k}}\sum_{J_{2}\in\binom{[b]}{2h}}\sum_{J_{3}\in\binom{[b]\setminus J_{2}}{k+s}}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbb{P}\left[\text{balls $J_{2}$ form $2$-collisions on bins $I_{2}$}\right]\\
\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[\text{balls $J_{3}$ form $3^{+}$-collisions on bins $I_{3}$}\right]\\
\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[\text{balls $[b]\setminus(J_{2}\cup J_{3})$ fall on bins
$[r]\setminus(I_{2}\cup I_{3})$ without collisions}\right]\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{I_{2}\in\binom{[r]}{h}}\sum_{I_{3}\in\binom{[r]\setminus I_{2}}{k}}\sum_{J_{2}\in\binom{[b]}{2h}}\sum_{J_{3}\in\binom{[b]\setminus J_{2}}{k+s}}\mathbb{P}\left[\text{balls $J_{2}$ fall within bins $I_{2}$}\right]\mathbb{P}\left[\text{balls $J_{3}$ fall within bins $I_{3}$}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\binom{r}{h}\binom{r}{k}\binom{b}{2h}\binom{b}{k+s}\left(\frac{h}{r}\right)^{2h}\left(\frac{k}{r}\right)^{k+s}

\displaystyle\leq r^{h+k}b^{2h+k+s}h^{2h}k^{k+s}r^{-2h-k-s}

\displaystyle\leq(bh)^{2h}r^{-h}\cdot(bk)^{\frac{3}{2}s}r^{-s}\hskip 250.00038pt\text{(Since $k\leq s/2$)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(bh)^{2}}{r}\right)^{h}\left(\frac{(bk)^{3/2}}{r}\right)^{s}\,,

where we used
k\leq s/2
which holds unless the probability is zero, in which case the
conclusion follows trivially.
∎

We will also need the following simple “birthday problem” bound:

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
\bm{d}=(\bm{d}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{d}_{r})\sim\mathsf{Multinomial}(b,r,(1/r,\dotsc,1/r))
.
Then the probability of seeing no collisions satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}\in\mathcal{C}_{r,b}(0,0,0)\right]\geq 1-\frac{b^{2}}{r}\,.

Proof.

This probability is

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}\in\mathcal{C}_{r,b}(0,0,0)\right]=\frac{r\cdot(r-1)\dotsm(r-b+1)}{r^{b}}\geq\left(\frac{r-b}{r}\right)^{b}=\left(1-\frac{b}{r}\right)^{b}\geq 1-\frac{b^{2}}{r}\,.

∎

5.4
YES and NO Dominoes Behave Similarly

We now show that each subtrace that is not too long must have similar probabilities of being
produced by a domino under the YES and NO distributions.

We first need the following simple bound, which informally encapsulates the property that the
“information” revealed by a domino decays as
O(\epsilon^{2})
even though its relative probability
masses are
\Theta(\epsilon)
-biased.

Proposition 5.13
.

For all non-negative integers
x
and
y
, and all
0<\epsilon<1
,

\frac{1}{2}(1+\epsilon)^{x}(1-\epsilon)^{y}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)^{x}(1+\epsilon)^{y}=1\pm\epsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{x+y}\,.

Proof.

By the binomial theorem, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(1+\epsilon)^{x}(1-\epsilon)^{y}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)^{x}(1+\epsilon)^{y}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{x}\binom{x}{i}\epsilon^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{y}\binom{y}{j}(-1)^{j}\epsilon^{j}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{x}\binom{x}{i}(-1)^{i}\epsilon^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{y}\binom{y}{j}\epsilon^{j}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=0}^{x}\sum_{j=0}^{y}\binom{x}{i}\binom{y}{j}\epsilon^{i+j}\left(\frac{(-1)^{i}+(-1)^{j}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=1\pm\epsilon^{2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{x}\binom{x}{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{y}\binom{y}{j}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=1\pm\epsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{x+y}\,,

where the third (in)equality holds because when
i=j=0
the entire inner expression is equal to

1
, when
i+j=1
it is zero since
i
and
j
have different parities, and otherwise we have

\epsilon^{i+j}\leq\epsilon^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
Let
\bm{t}=\bm{t}_{i}
denote the random variable corresponding to the subtrace produced by
a domino. Then for any binary string
t
, we have the following two cases:

1.

If
t
contains at least one “0” symbol and two “1” symbols, then

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=1\right]=\left(\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=0\right]\right)\left(1\pm\epsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{|t|-1}\right)\,.

2.

Otherwise,

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=1\right]=\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=0\right]\,.

Note that the probabilities are taken over the choice of distribution

\bm{\pi}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\bm{Z}}
and random vectors
\bm{A},\bm{B}
corresponding to the
(Poissonized) trace from
\bm{\pi}
.

Proof.

Note that if
t
is not in the regular language
1^{*}0^{*}1^{*}0^{*}
, all probabilities above are
zero (since such trace cannot be produced by a domino) and the claim holds.
Suppose
t
has form
1^{*}0^{*}1^{*}0^{*}
.

Without loss of generality, say
\bm{t}
is the trace produced by the first domino, so that

\bm{t}=1^{\bm{A}_{1}}0^{\bm{B}_{1}}1^{\bm{A}_{2}}0^{\bm{B}_{2}}
for

\bm{A}_{1}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(m\bm{p}_{1}),\bm{A}_{2}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(m\bm{p}_{2}),\bm{B}_{1}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(m\bm{q}_{1}),\bm{B}_{2}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(m\bm{q}_{2})
, where
\bm{p},\bm{q}
are
the partial distributions of
\bm{\pi}
and therefore
\bm{q}_{1}=\bm{q}_{2}=1/2n
.
Note that
|\bm{t}|=\bm{A}_{1}+\bm{B}_{1}+\bm{A}_{2}+\bm{B}_{2}
.

By standard arguments, once we condition on
|\bm{t}|=|t|
, the random variables

\bm{A}_{i}
and
\bm{B}_{i}
are distributed according to a multinomial distribution given by

|t|
trials and
4
bins with probabilities corresponding to the relative weights of the
probability masses on each position:

\displaystyle\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{A}_{1}=a_{1},\bm{B}_{1}=b_{1},\bm{A}_{2}=a_{2},\bm{B}_{2}=b_{2}\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{A}_{1}+\bm{B}_{1}+\bm{A}_{2}+\bm{B}_{2}=|t|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[(\bm{X}_{1},\bm{Y}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\bm{Y}_{2})=(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\right]\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{X}_{1},\bm{Y}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\bm{Y}_{2})\sim\mathsf{Multinomial}\left(|t|,\left(\frac{n}{2}\bm{p}_{1},\frac{1}{4},\frac{n}{2}\bm{p}_{2},\frac{1}{4}\right)\right)\,.

For convenience,
let
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}\vcentcolon=\frac{n}{2}\bm{p}_{1}
and
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}\vcentcolon=\frac{n}{2}\bm{p}_{2}
.
Note that, in the YES case (
\bm{Z}=0
), we have
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}
,
and in the NO case (
\bm{Z}=1
), we have one of the following with equal probability:

1.

\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)
and
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)
;

2.

\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)
and
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)
.

We now prove the claim. We start with the second case, which is simpler.
First, suppose
t
contains no “0” symbols. Then since the total weight of the 1-valued
positions is
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}+\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=1/2
regardless of the value of
\bm{Z}
, we have

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=1\right]=\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=0\right]=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|t|}\,.

On the other hand, suppose
t
contains at most one “1” symbol. If it contains no “1” symbols,
the same logic applies, so we can assume that
t
contains exactly one “1” symbol.
We may write the probability of
t
as the sum of the probabilities of all

(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})
that produce
t
as a binary string, i. e.
t=1^{a_{1}}0^{b_{1}}1^{a_{2}}0^{b_{2}}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of such tuples that produce
t
.
Using the multinomial formulation, this yields, for any possible values

p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2}
of
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1},\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}
,

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime}_{1},\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime}_{2}\right]=\sum_{(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{|t|!}{a_{1}!b_{1}!a_{2}!b_{2}!}\left(p^{\prime}_{1}\right)^{a_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{1}}\left(p^{\prime}_{2}\right)^{a_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{2}}\,.

Since
t
contains exactly one “1” symbol—say
t=0^{x}10^{y}
for some
x,y\geq 0
—,
we have that
1^{a_{1}}0^{b_{1}}1^{a_{2}}0^{b_{2}}=t
if and only if

1.

a_{1}=0
,
a_{2}=1
,
b_{1}=x
, and
b_{2}=y
; or, mutually exclusively,

2.

a_{1}=1
,
a_{2}=0
,
b_{1}+b_{2}=y
, and
x=0
.

Thus we may write the probability of
t=0^{x}10^{y}
as

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime}_{1},\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime}_{2}\right]=\frac{(x+y+1)!}{x!y!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{x+y}p^{\prime}_{2}+\mathds{1}\left[x=0\right]\frac{(y+1)!}{y!}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{y}p^{\prime}_{1}\,.

We verify that, when
\bm{Z}=1
, the cases where
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}
are positively
and negatively biased cancel out and we obtain the same probability as when
\bm{Z}=0
:

\displaystyle\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=1\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon),\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon),\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\frac{(x+y+1)!}{x!y!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{x+y}\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)+\mathds{1}\left[x=0\right]\frac{(y+1)!}{y!}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{y}\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\frac{(x+y+1)!}{x!y!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{x+y}\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)+\mathds{1}\left[x=0\right]\frac{(y+1)!}{y!}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{y}\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{(x+y+1)!}{x!y!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{x+y}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)+\mathds{1}\left[x=0\right]\frac{(y+1)!}{y!}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{y}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=0\right]\,,

completing the proof of the second case of the claim.

Let us return to the first case. Suppose
t
contains at least one “0” symbol and two “1”
symbols; say
t=1^{x}0^{z}1^{y}0^{w}
with
x+y\geq 2
and
z+w\geq 1
.
We start with the general multinomial formulation again: let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of tuples

(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})
satisfying
t=1^{a_{1}}0^{b_{1}}1^{a_{2}}0^{b_{2}}
. We have

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime}_{1},\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime}_{2}\right]=\sum_{(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{|t|!}{a_{1}!b_{1}!a_{2}!b_{2}!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{2}}\cdot\left(p^{\prime}_{1}\right)^{a_{1}}\left(p^{\prime}_{2}\right)^{a_{2}}\,.

Define
F_{a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}}\vcentcolon=\frac{|t|!}{a_{1}!b_{1}!a_{2}!b_{2}!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{a_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{a_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{2}}
,
so that

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=0\right]=\sum_{(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}F_{a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}}

and

\displaystyle\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{|t|!}{a_{1}!b_{1}!a_{2}!b_{2}!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)\right)^{a_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)\right)^{a_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{|t|!}{a_{1}!b_{1}!a_{2}!b_{2}!}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{b_{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}(1-\epsilon)\right)^{a_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4}(1+\epsilon)\right)^{a_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}F_{a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+\epsilon)^{a_{1}}(1-\epsilon)^{a_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)^{a_{1}}(1+\epsilon)^{a_{2}}\right)\,.

Thus it suffices to show that for every
(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathcal{S}
,

\frac{1}{2}(1+\epsilon)^{a_{1}}(1-\epsilon)^{a_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)^{a_{1}}(1+\epsilon)^{a_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{?}}}}{{=}}1\pm\epsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{|t|-1}\,,

and since
a_{1}+a_{2}\leq|t|-1
(because
t
contains at least one “0” symbol), this follows
from
Proposition

5.13
, completing the proof.
∎

5.5
Information Bound

Recall that we wish to upper bound the mutual information
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})
, which we will do,
using the chain rule of mutual information, by summing over the quantities

I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T}_{i_{j},r_{j}})
where each
\{i_{j},i_{j}+1,\dotsc,i_{j}+r_{j}-1\}
is a contiguous range
of dominoes (forming a partition) and
\bm{T}_{i_{j},r_{j}}
is the subtrace produced by such a range.
For simplicity, let
\bm{T}=\bm{T}_{i_{j},r_{j}}
denote one such variable.
Let
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
be the conditional distributions of
\bm{T}
under each value of
\bm{Z}
:
for each binary string
T
and
z\in\{0,1\}
,
P_{z}(T)\vcentcolon=\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{T}=T\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{Z}=z\right]
.

The following fact states that, if the pointwise ratios between
P_{1}
and
P_{0}
are close to
1
,
then the mutual information
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T})
is small.
Since the full argument will require a refined version
that also handles low-probability outcomes (for which the ratio bound may fail), we state this
fact without proof for intuition only. The formulation is inspired by
[
DK16
]
.

Fact 5.15
.

Let
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
be discrete probability distributions over some domain
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
\xi>0
and suppose that, for every
T\in\mathcal{X}
, it holds that

\frac{P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)}=1\pm\xi\,.

Then we have

\chi^{2}(P_{1}\|P_{0})\leq\xi^{2}\,,

where

\chi^{2}(P_{1}\|P_{0})=\underset{\bm{T}\sim P_{0}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\frac{P_{1}(\bm{T})}{P_{0}(\bm{T})}-1\right)^{2}\right]

is the Pearson
\chi^{2}
-divergence.
Moreover, if
\bm{Z}
is a uniform random bit and
\bm{T}
is distributed according to

P_{\bm{Z}}
, then

I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T})\leq\frac{1}{2}\chi^{2}(P_{1}\|P_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}\,.

Therefore, our task is to upper bound
\left\lvert\frac{P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)}-1\right\rvert
.
The following result accomplishes this for any range of
r
dominoes and string
T
that is not
too long compared to
r
. Later, we will see that strings
T
that are too long are so unlikely
that they have little effect on the mutual information.

Lemma 5.16
.

There exists a universal constant
c>0
such that the following holds.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and let
r\geq 1
be an integer.
