Let
\bm{T}
denote the subtrace produced by a range of
r
consecutive dominoes, and let

P_{z}
be the probability distribution of
\bm{T}
conditional on
\bm{Z}=z
as above.
Then for any binary string
T
satisfying
|T|^{4}\leq r/100
, we have

\frac{P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)}=1\pm c\cdot\epsilon^{2}\cdot\frac{|T|^{6}}{r^{2}}\,.

Proof.

Let us denote by
g_{z}(t)
the probability, as in

Lemma

5.14
, that any given domino produces subtrace
t
conditional on

\bm{Z}=z
and the length of the subtrace:

g_{z}(t)\vcentcolon=\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{t}=t\;\;\mid\;\;|\bm{t}|=|t|,\bm{Z}=z\right]
where
\bm{t}
is the random variable
corresponding the subtrace from the domino under consideration. Recall that, by definition of
the dominoes, the probabilities
g_{z}(t)
are the same for every domino.

For each
i\in[r]
, let
\bm{D}_{i}
be the random variable corresponding to the length of the
subtrace produced by the
i
-th domino in the range. As noted in

5.6
, the additive property of Poisson random variables
and the construction of dominoes implies that
\bm{D}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(2m/n)
for all
i

independently. For convenience, let
\lambda\vcentcolon=2m/n
.

For
T
to be the trace produced by the range under consideration, each domino in this range
must produce a subtrace
in such a way that 1) the total length of all subtraces is
|T|
; and 2) the subtrace from each
domino is equal to the appropriate substring of
T
. Toward this end, let
\mathcal{M}
denote the set
of vectors of subtrace lengths that add up to
|T|
:

\mathcal{M}\vcentcolon=\left\{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}:\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}=|T|\right\}\,.

Recalling
Definition

5.10
, we may write
\mathcal{M}
as the disjoint union

\mathcal{M}=\biguplus_{h,k,s\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)\,.

We will use the following notation to refer to substrings of
T
. For indices

1\leq a,b\leq|T|
, let
T[a..b]
denote the substring of
T
between indices
a
and
b

(inclusive) when
a\leq b
, and set
T[a..b]\vcentcolon=\varnothing
when
a>b
.
For a histogram
d\in\mathcal{M}
and for each
i\in[r]
, set

T(d,i)\vcentcolon=T\left[\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}d_{j}\right)\,..\,\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}d_{j}\right)+d_{i}-1\right]\,,

Then for all
d\in\mathcal{M}
,
T
is equal to the concatenation
T(d,1)\circ\dotsm\circ T(d,r)
.

We now have, for each
z\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle P_{z}(T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{D}_{i}=d_{i}\right]g_{z}(T(d,i))=\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{d_{i}}}{d_{i}!}g_{z}(T(d,i))

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-r\lambda}(r\lambda)^{|T|}}{|T|!}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(\frac{|T|!}{d_{1}!\dotsm d_{r}!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{|T|}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{z}(T(d,i))\right)\right]\,.

Notice that the first factor inside the summation is a multinomial probability: letting

\bm{d}=(\bm{d}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{d}_{r})\sim\mathsf{Multinomial}(|T|,r,(1/r,\dotsc,1/r))
,
the first factor is precisely
\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]
. This is the “balls and bins” process
introduced in
Definition

5.10
.
14
14

14

We have essentially “factored out” the
Poissonization for this part of the analysis.

As for the second factor, for each
d
and
i
define

\delta(d,i)\vcentcolon=\frac{g_{1}(T(d,i))}{g_{0}(T(d,i))}-1\,,

so that
g_{1}(T(d,i))=(1+\delta(d,i))g_{0}(T(d,i))
.
By slightly loosening
Lemma

5.14
for simplicity, we may bound each

\delta(d,i)
as follows:

\displaystyle d_{i}\leq 2

\displaystyle\implies\delta(d,i)=0\,,\text{ and}

\displaystyle d_{i}\geq 3

\displaystyle\implies\left\lvert\delta(d,i)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{d_{i}-1}\,.

We then obtain

\displaystyle P_{z}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-r\lambda}(r\lambda)^{|T|}}{|T|!}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))(1+z\delta(d,i))

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-r\lambda}(r\lambda)^{|T|}}{|T|!}\sum_{h,k,s\geq 0}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(1\pm z\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right]\epsilon^{2}2^{d_{i}-1}\right)\right)\,.

For any
d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)
, the term

\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(1\pm z\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right]\epsilon^{2}2^{d_{i}-1}\right)
is a product in
which all but
k
terms are simply
1
, since only
k
entries
d_{i}
may satisfy

d_{i}\geq 3
by definition of
(h,k,s)
-collision.
Therefore, upon expanding this product, we obtain
2^{k}
terms; one of them is
1
, and

2^{k}-1
of them each contain at least one
\epsilon^{2}
factor and a
2^{x}
factor for some

x\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right](d_{i}-1)
.
Thus, using the identity
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right](d_{i}-1)=s
from the definition of

(h,k,s)
-collision, we obtain

\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(1\pm z\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}\geq 3\right]\epsilon^{2}2^{d_{i}-1}\right)=1\pm z\epsilon^{2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}\,.

Therefore we can write

\displaystyle P_{z}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-r\lambda}(r\lambda)^{|T|}}{|T|!}\sum_{h,k,s\geq 0}\left(1\pm z\epsilon^{2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}\right)\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\right)\,.

Recall that we want to show that

P_{1}(T)/P_{0}(T)=1\pm\epsilon^{2}\cdot O\left(|T|^{6}/r^{2}\right)
. Substituting the
formulation above, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{h,k,s\geq 0}\left(1\pm\epsilon^{2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}\right)\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\right)}{\sum_{h,k,s\geq 0}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\right)}

\displaystyle=1\pm\sum_{h,k,s\geq 0}\left[\epsilon^{2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}\cdot\frac{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\right)}{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\right)}\right]\,.

Therefore, our goal is to show the following:

\sum_{h\geq 0,s\geq 2k\geq 2}\epsilon^{2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}\cdot\frac{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))}{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{?}}}}{{\leq}}c\cdot\epsilon^{2}\cdot\frac{|T|^{6}}{r^{2}}\,,

(18)

where we used the fact that
\epsilon^{2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}=0
when
k=0
to limit the range
of
k
in the summation to
k\geq 1
, and then used the fact that
s\geq 2k
for any
nonempty
\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)
to limit the range of
s
.

First, note that for any single-character binary string
t
(i. e. strings “0” and “1”),
we have
g_{0}(t)=1/2
. We may hence lower bound the denominator of (
18
)
by counting only those
d\in\mathcal{M}
that have no collisions at all
(i. e.
d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(0,0,0)
), whose total probability is lower bounded by

Proposition

5.12
:

\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\geq\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(0,0,0)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|}\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|}\left(1-\frac{|T|^{2}}{r}\right)>\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|+1}\,,

where we used the assumption that
|T|^{4}\leq r/100
in the last inequality.

We proceed similarly to upper bound the numerator of (
18
). For any

d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)
, the terms in
\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))
satisfying
d_{i}=1

are again equal to
1/2
, while all other terms are trivially at most
1
.
Moreover, by definition of
(h,k,s)
-collisions we have

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathds{1}\left[d_{i}=1\right]=|T|-(2h+k+s)
.
Hence, for any
h\geq 0
and
s\geq 2k\geq 2
, we have

\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|-(2h+k+s)}\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|}\cdot 2^{2h+\frac{3}{2}s}\,,

and therefore, using
Proposition

5.11
,

\displaystyle\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|}\cdot 2^{2h+\frac{3}{2}s}\cdot\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|}\cdot\left(\frac{(2h|T|)^{2}}{r}\right)^{h}\cdot\left(\frac{(2k|T|)^{3/2}}{r}\right)^{s}\,.

Combining the results above, along with the observation that
h,k,s\leq|T|
for any nonzero
terms in the numerator of (
18
), and using the notation
\lesssim
to
absorb constant factors, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{h\geq 0,s\geq 2k\geq 2}(2^{k}-1)2^{s}\cdot\frac{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{C}_{r,|T|}(h,k,s)}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))}{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{d}=d\right]\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{0}(T(d,i))}

\displaystyle\qquad<\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|T|\geq h\geq 0,\\
|T|\geq s\geq 2k\geq 2\end{subarray}}2^{\frac{3}{2}s}\cdot\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{|T|}\cdot\left(\frac{(2h|T|)^{2}}{r}\right)^{h}\cdot\left(\frac{(2k|T|)^{3/2}}{r}\right)^{s}}{\left(1/2\right)^{|T|+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad=2\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|T|\geq h\geq 0,\\
|T|\geq s\geq 2k\geq 2\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{(2h|T|)^{2}}{r}\right)^{h}\cdot\left(\frac{(4k|T|)^{3/2}}{r}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|T|\geq h\geq 0,\\
|T|\geq s\geq 2k\geq 2\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{4|T|^{4}}{r}\right)^{h}\left(\frac{8|T|^{3}}{r}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\qquad<2\cdot\sum_{h\geq 0}\left(\frac{4|T|^{4}}{r}\right)^{h}\left[\sum_{s\geq 2}\sum_{1\leq k\leq s/2}\cdot\left(\frac{8|T|^{3}}{r}\right)^{s}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\cdot\sum_{h\geq 0}\left(\frac{4|T|^{4}}{r}\right)^{h}\left[\sum_{s\geq 2}\frac{s}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{8|T|^{3}}{r}\right)^{s}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\left(\frac{|T|^{3}}{r}\right)^{2}\sum_{h\geq 0}\left(\frac{4|T|^{4}}{r}\right)^{h}

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\frac{|T|^{6}}{r^{2}}\,,

where we used the assumption that
|T|^{4}\leq r/100
to establish the convergence of the two
geometric series,
15
15

15

Namely, we used the formulas

\sum_{i\geq 0}x^{i}=\frac{1}{1-x}
and
\sum_{i\geq 2}ix^{i}=\frac{(2-x)x^{2}}{(1-x)^{2}}

for
|x|<1
.
thus concluding the proof.
∎

We now use this result to upper bound the mutual information between
\bm{Z}
and the subtrace
produced by a range of
\Theta(n/m)
consecutive dominoes.

Lemma 5.17
.

Suppose
n^{-1/4}<\epsilon<1
, and let

\frac{n}{m}\leq r\leq 2\frac{n}{m}
be a positive integer.
Suppose
\bm{Z}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(1/2)
and
let
\bm{T}
denote the subtrace generated by a range of
r
consecutive dominoes,
according to distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\bm{Z}}
.
Suppose
m=o\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\right)
.
Then as
n\to\infty
we have

I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T})=O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{4}\log^{12}n}{r^{4}}\right)\,.

Proof.

As before, let
P_{z}
denote the conditional probabilities of
\bm{T}
given
\bm{Z}=z
.
Let
Q\vcentcolon=(P_{0}+P_{1})/2
denote the (marginal) distribution of
\bm{T}
.

Our strategy will be to decompose the set of possible subtraces
T
according to whether

|T|\lesssim\log n
(the typical case) or
|T|\gtrsim\log n
. In the former case,

Lemma

5.16
will give that the ratio
P_{1}(T)/P_{0}(T)
is close to
1
, while
in the latter case, we will use Poisson concentration bounds to argue that such long traces
cannot contribute too much to the mutual information.

Concretely, we start by upper bounding
I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T})
by the sum a
\chi^{2}
-type
expression for
|T|\leq 20\log n
, and tail probabilities for
|T|>20\log n
:

\displaystyle I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T})

\displaystyle=H(\bm{T})-H(\bm{T}|\bm{Z})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{T}Q(T)\log(Q(T))+\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{P}\left[Z=z\right]\sum_{T}P_{z}(T)\log(P_{z}(T))

\displaystyle=-\sum_{T}\frac{P_{0}(T)+P_{1}(T)}{2}\log(Q(T))+\sum_{T}\left[\frac{P_{0}(T)}{2}\log(P_{0}(T))+\frac{P_{1}(T)}{2}\log(P_{1}(T))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T}\left[P_{0}(T)\log\left(\frac{P_{0}(T)}{Q(T)}\right)+P_{1}(T)\log\left(\frac{P_{1}(T)}{Q(T)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T}\left[P_{0}(T)\left(\frac{P_{0}(T)}{Q(T)}-1\right)+P_{1}(T)\left(\frac{P_{1}(T)}{Q(T)}-1\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T}\left[P_{0}(T)\left(\frac{P_{0}(T)-P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)+P_{1}(T)}\right)+P_{1}(T)\left(\frac{P_{1}(T)-P_{0}(T)}{P_{0}(T)+P_{1}(T)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T}\left[\frac{\left(P_{1}(T)-P_{0}(T)\right)^{2}}{P_{0}(T)+P_{1}(T)}\right]

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T:|T|\leq 20\log n}\frac{\left(P_{1}(T)-P_{0}(T)\right)^{2}}{P_{0}(T)}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T:|T|>20\log n}\left(P_{0}(T)+P_{1}(T)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T:|T|\leq 20\log n}P_{0}(T)\left(\frac{P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)}-1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\bm{T}|>20\log n\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{Z}=0\right]+\frac{1}{2}\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\bm{T}|>20\log n\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{Z}=1\right]\,.

We start with the first term in the last expression above. We want to show that, when

|T|\leq 20\log n
, we have
|T|^{4}=o(r)
, which is sufficient for satisfying the condition of

Lemma

5.16
. Recalling the assumptions
\frac{n}{m}\leq r\leq 2\frac{n}{m}
,

m=o\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\right)
and

\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|T|^{4}/20^{4}}{r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\log^{4}n}{r}\leq\frac{m\log^{4}n}{n}\ll\frac{\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\log^{4}n}{n}\leq 1\,,

and hence the condition
|T|^{4}\leq r/100
holds for sufficiently large
n
.
Therefore
Lemma

5.16
yields

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T:|T|\leq 20\log n}P_{0}(T)\left(\frac{P_{1}(T)}{P_{0}(T)}-1\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{T:|T|\leq 20\log n}P_{0}(T)\left(c\cdot\epsilon^{2}\cdot\frac{|T|^{6}}{r^{2}}\right)^{2}=O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{4}\log^{12}n}{r^{4}}\right)\,.

We now deal with the second component. Recall (see
5.6
)
that
|\bm{T}|
is distributed according to a
Poisson distribution completely determined by the number of dominoes in the range:

|\bm{T}|\sim\mathsf{Poi}\left(r\cdot\frac{2m}{n}\right)\,,

independently of
\bm{Z}
. Let
\lambda\vcentcolon=r\cdot\frac{2m}{n}
and note that

2\leq\lambda\leq 4\leq\log n
.
3.4
gives, for
z\in\{0,1\}
,

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\bm{T}|>20\log n\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{Z}=z\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[|\bm{T}|-\lambda>19\log n\right]\leq e^{-\frac{(19\log n)^{2}}{2(\lambda+19\log n)}}\leq e^{-\frac{361\log n}{40}}\leq\frac{1}{n^{9}}\,.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{9}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{r^{4}}\impliedby\frac{1}{n^{9}}\leq\frac{1/n}{16n^{4}/m^{4}}\iff\frac{16}{n^{4}}\leq m^{4}\iff\frac{2}{n}\leq m\,,

which holds trivially, completing the proof.
∎

Since subtraces produced by disjoint ranges are conditionally independent given
\bm{Z}
, applying
the chain rule along with the data processing inequality concludes the proof.

Lemma 5.18

(Refinement of
5.9
)
.

Suppose
n^{-1/4}<\epsilon<1
.
Let
\bm{Z}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(1/2)
,
\bm{\pi}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\bm{\pi}}
, and let
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
be a trace of
size
\mathsf{Poi}(m)
drawn from
\pi
. Then if

m=o\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\right)
, we have

I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})=O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{4}m^{5}}{n^{4}}\log^{12}n\right)=o(1)\,,

and hence any algorithm that succeeds in distinguishing the YES and NO cases with probability
at least
51\%
(over
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
) requires sample complexity at least

\Omega\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\cdot\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\right)\,.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary partition of the domain
[2n]
into consecutive ranges
R_{1},\dotsc,R_{m^{\prime}}

such that 1) each
R_{i}
is a contiguous range with multiple of
4
length, and hence
consists of
|R_{i}|/4
consecutive dominoes (recall that we assume even
n
for simplicity);
and 2) each
R_{i}
satisfies
\frac{n}{m}\leq|R_{i}|/4\leq 2\frac{n}{m}
.
It follows that
m^{\prime}=\Theta(m)
and, letting
r_{i}\vcentcolon=|R_{i}|/4
for each
i\in[m^{\prime}]
, each

r_{i}
satisfies the requirements of
Lemma

5.17
.

Let
\bm{T}_{i}
be the subtrace generated by range
R_{i}
, so that the final trace is obtained by
concatenation of all subtraces:

\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\bm{T}_{1}\circ\bm{T}_{2}\dotsm\circ\bm{T}_{m^{\prime}}\,.

The data processing inequality yields

I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})\leq I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{T}_{m^{\prime}})\,.

Note that, conditional on
\bm{Z}
, the entries of
\bm{\pi}
in different dominoes are
mutually independent as per the process described in
Definition

5.7
.
Then, recalling the distribution of subtraces described in
Definition

5.5
, it follows that
the
\bm{T}_{i}
are mutually independent conditional on
\bm{Z}
.
Thus the chain rule of mutual information and
Lemma

5.17
give

I(\bm{Z}:\bm{\mathcal{T}})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}I(\bm{Z}:\bm{T}_{i})=\Theta(m)\cdot O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{4}m^{4}\log^{12}n}{n^{4}}\right)=O\left(m^{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{n^{4}}\log^{12}n\right)=o(1)\,,

as desired. Finally, applying
5.3
establishes the second conclusion.
∎

Putting together
Proposition

5.2
and
Lemma

5.18

establishes
Theorem

5.1
:

Corollary 5.19

(Refinement of
Theorem

5.1
)
.

Let
\Pi_{1}
contain only the uniform distribution over
[2n]
, and let
\Pi_{2}
be the set of
distributions over
[2n]
that are
\epsilon
-far from uniform in total variation distance. Then

(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},51/100)
-testing under the parity trace requires sample complexity at least

\Omega\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)

samples.
Furthermore, this bound holds even if the input distribution

\pi
is guaranteed to have
1/2
mass uniformly distributed over the zero-valued (i. e. even)
coordinates.

Proof.

The lower bound of
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
holds by
Proposition

5.2
.
Moreover, we have

\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\geq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\iff\epsilon\geq\frac{\log^{10/3}n}{n^{1/4}}\,,

in which case
Lemma

5.18
establishes the bound.
∎

6
Distribution-Free Sample-Based Property Testing

In this section, we relate distribution testing under the parity trace to distribution-free
sample-based property testing. The main ideas of this section are:

1.

We define
labeled-distribution testing
as a generalized reformulation of
distribution-free sample-based property testing that makes the connection to distribution testing
more explicit.

2.

There is a natural type of labeled distribution properties, which we call
density
properties
, that includes some property testing problems, and some more challenging versions of
standard distribution testing problems. We use the edit distance and Ramsey theory to show that
testing these properties is equivalent to testing distributions under the parity trace.

3.

Using this equivalence, we get new tight positive results for distribution-free sample-based
testing (in the more general labeled-distribution definition) by applying
Theorem

1.1
.

4.

There is a
testing-by-learning
reduction for labeled-distribution testing, similar to
the standard testing-by-learning reduction of
[
GGR98
]
, that allows non-constructive upper
bounds on distribution testing under the parity trace. This will be used in

Section

7
to get upper bounds for some testing problems in the trace
reconstruction model.

The section is organized as follows:

Section

6.1
:

The definition of labeled distributions.

Section

6.2
:

The definition of edit distance, which is closely related to
labeled distributions and will be necessary for all of our applications in the remainder of the
paper.

Section

6.3
:

The definition of labeled distribution testing, and how
it generalizes the conventional distribution testing and distribution-free sample-based property
testing models.

Section

6.4
:

A testing-by-learning reduction for labeled-distribution
testing.

Section

6.5
:

The definition of
density properties
, and the
equivalence of testing density properties to distribution testing under the parity trace.

Section

6.6
:

The proof of
our main result on labeled distribution testing,
Theorem

1.5
, which is an
application of
Theorem

1.1
.

Section

6.7
:

An upper bound on testing uniform distributions
against unrestricted distributions under the parity trace
(
Theorem

6.34
).

Section

6.8
:

The equivalence between testing support size
k
under
the parity trace, and testing
k
-alternating functions in the distribution-free sample-based model
(
Theorem

6.38
), and an alternate proof of the lower bounds of
[
BFH21
]
for
testing halfspaces, among others.

6.1
Labeled Distributions

We shall now define labeled distributions and edit distance, which are closely related.

Definition 6.1

(Labeled Distribution)
.

On any fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
, a
labeled distribution
is a pair
(f,\mathcal{D})
of a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
and a probability distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{X}
. We write
\mathcal{D}_{f}
for the probability
distribution over
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
, where the density of any
(x,b)\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
is defined
as

\mathcal{D}_{f}(x,b)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}(x)&\text{ if }b=f(x)\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

In other words, a sample from
\mathcal{D}_{f}
is obtained by sampling
x\sim\mathcal{D}
and taking
(x,f(x))
.

We study the case
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{Z}
. For a labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
over

\mathbb{Z}
, it may be the case that
f
“alternates” an infinite number of times. We restrict our
attention to the “proper” labeled distributions, where
f
has a finite number of alternations
“on the left”​​, defined as follows.

Definition 6.2

(Proper Labeled Distributions)
.

A labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
is
1-proper
if there exists
t\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
f(x)=1

for all
x<t
. It is
0-proper
if, instead,
f(x)=0
for all
x<t
. If
(f,\mathcal{D})

is either 0- or 1-proper, we call it
proper
.

Remark 6.3
.

When studying labeled distribution testing, it suffices to consider proper labeled distributions.
This is because, for every labeled distribution
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and every
\delta>0
, there exists a

proper
labeled distribution
\mathcal{D}_{g}
such that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})<\delta
. So every
distribution is indistinguishable (to any algorithm with bounded sample size) from some proper
distribution.

Definition 6.4

(Alternation Sequence)
.

For any proper labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
, the
alternation sequence
is the unique
sequence
a_{1}<a_{2}<a_{3}<\dotsm
such that
f
is constant on the intervals
(-\infty,a_{1}]
,

(a_{1},a_{2}]
,
\dotsc
, and
f(a_{i-1})\neq f(a_{i})
. Note that if
(f,\mathcal{D})
is 1-proper, then
f

takes value
\mathsf{par}(i)
on the interval
(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
, and value 1 on
(-\infty,a_{1}]
. If

(f,\mathcal{D})
is 0-proper, it takes the opposite values. Note that this sequence always exists when

(f,\mathcal{D})
is proper, and it may be an infinite sequence.

Definition 6.5

(Density Sequence)
.

For any proper labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
with alternation sequence
a_{1}<a_{2}<a_{3}<\dotsm
, we define the
density sequence

\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as follows. If

(f,\mathcal{D})
is 1-proper, we define

\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(i)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}(-\infty,a_{1}]&\text{ if }i=1\\
\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]&\text{ if }i>1\,.\end{cases}

If
(f,\mathcal{D})
is 0-proper, we define

\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(i)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }i=1\\
\mathcal{D}(-\infty,a_{1}]&\text{ if }i=2\\
\mathcal{D}(a_{i-2},a_{i-1}]&\text{ if }i>2\,.\end{cases}

Note that
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
is a probability distribution, since
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(i)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}(x)=1
. For any set
\Xi
of proper labeled distributions, we write

\Pi(\Xi)\vcentcolon=\{\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}:(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi\}

for the set of density sequences (probability distributions) associated with the proper labeled
distributions in
\Xi
.

The following simple formula for TV distance for labeled distributions is often useful.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
(f,\mathcal{D})
and
(g,\mathcal{E})
be labeled distributions over
\mathbb{Z}
. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}\left[f(i)\neq g(i)\right](\mathcal{D}(i)+\mathcal{E}(i))+\mathds{1}\left[f(i)=g(i)\right]|\mathcal{D}(i)-\mathcal{E}(i)|\,.

Proof.

By definition,

\displaystyle\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(i,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0,1\}}|\mathcal{D}_{f}(i,b)-\mathcal{E}_{g}(i,b)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0,1\}\\
f(i)=g(i)=b\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{D}(i)-\mathcal{E}(i)|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0,1\}\\
f(i)=b,g(i)\neq b\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}(i)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0,1\}\\
f(i)\neq b,g(i)=b\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}(i)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}\left[f(i)\neq g(i)\right](\mathcal{D}(i)+\mathcal{E}(i))+\mathds{1}\left[f(i)=g(i)\right]|\mathcal{D}(i)-\mathcal{E}(i)|\,.\qed

6.2
Edit Distance

We define two notions of edit distance: one for labeled distributions on domain
\mathbb{Z}
, and one for
distributions over
\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 6.7

(Edit Distance for Labeled Distributions)
.

For any two proper labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})
and
(g,\mathcal{E})
on domain
\mathbb{Z}
, define

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),(g,\mathcal{E}))\vcentcolon=\inf\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}})\,,

where the infimum is taken over all proper labeled distributions
(f^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
and
(g^{\prime},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
that
have the same density sequences as the original distributions, i. e. that satisfy
\pi_{f^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
and
\pi_{g^{\prime},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}=\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}
.

Next, we will define the edit distance for distributions over
\mathbb{N}
. Recall that the TV distance is
not the natural distance metric for distribution testing under the parity trace, because
distributions may have maximum TV distance 1 while being indistinguishable under the parity trace.
Edit distance replaces the TV distance as the natural (pseudo-)metric for the parity trace.
We begin by introducing the notion of a
fractional string
.

Definition 6.8

(Fractional String)
.

A
fractional string
is a finite sequence
\sigma_{1}^{p_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{p_{2}}\dots\sigma_{n}^{p_{n}}

where each
fractional character

\sigma_{i}^{p_{i}}
consists of a symbol
\sigma_{i}\in\{0,1\}
and
a value
p_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

We now define the edit distance for fractional strings, which is an analog of the standard edit
distance for strings.

Definition 6.9

(Edit Distance for Fractional Strings)
.

Let
a=a_{1}^{p_{1}}a_{2}^{p_{2}}\dotsm a_{n}^{p_{n}}
be a fractional string. We define the following
permitted edit operations on
a
, with associated cost:

Insert:

For
i\in[n+1]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{ins}_{i,b}(a)
is the fractional string
obtained by inserting the fractional character
b^{0}
immediately before
a_{i}^{p_{i}}
. The cost of
this operation is 0.

Delete:

For
i\in[n]
such that
p_{i}=0
,
\mathsf{del}_{i}(a)
is the fractional string
obtained by deleting the fractional character
a_{i}^{p_{i}}=a_{i}^{0}
. The cost of this operation is 0.

Rearrange:

For
i\in[n-1]
such that
a_{i}=a_{i+1}
, and
-p_{i}\leq\delta\leq p_{i+1}
,

\mathsf{rearr}_{i,\delta}(a)
is the fractional string obtained by replacing

a_{i}^{p_{i}}a_{i+1}^{p_{i+1}}
with
a_{i}^{p_{i}+\delta}a_{i+1}^{p_{i+1}-\delta}
. The cost of this
operation is 0.

Adjust:

For
i\in[n]
and
\delta\geq-p_{i}
,
\mathsf{adj}_{i,\delta}(a)
is the
fractional string obtained by replacing
p_{i}
with
p_{i}+\delta
, so that the fractional character

a_{i}^{p_{i}}
becomes
a_{i}^{p_{i}+\delta}
. The cost of this operation is
|\delta|/2
.

For a fractional string
a
, we say that a sequence of operations
O_{1},\dotsc,O_{k}
is permitted if
for each
i\in[k]
,
O_{i}
is a permitted operation on the fractional string
(O_{i-1}\circ O_{i-2}\circ\dotsm\circ O_{1})(a)
.

For two fractional strings
a
and
b
, we define the
edit distance

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{fr-edit}}(a,b)
as the minimum
c
such that there exists a sequence of permitted
operations
O_{1},\dots,O_{k}
such that
O_{k}\circ O_{k-1}\circ\dotsm\circ O_{1}(a)=b
and the
sum of costs of operations
O_{i}
is
c
.

Let
\pi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be any finitely-supported probability distribution. We define the
fractional string
\mathsf{str}(\pi)
as follows. Since
\pi
is finitely-supported, there is some

k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pi(i)=0
for all
i>k
. Then we define

\mathsf{str}(\pi)\vcentcolon=1^{\pi(1)}0^{\pi(2)}1^{\pi(3)}0^{\pi(4)}\dotsm(\mathsf{par}(k))^{\pi(k)}\,.

Finally, we may define the edit distance for distributions.

Definition 6.10

(Edit Distance for Distributions)
.

For two finitely-supported distributions
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
, we define

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\vcentcolon=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{fr-edit}}(\mathsf{str}(\pi),\mathsf{str}(\pi^{\prime}))\,.

The following alternate characterization of edit distance is helpful. We defer the proof to

Section

D.2
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
be finitely-supported distributions over
\mathbb{N}
. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=\inf\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})\,,

where the infimum is taken over labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})
and
(g,\mathcal{E})
such that
\pi=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}
.

From this lemma, we can see that the edit distance for distributions and labeled distributions are
essentially equivalent: for two proper labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})
and
(g,\mathcal{E})
, the lemma
implies

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),(g,\mathcal{E}))=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}})\,.

It is easy to see that the following inequality holds in general:

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\,.

(19)

This can be verified by taking
(f,\mathcal{D})
where
f(i)=\mathsf{par}(i)
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
f(i)=1

for
i\leq 0
, and
\mathcal{D}(i)=\pi(i)
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathcal{D}(i)=0
for
i\leq 0
. Define

(g,\mathcal{E})
similarly for
\pi^{\prime}
. This satisfies
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}=\pi
and
\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}=\pi^{\prime}
, and

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
.

6.3
Labeled Distribution Testing

We now introduce
labeled distribution testing
. For labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})
and

(g,\mathcal{E})
over a fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
, we abuse notation and write

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}((f,\mathcal{D}),(g,\mathcal{E}))\vcentcolon=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})\,,

so that, for a property
\Xi
of labeled distributions, we have

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)\vcentcolon=\{(f,\mathcal{D}):\forall(g,\mathcal{E})\in\Xi,\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})>\epsilon\}\,.

Definition 6.12

(Labeled Distribution Testing)
.

Let
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
be properties of labeled distributions over a fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
.
A
(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester, with sample complexity
m
, is an algorithm

A
that satisfies the following, for every labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
over
\mathcal{X}
:

1.

If
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi_{1}
, then
\underset{S_{f}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}_{f},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(S_{f})\text{ accepts }\right]\geq\alpha
; and

2.

If
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi_{2}
, then
\underset{S_{f}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}_{f},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(S_{f})\text{ rejects }\right]\geq\alpha
.

The canonical form of this problem has
\Xi_{2}\vcentcolon=\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})
for some
\epsilon>0
.

We prove here that one can obtain the standard distribution testing and distribution-free
sample-based property testing models from our more general labeled distribution testing model. To
obtain, from the labeled distribution testing model, the standard distribution testing model, where
the goal is to test a property
\Pi
of distributions over domain
\mathcal{X}
, it suffices to choose the
property
\Xi=\Lambda\times\Pi
, where
\Lambda
contains only the constant 0 function over
domain
\mathcal{X}
.

It is slightly less obvious how to obtain distribution-free sample-based property testing from the
labeled distribution testing model. Distribution-free sample-based property testing is defined as
follows.

Definition 6.13

(Distribution-Free Sample-Based Property Testing)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a property of functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
for some fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
, and let

\epsilon>0
. A
(\Lambda,\epsilon,\alpha)
-distribution-free sample-based property tester, with
sample complexity
m
, is an algorithm
A
that satisfies the following, for every function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
and distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{X}
:

1.

If
f\in\Lambda
, then
\underset{S_{f}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}_{f},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(S_{f})\text{ accepts }\right]\geq\alpha
; and

2.

If
\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]>\epsilon
for all
g\in\Lambda
, then
\underset{S_{f}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}_{f},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(S_{f})\text{ rejects }\right]\geq\alpha
.

This problem cannot be expressed neatly as the problem of distinguishing between properties

\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
of functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
, because the set of functions that should be
rejected depends on the distribution
\mathcal{D}
. But we can express it as distinguishing two properties

\Xi_{1}
,
\Xi_{2}
of labeled distributions, as shown in the next two propositions.

Proposition 6.14
.

Fix a domain
\mathcal{X}
and let
(f,\mathcal{D})
,
(g,\mathcal{E})
be labeled distributions. Then

\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{G})\,.

Proof.

For any event
X\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
, write
\mathcal{D}_{f}(X)\vcentcolon=\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[(x,f(x))\in X\right]
. Write
\epsilon\vcentcolon=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})
, so that for any event
X\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
, we have
\left|\mathcal{D}_{f}(X)-\mathcal{E}_{g}(X)\right|\leq\epsilon
.

Let
\Delta\vcentcolon=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:f(x)\neq g(x)\}
and define the event
E\vcentcolon=\{(x,b):x\in\Delta,b=g(x)\}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{f}(E)=0
by definition, and
\left|\mathcal{D}_{f}(E)-\mathcal{E}_{g}(E)\right|\leq\epsilon
, so
\mathcal{E}_{g}(E)\leq\epsilon
. Then also
\mathcal{E}(\Delta)=\mathcal{E}_{g}(E)\leq\epsilon
.

Define the event
F\vcentcolon=\{(x,b):x\in\Delta,b\in\{0,1\}\}
, which satisfies
\mathcal{D}_{f}(F)=\mathcal{D}(\Delta)
and
\mathcal{E}_{g}(F)=\mathcal{E}(\Delta)
. Then
\left|\mathcal{D}(\Delta)-\mathcal{E}(\Delta)\right|\leq\epsilon
, so
\mathcal{D}(\Delta)\leq\mathcal{E}(\Delta)+\epsilon\leq 2\epsilon
.
We conclude that

\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]=\mathcal{D}(\Delta)\leq 2\epsilon\,.\qed

Proposition 6.15
.

Fix any domain
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\Lambda
be any property of functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
, and let
\Pi
be the
set of all distributions over
\mathcal{X}
. Then, for
\Xi=\Lambda\times\Pi
,

1.

If there is a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Xi),\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity
m
, then there is a

(\Lambda,\epsilon,\alpha)
-distribution-free sample-based tester with sample
complexity
m
.

2.

If there is a
(\Lambda,\epsilon,\alpha)
-distribution-free sample-based tester with sample
complexity
m
, then there is a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
-labeled distribution tester with
sample complexity
m
.

Proof.

For the first conclusion, the input to the distribution-free sample-based property tester is a
function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{X}
. The algorithm will take a labeled
sample
S_{f}
where
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)
, and simulate the labeled distribution tester on
S_{f}
.
Suppose that
f\in\Lambda
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Lambda\times\Pi=\Xi
, so the labeled distribution
tester will accept with probability at least
\alpha
.

Now suppose that
f
is
\epsilon
-far from
\Lambda
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
. Then for all
g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
and all distributions
\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{X}
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})\geq\frac{1}{2}\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]>\epsilon/2
, due to
Proposition

6.14
. Therefore

(f,\mathcal{D})\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Xi)
, so the tester will reject with probability at least

\alpha
.

For the second conclusion, we obtain a labeled distribution tester for
\Xi
by taking, on input

(f,\mathcal{D})
, the labeled sample
S_{f}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}_{f},m)
, and running the distribution-free
sample-based property tester on
S_{f}
.

If
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
then
f\in\Lambda
, so the property tester will accept with probability at
least
\alpha
.

If
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})>\epsilon
for all
(g,\mathcal{E})\in\Xi
, then in particular

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})>\epsilon
for all
g\in\Lambda
. Then there is an event
E\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
such that
\left|\mathcal{D}_{f}(E)-\mathcal{D}_{g}(E)\right|>\epsilon
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
f(x)\neq g(x)
for each
(x,b)\in E
(since we may
remove any set of pairs
(x,b)
where
f(x)=g(x)
without changing this difference). Then we can
partition
E=E_{f}\cup E_{g}
where
E_{f}\vcentcolon=\{(x,b)\in E:b=f(x)\}
and
E_{g}\vcentcolon=\{(x,b)\in E:b=g(x)\}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{f}(E)=\mathcal{D}_{f}(E_{f})
and
\mathcal{D}_{g}(E)=\mathcal{D}_{g}(E_{g})
.

It must be the case that either
\mathcal{D}_{f}(E_{f})>\epsilon
or
\mathcal{D}_{g}(E_{g})>\epsilon
. If
\mathcal{D}_{f}(E_{f})>\epsilon
, then we can choose
\Delta\vcentcolon=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:(x,f(x))\in E_{f}\}
, so

\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]\geq\mathcal{D}(\Delta)=\mathcal{D}_{f}(E_{f})>\epsilon\,.

If
\mathcal{D}_{g}(E_{g})>\epsilon
, a similar conclusion holds. Since this holds for all
g\in\Lambda
, we
see that the property tester should reject with probability at least
\alpha
.
∎

6.4
Testing-by-Learning

[
GGR98
]
observed that a proper learning algorithm for a hypothesis class
\Pi
can be used as a
property tester, by including an extra “verification” step. It is convenient for us to adapt the
same technique to a different type of learning algorithm that works for classes of labeled
distributions, where the “hypothesis class” is not just a set of functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
, but a
joint set of function-distribution pairs.

For fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
and property
\Pi
of distributions over
\mathcal{X}
, write

\textsc{close}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi)
for the set of distributions
\pi
over
\mathcal{X}
satisfying

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\Pi)\leq\epsilon
.

Definition 6.16

(Labeled Distribution Learning)
.

Let
\Xi
be a property of labeled distributions on some fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
. A
labeled
distribution learning algorithm
for
\Xi
, with success probability
\alpha
, error
\epsilon>0
,
and sample complexity
m
, is an algorithm
A
that, on any input
(f,\mathcal{D})
, receives a labeled
sample
S_{f}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}_{f},m)
and outputs a function
g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
, and
succeeds
with
probability at least
\alpha
over
S_{f}
and the randomness of the algorithm. The
success

event is defined as follows:

Success:

If
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
, then
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})<\epsilon
.

We call the algorithm
proper
if the success event also has the following additional
conditions:

Success
∗
:

If
(f,\mathcal{D})\notin\Xi
, then there exists
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
.

If
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
, then there exists
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
and

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})<\epsilon
.

Definition 6.17

(Learner-Verifier Pair)
.

Let
\Xi
be a property of labeled distributions on some fixed domain
\mathcal{X}
,
let
\epsilon,\delta>0
and let
A
be a
proper
labeled distribution learning
algorithm for
\Xi
with success probability
1-\delta/3
, error
\epsilon/4
, and sample
complexity
m_{A}
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{A}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{X}}
be the range of
A
. For every

g\in\mathcal{G}_{A}
, let
\Pi_{g}
be the property

\Pi_{g}\vcentcolon=\{\mathcal{E}:(g,\mathcal{E})\in\Xi\}\,.

Suppose
B=\{B_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{A}}
is a family of algorithms such that, for every
g\in\mathcal{G}_{A}
,
algorithm
B_{g}
is a

(\textsc{close}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi_{g}),\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi_{g}),1-\delta/3)
-distribution tester
with sample complexity
m_{B}
. We call
(A,B)
a
learner-verifier pair
for
\Xi

with success probability
1-\delta
, error
\epsilon
, and sample complexity
m_{A}+m_{B}
.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
\Xi
be a property of labeled distributions such that there exists a learner-verifier pair

(A,B)
with success probability
1-\delta
, error
\epsilon>0
, and sample complexity
m=m_{A}+m_{B}
. Then there is a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
-labeled distribution tester with sample
complexity
m+O(1/\epsilon)
.

Proof.

On input
(f,\mathcal{D})
, the tester performs the following.

1.

Use
m_{A}
samples to run the proper learner
A
, and obtain an output function
g
.

2.

Use
O(1/\epsilon)
samples from
\mathcal{D}
to compute an estimate
z
of
\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]
, and reject if this is greater than
\tfrac{3}{8}\epsilon
.

3.

Use
m_{B}
samples to run the distribution tester
B_{g}
.

Suppose that
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
. Suppose that the algorithms
A
succeeds, which occurs with
probability at least
1-\delta/3
. Then there exists
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
and

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})\leq\epsilon/4\,,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})\leq\epsilon/4\,,

by the conditions on the algorithm
A
, which is a proper learner. By the multiplicative Chernoff
bound, we have
z<\tfrac{3}{8}\epsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta/3
, after using

O(1/\epsilon)
samples, so the second step passes. Finally, assume the algorithm
B_{g}
succeeds,
which occurs with probability
1-\delta/3
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
, and
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})\leq\epsilon/4
, we have

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\Pi_{g})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})\leq\epsilon/4\,.

So
\mathcal{D}\in\textsc{close}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi_{g})
, and the algorithm
B_{g}
will accept. The probability of any of
these steps failing is at most
\delta
, by the union bound.

Now suppose that
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
. Suppose for contradiction that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\Pi_{g})<\epsilon/2\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})<\epsilon/2\,.

Then there exists
\mathcal{E}\in\Pi_{g}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
and
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})<\epsilon/2
.
Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})+\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\mathcal{E}_{g})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})+\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})<\epsilon\,,

which is a contradiction. So it must be that either
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\Pi_{g})\geq\epsilon/2
, in
which case the third step rejects with probability at least
1-\delta/3
, or that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})\geq\epsilon/2
, in which case the second step rejects with probability at least

1-\delta/3
, again using the multiplicative Chernoff bound.
∎

Remark 6.19
.

This formalization captures the standard testing-by-learning reduction, when
\Xi
is a property of
labeled distributions obtained by choosing a property
\Lambda_{1}
of functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
setting
\Lambda_{2}
to be the set of all distributions over
\mathcal{X}
, and letting
\Xi=\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}
. In this case, the learner
A
is the standard PAC learning algorithm (see the
proof of
Lemma

6.35
), and the
verifier
B
simply accepts everything.

6.5
Density Properties and Distribution Testing Under the Parity Trace

Labeled-distribution testing is a more general reformulation of distribution-free sample-based
property testing, which allows a richer class of properties to be defined. For the remainder of

Section

6
, we are interested in a certain family of
labeled-distribution properties that we call
density properties
.

Definition 6.20

(Density Property)
.

A property
\Xi
of proper labeled distributions is a
density property
if there exists a set

\Pi
of probability distributions over
\mathbb{N}
such that
\Pi=\Pi(\Xi)
; i. e. for any proper labeled
distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
, it holds that
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
if and only if
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}\in\Pi
.

In this subsection, our goal is to establish the relationships between testing density properties
and distribution testing under the parity trace, which are illustrated in

Figure

1
. Here we prove the
\to
relations; examples showing the
\not\to

relations are discussed in
Appendix

E
.

(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{TV})

\begin{subarray}{c}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.8375pt\kern-3.04367pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.3125pt\kern-2.49922pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}

(\textsf{ParityTrace},\mathsf{TV})

\downarrow\quad\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.04861pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.84029pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}

\downarrow\quad\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.04861pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.84029pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\uparrow}}}

(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{edit})

\begin{subarray}{c}\longrightarrow\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}

(\textsf{ParityTrace},\mathsf{edit})

Figure 1:
Summary of the relative strengths of testing models and distance metrics. An arrow

(\mathcal{M}_{1},d_{1})\to(\mathcal{M}_{2},d_{2})
means that a tester in model
\mathcal{M}_{1}
with respect to distance
d_{1}

implies a tester in model
\mathcal{M}_{2}
with respect to distance
d_{2}
, while
\not\to
means that the
implication does not hold in general.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
\Xi
be any density property, and let
\epsilon>0
. Then
\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\pi\in\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
, so that
\pi=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
for some
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
. Suppose for contradiction that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
for
some
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}\in\Pi(\Xi)
. Let
a_{1}<a_{2}<a_{3}<\dotsm
be the alternation sequence
of
f
, and define
a_{0}=-\infty
. Then we define the labeled distribution
\mathcal{F}_{f}
as follows.
Below, we assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{D}_{f}
is 1-proper; if it is 0-proper, we require
to adjust some of the indices by 1.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\delta_{i}\vcentcolon=\pi^{\prime}(i)-\sum_{x\in(a_{i-1},a_{i}]}\mathcal{D}(x)
.
If
\delta_{i}\geq 0
, for
x\in(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
, we may obtain
\mathcal{F}
on interval
(a_{i-1},a_{i}]

by setting
\mathcal{F}(z)=\mathcal{D}(z)+\delta
for an arbitrarily chosen element
z\in(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
, and
then choosing
\mathcal{F}(x)=\mathcal{D}(x)
for the remaining
x\neq z
in this interval.

If
\delta_{i}<0
, we may obtain
\mathcal{F}
on interval
(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
by subtracting a total of

|\delta_{i}|
from the densities
\mathcal{D}(x)
inside the interval; this is possible, since we must have

\sum_{a_{i-1}<x\leq a_{i}}\mathcal{D}(x)\geq|\delta_{i}|
.

It is easy to verify that this construction satisfies
\pi_{f,\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}
. Since

\Xi
is a density property, it holds that
\mathcal{F}_{f}\in\Xi
. We can see that

\displaystyle\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{F}_{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x}|\mathcal{D}(x)-\mathcal{F}(x)|=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\left(\sum_{a_{i-1}<x\leq a_{i}}|\mathcal{D}(x)-\mathcal{F}(x)|\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}|\delta_{i}|=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\epsilon\,.

But this contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
. So

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))
.
∎

Density properties are closed under Boolean operations; in particular, we require the next fact,
which follows by definition.

Fact 6.22
.

Suppose
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
are density properties. Then
\Xi_{1}\cap\Xi_{2}
is a density property.

We will show that labeled distribution testing, and distribution testing under the parity trace,
are essentially equivalent for density properties. The first step is to show that labeled
distribution testers for density properties can always be transformed into a restricted type of
tester that ignores the absolute position of the sample points, and keeps only their labels and
their order. This proof is inspired by one in
[
DKN15a
]
. We require some notation.

For any multiset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
of size
m
, write
S=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dotsm,x_{m}\}
where we put

x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\dotsm\leq x_{m}
. Then for any
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,1\}
, we will write the ordered
sequence of points in
S
labeled by
f
as

S_{f}\vcentcolon=\left((x_{1},f(x_{1})),(x_{2},f(x_{2})),\dotsc,(x_{m},f(x_{m}))\right)\,.

Then we define

\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f})\vcentcolon=(f(x_{1}),f(x_{2}),\dotsc,f(x_{k}))\,.

Fact 6.23
.

Let
(f,\mathcal{D})
be any proper labeled distribution. For any
m
, let
T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}},m)

and
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)
. Then the random variables
\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f})
and
\mathsf{trace}(T)
are identically
distributed.

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
be any density properties, let
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and suppose there is
an algorithm
A
and a number
m
such that:

1.

If
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{1}

then
\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(S_{f})\text{ accepts }\right]\geq\alpha
; and,

2.

If
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{2}

then
\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(S_{f})\text{ rejects }\right]\geq\alpha
.

Then for any
\delta>0
, there is an algorithm
A^{\prime}
satisfying

1.

If
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{1}

then
\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A^{\prime}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ accepts }\right]\geq\alpha-\delta
; and,

2.

If
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{2}

then
\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A^{\prime}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ rejects }\right]\geq\alpha-\delta
.

To prove this lemma, we require the infinite Ramsey theorem. For any set
X
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

{X\choose n}
denote the set of size
n
subsets of
X
.

Theorem 6.25

(Infinite Ramsey Theorem
[
Ram09
]
)
.

Fix any
c,n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
X
be any countably infinite set. For any coloring of
{X\choose n}
by
c
colors, there exists an infinite set
Y\subseteq X
such that sets in
{Y\choose n}

have the same color.

We may now prove our
Lemma

6.24
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.24
.

Since
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}
are density
properties, they have associated sets of density sequences
\Pi(\Xi_{1})
and
\Pi(\Xi_{2})
.

On input
(f,\mathcal{D})
, the algorithm
A
receives
S_{f}
, where
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)
. For each multiset

S\subseteq\mathbb{N}
of size
m
, the algorithm’s decision on
S_{f}
can be written as
A(S_{f})=A_{S}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))
, where
A_{S}:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}
. There are at most
b=2^{2^{m}}
possible decision
functions. We identify each possible decision function
\Delta:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}
with an element of

[b]
.

For each subset
S\subset\mathbb{N}
of size
m
, we color
S
with the function
A_{S}
, which we have
identified with an element of
[b]
. By
Theorem

6.25
, there exists an infinite set
N\subset\mathbb{N}
such that all
S\subset\mathbb{N}
of size
m
have the same color. Then there exists a decision
function
\Delta:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}
such that, for each
S\in{N\choose m}
,
A_{S}=\Delta
.

We now define the algorithm
A^{\prime}
as follows. On input
\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f})=(f(x_{1}),f(x_{2}),\dotsc,f(x_{m}))
,
A^{\prime}
will simply output
\Delta(f(x_{1}),f(x_{2}),\dotsc,f(x_{m}))
. It remains to show that
this algorithm will be correct.

Fix any input
(f,\mathcal{D})
; without loss of generality, we assume
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi_{1}
, since the
analogous argument will hold for
\Xi_{2}
. Let
a_{1}<a_{2}<a_{3}<\dotsm
be the alternation
sequence for
f
. We will also assume that
(f,\mathcal{D})
is 1-proper, since a similar argument will
hold when
(f,\mathcal{D})
is 0-proper (the difference being that we would have
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(1)=0
and

\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(i+1)=\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
instead of
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(i)=\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
).

Choose
C=\lceil m^{2}/\delta\rceil
. Since
N
is infinite, we may choose a one-to-one mapping

\gamma:\mathbb{N}\to N\cup\{\infty\}
, satisfying

1<\gamma(a_{1})<\gamma(a_{2})<\dotsm<\gamma(a_{k})<\dotsm\,,

and define
\gamma(a_{0})=1
for ease of notation. We may choose
\gamma
to satisfy
|N\cap(\gamma(a_{i-1}),\gamma(a_{i})]|\geq C
for each
i\geq 1
. Define a distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by
assigning value
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{C}\cdot\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
for the first
C
elements
x\in N\cap(\gamma(a_{i-1}),\gamma(a_{i})]
, and define the function
f^{\prime}:\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,1\}
as the unique
function with alternation sequence
\gamma(a_{1})<\gamma(a_{2})<\dotsm
. Observe that, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\pi_{f^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}(i)=\mathcal{D}(\gamma(a_{i-1}),\gamma(a_{i})]=C\cdot\frac{1}{C}\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}(i)\,,

so
(f^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\in\Xi_{1}
because
\Xi_{1}
is a density property. So

\alpha\leq\underset{S^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A_{S^{\prime}}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}))\text{ accepts }\right]\,.

We have
\supp(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\subseteq N
, so
S^{\prime}\subseteq N
with probability 1. So, if
S^{\prime}
is a set of
size
m
(i. e. each element of the multiset
S
occurs with multiplicity 1), then
A_{S^{\prime}}=\Delta
.
Let
F(S^{\prime})
be the event that
S^{\prime}
is a set of size
m
. Since each element of
N
has density at
most
1/C
, the union bound gives

\underset{S^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\neg F(S^{\prime})\right]<\frac{m^{2}}{C}\leq\delta\,.

Then

\displaystyle\alpha\leq\underset{S^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A_{S^{\prime}}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}))\text{ accepts }\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underset{S^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Delta(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}))\text{ accepts }\right]+\underset{S^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\neg F(S^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle<\underset{S^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Delta(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}))\text{ accepts }\right]+\delta\,.

Now observe that, for
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)
, the variables

\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}})
and
\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f})
are identically distributed. So we have

\alpha-\delta<\underset{S^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Delta(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}))\text{ accepts }\right]=\underset{S}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Delta(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ accepts }\right]\,.

This concludes the proof, since we may repeat the analogous argument for
\Xi_{2}
.
∎

We may now establish the general equivalence between labeled distribution testing and distribution
testing under the parity trace. (Note that the second part of the lemma below does not require that

\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
are density properties.)

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
be properties of labeled distributions. Then:

1.

Suppose that
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
are density properties.
If there is a
(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2})
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity
m
and
success probability
\alpha
, then for any
\delta>0
there is a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\Pi(\Xi_{2}))
-distribution tester under the parity trace, with sample complexity
m
and success
probability
\alpha-\delta
.

2.

If there is a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\Pi(\Xi_{2}))
-distribution tester under the parity
trace with sample complexity
m
and success probability
\alpha
, then there is a
(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2})
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity
m
and success probability
\alpha
.

Proof.

Suppose there is a
(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester
A
, with sample
complexity
m
. By
Lemma

6.24
, for any
\delta>0
, there is a tester

A^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{1}

\displaystyle\implies\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A^{\prime}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ accepts }\right]>\alpha-\delta

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{2}

\displaystyle\implies\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A^{\prime}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ rejects }\right]>\alpha-\delta\,.

Suppose
\pi\in\Pi(\Xi_{1})
. Then there exists
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{1}
such that
\pi=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
. Using the fact that
\mathsf{trace}(T)
and
\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f})
are identically distributed when

T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)
and
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)
(
6.23
), we have

\underset{T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A^{\prime}(\mathsf{trace}(T))\text{ accepts }\right]=\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A^{\prime}(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ accepts }\right]>\alpha-\delta\,.

The analogous argument holds when
\pi\in\Pi(\Xi_{2})
, which concludes the first part of the
proof.

Now suppose there is a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\Pi(\Xi_{2}))
-distribution tester under the parity
trace, with sample complexity
m
and success probability
\alpha
, so there is an algorithm
A

such that

\displaystyle\pi\in\Pi(\Xi_{1})

\displaystyle\implies\underset{T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(T)\text{ accepts }\right]>\alpha

\displaystyle\pi\in\Pi(\Xi_{2})

\displaystyle\implies\underset{T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(T)\text{ rejects }\right]>\alpha\,.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{1}
. Then
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}\in\Pi(\Xi_{1})
. Again using the fact that

\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f})
and
\mathsf{trace}(T)
are identically distributed when
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)
and
T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}},m)
(
6.23
), we have

\underset{S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(\mathsf{trace}^{*}(S_{f}))\text{ accepts }\right]=\underset{T\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)}{\mathbb{P}}\left[A(\mathsf{trace}(T))\text{ accepts }\right]>\alpha\,.

The analogous argument holds when
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Xi_{2}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

To introduce the distance metrics into the equivalence, we require:

Proposition 6.27
.

Let
\Xi
be any density property and let
\epsilon>0
. Then
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
is a
density property, and

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))=\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))\,.

Proof.

It is evident that
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
is a density property, because
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),(g,\mathcal{E}))=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}})
for any labeled distributions
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and

\mathcal{E}_{g}
, so that
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
is defined entirely by the density sequences.

We first prove
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))\subseteq\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
.
Let
\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))
, so that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})>\epsilon
for all

\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi(\Xi)
. Suppose for contradiction that
\pi\notin\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
. Let

(f,\mathcal{D})
be any labeled distribution such that
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}=\pi
. Then
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),\Xi)\leq\epsilon
, so there exists
(g,\mathcal{E})\in\Xi
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
with
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}=\pi_{f^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
, such that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}},\mathcal{E}_{g})\leq\epsilon
. But then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi(\Xi))\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{f^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}},\mathcal{E}_{g})\leq\epsilon\,,

which is a contradiction. This establishes
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))\subseteq\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
.

Next, we prove
\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))
. Let
\pi\in\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
, so that
\pi=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
for some
(f,\mathcal{D})
that satisfies

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),\Xi)>\epsilon
. Suppose for contradiction that
\pi\notin\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))
, so that there exists
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi(\Xi)
such that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
. Then
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}
for some
(g,\mathcal{E})\in\Xi
, so

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),\Xi)\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),(g,\mathcal{E}))=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\epsilon\,,

which is a contradiction. This concludes the proof.
∎

We may now establish the arrow
(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{edit})\to(\textsf{ParityTrace},\mathsf{edit})
from
Figure

1
.

Lemma 6.28
.

Let
\Xi
be any density property and suppose there is a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity
m
. Then for any
\delta>0
,
there is a
(\Pi(\Xi),\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi)),\alpha-\delta)
-distribution tester under
the parity trace, with sample complexity
m
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

6.27
,
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
is a density property.
Therefore
Lemma

6.26
yields a

(\Pi(\Xi),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)),\alpha-\delta)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace, for any
\delta>0
, with sample complexity
m
. By
Proposition

6.27
, we
obtain a
(\Pi(\Xi),\textsc{far}_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{edit}}(\Pi(\Xi)),\alpha-\delta)
-distribution tester under
the parity trace.
∎

The following simple fact establishes
(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{TV})\to(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{edit})
from

Figure

1
.

Fact 6.29
.

Let
\Xi
be any property of (proper) labeled distributions, and let
\epsilon>0
. Then

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
.

Proof.

This follows from the inequality
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),(g,\mathcal{E}))\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})
for
any two proper labeled distributions
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathcal{E}_{g}
.
∎

We now state a convenient lemma for later use.

Lemma 6.30
.

Let
\Xi
be any density property and suppose there is a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity
m
. Then for any
\delta>0
,
there is a
(\Pi(\Xi),\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi)),\alpha-\delta)
-distribution tester under the
parity trace, with sample complexity
m
.

The arrow
(\textsf{ParityTrace},\mathsf{edit})\rightarrow(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{edit})
is proved as follows. Suppose we have
a
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi),\alpha)
-distribution tester under the parity trace.
Let
\Xi=\Xi(\Pi)
be the corresponding density property, so that
\Pi=\Pi(\Xi)
.
By
Proposition

6.27
,

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))=\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
, and thus by

Lemma

6.26
we have a

(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester.

The arrow
(\textsf{ParityTrace},\mathsf{TV})\rightarrow(\textsf{LabeledDist},\mathsf{TV})
is similar. Suppose we have
a
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi),\alpha)
-distribution tester under the parity trace.
Let
\Xi=\Xi(\Pi)
be the corresponding density property, so that
\Pi=\Pi(\Xi)
.
By
Lemma

6.21
,

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi))
, so we get a

(\Pi(\Xi),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)),\alpha)
-distribution tester under the parity trace.
By
Lemma

6.26
we have a

(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),\alpha)
-labeled distribution tester.

6.6
Testing Uniformly
k
-Alternating Functions

We now prove our main result for labeled distribution testing, restated below for convenience,
which is an application of our main
Theorem

1.1
. First, we observe that the edit and TV
distances coincide when one of the distributions is uniform; we defer the proof to

Section

D.4
.

Lemma 6.31
.

There exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that the following holds.
Let
\pi
be the distribution over
\mathbb{N}
that is uniformly supported on
[k]
, and

\pi^{\prime}
be another probability distribution over
\mathbb{N}
supported within
[k]
.
Then
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\geq c\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
.

Remark 6.32
.

The statement of our main
Theorem

1.1
leaves open the possibility of a uniformity tester
under the parity trace, with respect to the
edit distance
, that beats the lower bound of that
theorem. This is because an edit distance tester is weaker than a TV distance tester, due to
inequality (
19
). The above lemma shows that we may strengthen the
lower bound in
Theorem

1.1
to hold for testers in the edit distance as well.

Theorem 6.33
.

Let
\Xi_{1}
be the uniformly
2k
-alternating labeled distributions, and let
\Xi_{2}
be the

2k
-alternating labeled distributions that are
\epsilon
-far in total variation distance from

\Xi_{1}
. Then the sample complexity of
(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},2/3)
-labeled distribution testing is

\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{k^{4/5}}{\epsilon^{4/5}}+\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
.

Proof of upper bound.

Let
K
be the property of
2k
-alternating labeled distributions, which is a density property,
with
\Pi(K)
being the property of density sequences supported on
[2k]
. Then
\Xi_{2}=\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K
and
\Pi(\Xi_{2})=\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K)=\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1}))\cap\Pi(K)
, while
\Pi(\Xi_{1})
contains only the uniform
distribution
\mu
supported on
[2k]
. By
Lemma

6.21
,

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1}))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))
. Therefore, a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))\cap\Pi(K),\alpha)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, with
sample complexity
m
, is also a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K),\alpha)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, with sample complexity
m
. The conclusion now
follows from
Lemma

6.26
.
∎

Proof of lower bound.

We begin with a specialized variant of the argument from

Lemma

6.28
.
Suppose there is a
(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},2/3)
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity
m
,
and recall that
\Xi_{2}=\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K
. By
6.29
, we have

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}(\Xi_{1})\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}(\Xi_{1})
, so this is also a
(\Xi_{1},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K,2/3)
-labeled distribution tester. By
Proposition

6.27
,

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})
is a density property, so by
6.22
,

\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K
is a density property. From

Lemma

6.26
, we then obtain a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K),2/3-\delta)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, for
any
\delta>0
, with sample complexity
m
. Observe that
\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1})\cap K)=\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi_{1}))\cap\Pi(K)
. By
Proposition

6.27
, we have a

(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))\cap\Pi(K),2/3-\delta)
-distribution tester under
the parity trace.

Note that
\Pi(\Xi_{1})=\{\mu\}
, where
\mu
is the uniform distribution supported on
[2k]
.
Let
0<c<1
be the constant from
Lemma

6.31
, and consider any distribution

\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/c}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))
. Then by
Lemma

6.31
, we have

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\mu)\geq c\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\mu)>c\cdot(\epsilon/c)=\epsilon\,,

so
\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))
. Then
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/c}(\Pi(X_{1}))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))
.

Therefore, our tester is also a
(\Pi(\Xi_{1}),\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/c}(\Pi(\Xi_{1}))\cap\Pi(K),2/3-\delta)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, with sample complexity
m
. By
Theorem

5.1
, we must have the desired lower bound of

m=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)^{4/5}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)\,.\qed

6.7
Promise-Free Testing
k
-Alternating and Uniformly
k
-Alternating
Functions

Theorem

1.1
proves a tight bound on testing whether a distribution supported on
[k]
is
uniform, under the parity trace. We use testing-by-learning to prove a bound on the harder problem
of testing whether a distribution is uniform on
[k]
,
without
the promise that the input is
supported on
[k]
.

Theorem 6.34
.

Fix domain
\mathbb{N}
. Let
\Pi
contain only the uniform distribution over
[k]
. There is a
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, with sample complexity

O\left(\frac{k}{\epsilon}+\frac{k}{\epsilon^{2}\log k}\right)
.

This will follow from the next lemma, using fact that
\Pi=\Pi(\Xi)
, where
\Xi
is the property
of uniformly
k
-alternating labeled distributions, together with

Lemma

6.30
.

Lemma 6.35
.

Let
\Xi
be the uniformly
k
-alternating functions. Then there is a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),2/3)
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity

O\left(\frac{k}{\epsilon}+\frac{k}{\epsilon^{2}\log k}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
c\in(0,1)
be the universal constant in
Lemma

6.31
. We will construct a
learner-verifier pair. Let
A
be the standard PAC learning algorithm for the class of

k
-alternating functions, with error
c\epsilon/4
. This algorithm, using a sample of size
m_{A}=O(k/\epsilon)
, outputs a
k
-alternating function
g:\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,1\}
, such that with probability at
least
8/9
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})=\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)\neq g(x)\right]<c\epsilon/4\,,

where the equality is due to
D.1
. It is clear that there exists a distribution

\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
.

Suppose that
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
. We must show that there exists a distribution
\mathcal{E}
such that

\mathcal{E}_{g}\in\Xi
and
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})\leq\epsilon/4
. Let
\mu
be the uniform distribution
over
[k+1]
. Using
Proposition

D.2
, we obtain
\mathcal{E}
such that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\mathcal{E}_{g})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\mu)\,.

Using
\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}=\mu=\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
and
Lemma

6.31
, we have

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}})\leq\frac{1}{c}\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}})\leq\frac{1}{c}\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\mathcal{D}_{f})\,,

where the last inequality holds by definition. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\mathcal{E}_{g})\leq\frac{1}{c}\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\mathcal{D}_{f})\leq\epsilon/4\,.

So algorithm
A
satisfies the conditions for the learner-verifier pair. It remains to construct the
verifier
B
. Let
g:\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,1\}
be any possible output of the learner, which must be a

k
-alternating function. Then
\Pi_{g}
is the set of all distributions
\mathcal{E}
such that
\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}=\mu
, where
\mu
is the uniform distribution over
[k+1]
. Define the algorithm
B_{g}
as follows:

1.

Sample
S\sim\mathsf{samp}(\mathcal{D},m_{B})
and construct the multiset
S^{\prime}
by taking each
x\in S
and
including the number
i\in[k+1]
in
S^{\prime}
, where
i
is the unique interval
(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
that
contains
x
. Then
S^{\prime}
is distributed as
\mathsf{samp}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},m_{B})
.

2.

Use an
(\epsilon/4,\epsilon/2)
-tolerant uniformity tester on sample
S^{\prime}
to test if

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\mu)<\epsilon/4
or
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{D}},\mu)>\epsilon/2
.
This step requires
m_{B}=O\left(\frac{k}{\epsilon^{2}\log k}\right)
samples
[
VV17b
]
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{E}\in\textsc{close}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi_{g})
, so there exists
\mathcal{F}
such that

\pi_{g,\mathcal{F}}=\mu
and
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{E}_{g},\mathcal{F}_{g})<\epsilon/4
. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}},\mu)=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{F}})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{E}_{g},\mathcal{F}_{g})<\epsilon/4\,,

so the tolerant uniformity tester will accept.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{E}\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi_{g})
. For contradiction, suppose that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}},\mu)\leq\epsilon/2\,.

Using
Proposition

D.2
, we obtain
\mathcal{F}
such that
\pi_{g,\mathcal{F}}=\mu

(so
\mathcal{F}\in\Pi_{g}
), and

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{E}_{g},\mathcal{F}_{g})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}},\pi_{g,\mathcal{F}})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}},\mu)\leq\epsilon/2\,.

This contradicts
\mathcal{E}\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi_{g})
, so it must be the case that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}},\mu)>\epsilon/2
. Then the tolerant uniformity tester will correctly
reject.
∎

We also note that the no-promise problem of testing the
k
-alternating labeled distributions
inherits an upper bound of
O(k/\epsilon)
from the equivalence to distribution-free sample-based
testing:

Lemma 6.36
.

Let
\Xi
be the set of labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})
such that
f
is a
k
-alternating
function and
\mathcal{D}
is any distribution over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
m(k,\epsilon)
be the optimal sample complexity of a distribution-free sample-based
tester for
k
-alternating functions.
Then the optimal sample complexity of

(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi))
-labeled distribution testing is
\Theta(m(k,\epsilon))
.
In particular, there is such a tester with sample complexity
O(k/\epsilon)
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows from
Proposition

6.15
.
The second part follows from standard PAC learning theory, since the class of
k
-alternating
functions has VC dimension
k+1
, along with the testing by learning reduction
[
GGR98
]
.
∎

As with the previous result, this lemma implies a bound for testing the
support size

of distributions under the parity trace; this is the starting point for our next discussion, on the
connections between distribution testing under the parity trace and distribution-free sample based
testing.

6.8
Distribution-Free Sample-Based Property Testing

We now prove that testing support size
k
under the parity trace is equivalent to testing

k
-alternating functions in the standard distribution-free sample-based model (whose optimal sample
complexity is unknown
[
BFH21
]
). This has the interesting consequence, in

Lemma

6.43
, that an improved lower bound on testing support size under the
parity trace could give a better lower bound for testing halfspaces in the distribution-free
sample-based model.

We require the following proposition about edit distance, which is proved in

Section

D.5
.

Proposition 6.37
.

Let
\Xi
be the property of proper labeled distributions
(g,\mathcal{E})
where
\pi_{g,\mathcal{E}}
has support
size at most
k
. Then for any proper labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})
,
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}((f,\mathcal{D}),\Xi)\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}((f,\mathcal{D}),\Xi)
.

Theorem 6.38
.

Let
\Pi
be the class of distributions on domain
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most
k
. Let

m_{1}(k,\epsilon)
be the optimal sample complexity of a
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, and let
m_{2}(k,\epsilon)
be the optimal sample
complexity of a distribution-free sample-based tester for
k
-alternating functions. Then

m_{1}(k,\epsilon)=\Theta(m_{2}(k,\epsilon))
.

Proof of first direction.

We wish to construct a
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace, with sample complexity
O(m_{2}(k,\epsilon))
.
Let
\Xi
be the set of labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})
such that
f
is a
(k-1)
-alternating
function and
\mathcal{D}
is any distribution over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\Xi^{\prime}
be the set of labeled distributions

(f,\mathcal{D})
on domain
\mathbb{Z}
such that
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
has support size at most
k
, so that
\Pi(\Xi^{\prime})=\Pi
.
Lemma

6.36
gives a

(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),3/4)
-labeled distribution tester with sample complexity

O(m_{2}(k,\epsilon))
.
We will construct a
(\Xi^{\prime},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime}),3/4)
-labeled distribution tester with
sample complexity
O(m_{2}(k,\epsilon))
, from which the conclusion will hold by

Lemma

6.30
.

Observe that
\Xi\subset\Xi^{\prime}
since for any
(k-1)
alternating function
f
and any distribution

\mathcal{D}
,
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
has support size at most
k
. We show that for any labeled distribution
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi^{\prime}
, there exists a labeled distribution
(g,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
such that
