\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})=0
, so that
\mathcal{D}_{f}=\mathcal{D}_{g}
. Let
a_{1}<a_{2}<\dotsm
be the alternation sequence for
f
,
and use the convention
a_{0}=-\infty
. Since
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
has support size at most
k
, there are
at most
k
intervals
(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
such that
\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]>0
. Construct
g
by assigning

g(x)=1-f(x)
for all
x
belonging to any interval
(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
that satisfies
\mathcal{D}(a_{i-1},a_{i}]=0
. By
D.4
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{D}_{g})=0
. The resulting
function has at most
k-1
alternation points, so
(g,\mathcal{D})\in\Xi
.

Suppose
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
and suppose for contradiction that there is
(g,\mathcal{E})\in\Xi^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})\leq\epsilon
. Then there is
(g^{\prime},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\in\Xi
such
that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}}=\mathcal{E}_{g}
, so
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{E}_{g})\leq\epsilon
,
and
(f,\mathcal{D})\notin\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)
, a contradiction. So
(f,\mathcal{D})\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime})
. Then
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi)=\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime})
.

Then any
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),3/4)
-labeled distribution tester is also a
(\Xi^{\prime},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime}),3/4)
-labeled distribution tester, since samples from elements of
\Xi
are
indistinguishable from samples from elements of
\Xi^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of second direction.

We wish to construct a
(\Xi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi),2/3)
-labeled distribution tester; then the
conclusion will follow from
Proposition

6.15
.

As shown in the upper bound argument, this is equivalent to a
(\Xi^{\prime},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime}),2/3)
-labeled distribution tester, where
\Xi^{\prime}
is the class of labeled distributions
(f,\mathcal{D})

where
\pi_{f,\mathcal{D}}
has support size at most
k
. Note that
\Pi=\Pi(\Xi^{\prime})
.

By
Proposition

6.37
,
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime})=\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime})
, so this is equivalent to a
(\Xi^{\prime},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime}),2/3)
-labeled distribution tester. Since
\Xi^{\prime}
and
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime})
are density
properties, it suffices to obtain a
(\Pi(\Xi^{\prime}),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Xi^{\prime})),2/3)
-distribution
tester under the parity trace, due to
Lemma

6.26
. Finally,
apply
Proposition

6.27
.
∎

The above theorem relates the sample complexity of testing
k
-alternating functions to the
complexity of testing support size under the parity trace, with respect to the edit distance.
From here, we will reproduce the result of
[
BFH21
]
, that testing
k
-alternating functions
requires
\Omega(k/\log k)
samples, which will follow from the
\Omega(n/\log n)
lower bound
for estimating support size, due to
[
VV11
,
WY19
]
.
We use the following formulation of the result of
[
VV11
]
:

Theorem 6.39

(
[
VV11
]
)
.

For any sufficiently small constant
\delta>0
, there exists a pair of distributions

\pi^{+},\pi^{-}
whose non-zero densities are at least
\frac{1}{n}
,
such that
\pi^{+}
has support size at least
(1-\delta)n
,
\pi^{-}
has support size at most

(1+\delta)\frac{n}{2}
, and distinguishing between them requires

\Omega\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)
samples.

Their result also applies to estimating the
entropy
of distributions, in which
case they obtain an
\Omega(n/\epsilon\log n)
lower bound by constructing distributions

\pi^{+}_{\epsilon},\pi^{-}_{\epsilon}
that with probability
\epsilon
draw from
\pi^{+},\pi^{-}

respectively, and otherwise draw an element
\bot
; this shrinks the entropy gap to an
\epsilon

fraction of the original gap, and distinguishing between
\pi^{+}_{\epsilon}
and
\pi^{-}_{\epsilon}

requires an
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
fraction more samples.
While this argument does not apply to the support size estimation problem, which requires that
densities be lower bounded by
1/n
, it does apply to testing support size against TV
distance:

Corollary 6.40
.

Let
\Pi
be the set of distributions over
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most
n
, and let

\epsilon>0
. Then any
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi))
-distribution tester requires sample
size at least
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon\log n}\right)
.

To apply this lower bound, we reduce from testing with respect to TV distance, to testing with
respect to the edit distance. We require the following lemma, whose proof we defer to

Section

D.6
.

Lemma 6.41
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\Pi_{k}
be the set of distributions over
\mathbb{N}
supported on at most
k
elements, and
let
\Pi_{2k}
be the set of distributions over
\mathbb{N}
supported on at most
2k
elements.
Let
\pi
be a finitely-supported probability distribution over
\mathbb{N}
, and let
\pi^{\prime}
be the
probability distribution over
\mathbb{N}
given by
\pi^{\prime}(2i-1)=\pi^{\prime}(2i)\vcentcolon=\pi(i)/2
for each

i\in\supp(\pi)
.
Then
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi^{\prime},\Pi_{2k})\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\Pi_{k})
.

We may now establish the simple reduction.

Lemma 6.42
.

Let
\Pi_{2k}
be the set of distributions on domain
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most
2k
, and let

\epsilon>0
. Then the sample complexity of a
(\Pi_{2k},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi_{2k}))
-distribution tester is at least

\Omega\left(\frac{k}{\epsilon\log k}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi_{k}
be the set of distributions on domain
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most
k
. We will
reduce
(\Pi_{k},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi_{k}))
-distribution testing to
(\Pi_{2k},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi_{k}))
-distribution testing, from which the conclusion follows, due to

Corollary

6.40
.

On input distribution
\pi
, the algorithm proceeds as follows. We define the distribution
\pi^{\prime}

where for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\pi^{\prime}(2i)\vcentcolon=\pi(i)/2
and
\pi^{\prime}(2i-1)\vcentcolon=\pi(i)/2
. The
algorithm may simulate a sample from
\pi^{\prime}
by sampling
\bm{i}\sim\pi
and then taking
2\bm{i}
or

2\bm{i}-1
with equal probability. The algorithm then simulates the
(\Pi_{2k},\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi_{2k}))
-distribution tester on input
\pi^{\prime}
. If
\pi\in\Pi_{k}
, then it
is clear that
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{2k}
, so the algorithm will correctly accept (with probability at least

2/3
). If
\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{TV}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi_{k})
, then by
Lemma

6.41
,
\pi^{\prime}\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi_{2k})
, so the algorithm will correctly reject (with probability at
least
2/3
).
∎

We are now prepared to recover a number of the results of
[
BFH21
]
for testing properties with
domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, including halfspaces, intersections of halfspaces, and decision
trees. The idea is to reduce from testing
k
-alternating functions to testing the property in
question, by taking the one-dimensional space and embedding it into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in an appropriate way.
This technique was also used in
[
ES20
,
BFH21
]
. We provide a formal proof for halfspaces, and
refer to
[
BFH21
]
for the details on intersections of halfspaces and decision trees.

Lemma 6.43
.

Let
\Pi
be the property of distributions on
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most
k
. For any
d
and

\epsilon>0
, let
h(d,\epsilon)
be the sample complexity of testing
halfspaces
on domain

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, in the distribution-free sample-based model.Then there is a
(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Pi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, with sample complexity

O(h(k+1,\epsilon))
.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

6.38
and the following reduction from testing

k
-alternating functions to testing halfspaces.

On input
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,1\}
and distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathbb{Z}
, consider the one-to-one function

\psi(x)\vcentcolon=(1,x,x^{2},\dotsc,x^{k})
and the distribution
\psi\mathcal{D}
defined as the
distribution over
\psi(x)
where
x\sim\mathcal{D}
. The tester will simulate the halfspace tester on
samples
(\psi(x),f(x))
where
(x,f(x))\sim\mathcal{D}_{f}
.

Note that a function
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,1\}
is
k
-alternating if and only if there exists a degree
k

polynomial
p:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)=\sign(p(x))
, where we define
\sign(z)=0
of
z\leq 0
and
\sign(z)=1
if
z>0
. Then
f
is
k
-alternating if and only if there exists
w=(w_{0},w_{1},\dotsc,w_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
such that
f(x)=\sign(\langle w,\psi(x)\rangle)
. So
f
is

k
-alternating if and only if there exists a halfspace
g:\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\to\{0,1\}
such that
f(x)=g(\phi(x))
on all
x
.

Write
\psi(\mathcal{D}_{f})
for the distribution of
(\psi(x),f(x))
when
x\sim\mathcal{D}
.
So for any
k
-alternating function
f
, there exists a halfspace
g
such that
\psi(\mathcal{D}_{f})=(\psi\mathcal{D})_{g}
. On the other hand, for any halfspace
g
, there exists a
k
-alternating function
f

such that
\psi(\mathcal{D}_{f})=(\psi\mathcal{D})_{g}
.

Then, for any
k
-alternating function
f
and distribution
\mathcal{D}
, samples from
\psi(\mathcal{D}_{f})
are
indistinguishable from samples from
(\psi\mathcal{D})_{g}
, where
g
is a halfspace, so a halfspace tester
will accept. On the other hand, for any function
f
that is
\epsilon
-far from
k
-alternating
under distribution
\mathcal{D}
, consider an arbitrary function
f^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\to\{0,1\}
such that

f^{\prime}(\psi(x))=f(x)
on all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
(\psi\mathcal{D})_{f^{\prime}}=\psi(\mathcal{D}_{f})
, so halfspace tester
will perform identically on the simulated samples
(\psi(x),f(x))
as on the samples
(z,f^{\prime}(z))\sim(\psi\mathcal{D})_{f^{\prime}}
, so the tester performs as if it was given input
f^{\prime}
and
\psi\mathcal{D}
.

If there exists a halfspace
g
such that
\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f^{\prime}(\psi(x))=g(\psi(x))\right]\leq\epsilon
, then
\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[f(x)=g(\psi(x))\right]\leq\epsilon
, where
g(\psi(\cdot))
is

k
-alternating, which is a contradiction. So it must be that
f^{\prime}
is
\epsilon
-far from being a
halfspace with respect to
\psi\mathcal{D}
, so the halfspace tester rejects
f^{\prime}
.
∎

With this reduction, together with the lower bound provided by

Lemma

6.42
, we recover the following bounds. Note that
[
BFH21
]

only stated their bounds for constant
\epsilon
, but the amplification argument above could also
be applied directly to their results.

Corollary 6.44

(See
[
BFH21
]
)
.

The following lower bounds hold for the sample complexity of testing in the distribution-free
sample-based model:

1.

k
-Alternating functions over
\mathbb{R}
:
\Omega\left(\frac{k}{\epsilon\log k}\right)
;

2.

Halfspaces over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon\log n}\right)
;

3.

Intersections of
k
halfspaces over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:
\Omega\left(\frac{nk}{\epsilon\log(nk)}\right)
;

4.

Size
k
decision trees over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:
\Omega\left(\frac{k}{\epsilon\log k}\right)
.

7
Property Testing in the Trace Reconstruction Model

We begin by formally defining property testing in the trace reconstruction model. We then discuss
the connection between the (relative) edit distance on strings and the edit distance on
distributions that we introduced, which will be a crucial component of our results.

For a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and retention rate
\rho\in(0,1)
,
\mathsf{del}(x,\rho)
is the distribution
of substrings of
x
obtained by deleting each character of
x
independently with probability

1-\rho
. A sample
\bm{T}\sim\mathsf{del}(x,\rho)
is called a
trace
from
x
with deletion rate

1-\rho
.

Definition 7.1

(Trace Testing)
.

Let
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
be properties of strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
, and let
\alpha,\rho\in(0,1)
,
which we call the
success probability
and
retention rate
, respectively. A

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,\alpha)
-trace testing algorithm using
m
traces
is an algorithm
A

such that, for
m
independent traces
\bm{T}_{1}(x),\dotsc,\bm{T}_{m}(x)
obtained from
x
with
deletion rate
1-\rho
,

1.

If
x\in\Psi_{1}
then

\mathbb{P}\left[A(\bm{T}_{1}(x),\dotsc,\bm{T}_{m}(x))\text{ accepts }\right]\geq\alpha
; and,

2.

If
x\in\Psi_{2}
then

\mathbb{P}\left[A(\bm{T}_{1}(x),\dotsc,\bm{T}_{m}(x))\text{ rejects }\right]\geq\alpha
.

Many of our results refer to
n
-block strings and uniform
n
-block strings, which we now define.

Definition 7.2
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We say
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
is an
n
-block string
if
x
consists of at most
n
blocks,
where a block is an all-1s string or all-0s string. For integer
n
that divides
N
, the

1-uniform
n
-block string
is

1^{N/n}0^{N/n}1^{N/n}\dotsm\mathsf{par}(n)^{N/n}
and the
0-uniform
n
-block string

is
0^{N/n}1^{N/n}0^{N/n}\dotsm(1-\mathsf{par}(n))^{N/n}
. We say that
x
is
a
uniform
n
-block string
if it is the 1-uniform or 0-uniform
n
-block string.

Definition 7.3

(Relative Edit Distance)
.

Writing
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{string-edit}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{0,1\}^{*}\to\mathbb{Z}
for the edit distance on
strings, we define the
relative
string edit distance
on strings
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and
y\in\{0,1\}^{M}
as

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,y)=\frac{2}{N+M}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{string-edit}}(x,y)\,.

We define a correspondence between strings and probability distributions, which allow us to
relate property testing for trace reconstruction to distribution testing under the parity trace.

Definition 7.4

(String to Distribution Correspondence)
.

For any fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
and probability distribution
\pi
over
\mathbb{N}
, whose densities are integer
multiples of
1/N
, we define the string
\psi(\pi)\in\{0,1\}^{N}
as

\psi(\pi)\vcentcolon=1^{\pi(1)\cdot N}0^{\pi(2)\cdot N}1^{\pi(3)\cdot N}0^{\pi(3)\cdot N}\dotsm\,,

where
b^{k}
denotes the character
b
repeated
k
times. This map is not one-to-one. But, for
strings
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, we define a probability distribution
\psi^{-1}(x)
as follows. Define the
function
f_{x}:\mathbb{N}\to\{0,1\}
as
f_{x}(i)=x_{i}
for each
i\in[N]
(and 1 elsewhere), and let
\mathcal{D}

be the uniform distribution over
[N]
. Then

\psi^{-1}(x)\vcentcolon=\pi_{f_{x},\mathcal{D}}\,.

One may verify that
\psi(\psi^{-1}(x))=x
for any string
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
.
To each property
\Psi
of strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
, we associate the property of probability
distributions
\Pi=\Pi(\Psi)\vcentcolon=\{\psi^{-1}(x):x\in\Psi\}
, with
\psi^{-1}
as
defined in
Definition

7.4
.
For any such
\Psi
, let
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi)
denote the set of strings
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}

such that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)>\epsilon
, where

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)\vcentcolon=\min_{y\in\Psi}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,y)\,.

Observation 7.5
.

If
x
is an
n
-block string, then
\psi^{-1}(x)
is supported on at most
n
elements.
If
x
is the 1-uniform
n
-block string, then
\psi^{-1}(x)
is the uniform distribution over

\{1,2,\dotsc,n\}
.
If
x
is the 0-uniform
n
-block string, then
\psi^{-1}(x)
is the uniform distribution over

\{2,3,\dotsc,n+1\}
.

Recall that we have defined the edit distance on distributions as the natural metric for
distribution testing under the parity trace. The next lemma shows that the edit distance for
distributions is essentially equivalent to the relative edit distance on strings, under the
string-to-distribution correspondence. This will allow us to obtain equivalences between
distribution testing under the parity trace, and property testing for trace reconstruction.
We defer the proof to
Section

D.3
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Fix any
N
and let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
be probability distributions over
\mathbb{N}
whose densities are integer
multiples of
1/N
. Then

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),\psi(\pi^{\prime}))\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),\psi(\pi^{\prime}))
.

7.1
Single-Trace Upper Bounds

We seek to obtain algorithms for testing properties of strings, with respect to the relative
edit distance, by reducing to testing properties of distributions under the parity
trace, with respect to the edit distance on distributions. We will make use of the following simple
technique, which turns a trace from a string (i. e. produced by a deletion channel) into
(the parity trace of) a Poissonized sample from the associated probability distribution
(i. e. the result of sampling with replacement).

Proposition 7.7
.

Fix
\rho\in(0,1)
. There exists an algorithm
Poissonize
which consumes a binary
string and produces another binary string satisfying the following. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and

x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, and suppose the input is a random trace from
x
with deletion
rate
1-\rho
. Then the output is distributed as
\mathsf{trace}(\bm{S})
, where

\bm{S}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\psi^{-1}(x),\bm{m})
and

\bm{m}\sim\mathsf{Poi}\left(N\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\rho}\right)\right)
.

Proof.

The idea is to treat each symbol in the input as indicating the event that a corresponding
Poisson random variable was non-zero, and then up-sample the symbol to the appropriate
conditional distribution to obtain a Poissonized sample.

Let
\lambda\vcentcolon=\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\rho}\right)
and let
\mathsf{Poi}_{>0}(\lambda)
denote the
distribution of a
\mathsf{Poi}(\lambda)
random variable conditional on being nonzero.

The algorithm proceeds as follows: on input string
s
, for each symbol
s_{j}
from left to
right, independently sample
\bm{z}_{j}\sim\mathsf{Poi}_{>0}(\lambda)
and append
\bm{z}_{j}
copies of

s_{j}
to the output.

For each
i\in[N]
, let
\bm{X}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\rho)
independently.
Then the input is distributed as

x_{1}^{\bm{X}_{1}}x_{2}^{\bm{X}_{2}}\dotsm x_{N-1}^{\bm{X}_{N-1}}x_{N}^{\bm{X}_{N}}\,.

Let
\pi\vcentcolon=\psi^{-1}(x)
, say it is supported on
[n]
. For each
i\in[n]
, let

\bm{Y}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(N\lambda\pi(i))
independently.
Then the target output distribution is identical to that of

1^{\bm{Y}_{1}}0^{\bm{Y}_{2}}\dotsm\mathsf{par}(n-1)^{\bm{Y}_{n-1}}\mathsf{par}(n)^{\bm{Y}_{n}}\,.

By additivity of the Poisson distribution and definition of
\psi^{-1}
, this distribution
is identical to

x_{1}^{\bm{Z}_{1}}x_{2}^{\bm{Z}_{2}}\dotsm x_{N-1}^{\bm{Z}_{N-1}}x_{N}^{\bm{Z}_{N}}\,.

where for each
i\in[N]
,
\bm{Z}_{i}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(\lambda)
independently.

By considering the random process that produces the trace from
x
along with the random process
of the algorithm, we may identify each symbol in the output of the algorithm with the location
i\in[N]
corresponding to the appearance of
x_{i}
in the trace. For each
i\in[N]
, let

\bm{K}_{i}
be the random variable denoting how many times
x_{i}
was appended to the output. Then

\bm{K}_{i}
is distributed according to the following random process: if
\bm{X}_{i}=0
then

\bm{K}_{i}\leftarrow 0
, otherwise
\bm{K}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Poi}_{>0}(\lambda)
. Note that the
\bm{K}_{i}
are
mutually independent, and the output of the algorithm is

x_{1}^{\bm{K}_{1}}x_{2}^{\bm{K}_{2}}\dotsm x_{N-1}^{\bm{K}_{N-1}}x_{N}^{\bm{K}_{N}}\,.

Therefore we will be done if, for each
i\in[N]
,
\bm{Z}_{i}
and

\bm{K}_{i}
are distributed identically, which we now check. We have

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{K}_{i}=0\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}_{i}=0\right]=1-\rho
and

\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}=0\right]=e^{-\lambda}=1-\rho
, and for each
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}=k\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}>0\right]\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}=k\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{Z}_{i}>0\right]=\rho\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{Poi}_{>0}(\lambda)=k\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}_{i}=1\right]\cdot\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{K}_{i}=k\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{X}_{i}=1\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{K}_{i}=k\text{ and }\bm{X}_{i}=1\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{K}_{i}=k\right]\,.\qed

Remark 7.8
.

Although we assume that
\rho
is explicitly known to obtain
Proposition

7.7
,
this assumption is not crucial: if we only knew a lower bound
\rho_{0}
on
\rho
, we could
obtain essentially equivalent results by sub-sampling
\mathsf{Bin}(N,\rho^{\prime})
elements from
the trace with
\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{0}/C
for some large constant
C
. Then, except with negligible
probability of failure, the sample would be distributed as a trace with known deletion rate

1-\rho^{\prime}
.

Equipped with this result, we obtain testers in the trace reconstruction model from testers
in the parity trace model via a black-box reduction.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
N,m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
be properties of strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
, and let
\alpha>0
.
If there is a Poissonized
(\Pi(\Psi_{1}),\Pi(\Psi_{2}),\alpha)
-distribution tester under the
parity trace with sample complexity
m
, then there is a

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,\alpha)
-trace tester using one trace, for
\rho=1-e^{-m/N}
.

Proof.

Let
A
be a Poissonized
(\Pi(\Psi_{1}),\Pi(\Psi_{2}),\alpha)
-distribution
tester under the parity trace with sample complexity
m
. Our trace tester
B
works as follows:

1.

Receive a trace
x
with deletion rate
1-\rho
.

2.

Let
y\leftarrow\textsc{Poissonize}(x)
.

3.

Return
A(y)
.

Let
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
be random variables denoting the inputs to
B
and
A
, respectively.
By
Proposition

7.7
,
\bm{y}
is distributed as
\mathsf{trace}(\bm{S})
where

\bm{S}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\psi^{-1}(x),\bm{m})
and
\bm{m}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(N\lambda)
, where

\lambda=\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\rho}\right)=m/N
.
In other words,
\bm{y}
is distributed as the parity trace of a sample from
\psi^{-1}(x)
of
size
\mathsf{Poi}(m)
. Moreover, by definition of
\Pi(\Psi_{1})
we have that if

x\in\Psi_{1}
then
\psi^{-1}(x)\in\Pi(\Psi_{1})
, and the same for
\Psi_{2}
. Therefore
the correctness of
B
follows from the correctness of
A
.
∎

We now conclude each of our single-trace upper bounds from

Section

1.4
, using the following immediate consequence of
the equivalence of edit distances between strings and distributions.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, and let
\Psi
be a property of strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
.
Then
\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi(\Psi))
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))
. By definition of

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))
, we have
\pi=\psi^{-1}(x)
for some

x\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi)
. Thus
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and for each
y\in\Psi
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,y)>\epsilon
, and by
Lemma

7.6

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\psi^{-1}(x),\psi^{-1}(y))>\epsilon/2
.

By definition of
\Pi(\Psi)
, for each
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi(\Psi)
we have

\pi^{\prime}=\psi^{-1}(y^{\prime})
for some
y^{\prime}\in\Psi
. But then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\psi^{-1}(x),\psi^{-1}(y^{\prime}))>\epsilon/2
. Therefore

\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi(\Psi))
.
∎

Our result for testing
n
-block strings will require the following equivalence between the
relative edit distance of strings to the property of
n
-block strings, and the edit distance of
appropriate probability distributions to the property of distributions supported on at most
n

elements. We defer the proof to
Section

D.7
.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\Psi
be the set of
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
, and
let
\Pi
be the set of probability distributions over
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most

n
. Then for every distribution
\pi
over
\mathbb{N}
whose densities are integer multiples of
1/N

and for
x=\psi(\pi)
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)\,.

This implies:

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\Psi
be a property of strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
and let
\Pi
be a property
of probability distributions over
\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that for every distribution
\pi
over
\mathbb{N}
whose densities are integer multiples
of
1/N
and for
x=\psi(\pi)
, it holds
that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)
. Then

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi)\,.

Proof.

Let
I
be the set of distributions over
\mathbb{N}
whose densities are integer multiples of
1/N
.
We claim that
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi(\Psi))\cap I\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi)
.

Fix any
\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi(\Psi))\cap I
. We have that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\psi^{-1}(y))>\epsilon/2
for every
y\in\Psi
, and since all densities
in
\pi
are integer multiples of
1/N
by
\pi\in I
, letting
x\vcentcolon=\psi(\pi)
we
conclude by
Lemma

7.6
that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,y)>\epsilon/2
.
Therefore
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)>\epsilon/2
, and using
the hypothesis, we conclude that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)>\epsilon/4
and thus
\pi\in\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi)
,
establishing the first claim.

By
Proposition

7.10
,

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi(\Psi))
,
and therefore

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\cap I\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/2}(\Pi(\Psi))\cap I\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi)
. Finally, note that

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\cap I=\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))

because every member of the latter has the form
\pi=\psi^{-1}(x)
for some
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,
so we are done.
∎

The following result establishes the upper bound portion of

Theorem

1.9
.

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, and let
\Psi
be the set of
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
.
There is a
(\Psi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace
with expected trace size
\rho N=O(n/\epsilon)
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi
be the class of distributions over
\mathbb{N}
with support size at most
n
.
By
Theorem

6.38
, there is a

(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace, which we may assume is Poissonized by
Proposition

A.1
, with sample complexity

O(n/\epsilon)
.

Since for every
x\in\Psi
we have that
\psi^{-1}(x)
has support size at most
n

(
7.5
), it
follows that
\Pi(\Psi)\subseteq\Pi
.

Proposition

7.12
together with

Proposition

7.11
gives that

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi)
.

Therefore we obtain a

(\Pi(\Psi),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi)),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace with sample complexity
m=O(n/\epsilon)
.
Then
Lemma

7.9
yields a

(\Psi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace for

\rho=1-e^{-m/N}\leq m/N
, i. e. expected trace size
\rho N=O(n/\epsilon)
.
∎

Our results for trace testing uniform
n
-block strings are simpler to obtain from distribution
testing under the parity trace, because now the corresponding property
\Pi
of probability
distributions contains only the distributions corresponding to the uniform
n
-block strings.

Notation.

For fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
n
that divides
N
, let

u^{(1)}
denote the 1-uniform
n
-block string and let
u^{(0)}
denote the 0-uniform
n
-block
string.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
divides
N
.
Let
\Psi
contain only the 1-uniform
n
-block string
u^{(1)}
, and let

\Pi
contain only the uniform distribution over
[n]
.
For every distribution
\pi
over
\mathbb{N}
whose densities are integer multiples of
1/N
and
for
x=\psi(\pi)
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)\,.

Proof.

Let
\pi^{*}
be the uniform distribution over
[n]
, so that
\pi^{*}=\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)})

(
7.5
).
The two inequalities are immediate consequences of
Lemma

7.6
.
First, we have

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{*})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(1)})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)
.
Similarly,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\Pi)=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{*})\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(x,u^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(x,\Psi)
.
∎

We first show a tester for the 1-uniform
n
-block strings, and then generalize it to both types
of uniform strings to obtain
Theorem

1.8
, restated here:

Theorem 7.15
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
divides
N
, and let
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\Psi
contain only the uniform
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
. There is a

(\Psi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace with expected
trace size
\rho N=O\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}+\frac{n}{\epsilon^{2}\log n}\right)
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
divides
N
, and let
\epsilon>0
. Let
\Psi
contain only the
1-uniform
n
-block string
u^{(1)}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
. There is a
(\Psi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace with expected trace size
\rho N=O\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}+\frac{n}{\epsilon^{2}\log n}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi
contain only the uniform distribution
\pi=\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)})
over
[n]
.
By
Theorem

6.34
, there is a

(\Pi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity trace, which we
may assume is Poissonized by
Proposition

A.1
, with sample complexity

O\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}+\frac{n}{\epsilon^{2}\log n}\right)
.

Note that
\Pi=\{\pi\}=\{\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)})\}=\Pi(\Psi)
.
Moreover,
Proposition

7.12
together with

Proposition

7.14
gives that

\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi))\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{\epsilon/4}(\Pi)
, so we obtain a

(\Pi(\Psi),\Pi(\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace with sample complexity
m=O\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}+\frac{n}{\epsilon^{2}\log n}\right)
.
Then
Lemma

7.9
yields a

(\Psi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace for

\rho=1-e^{-m/N}
, i. e. expected trace size

\rho N=O\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}+\frac{n}{\epsilon^{2}\log n}\right)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.15
.

Use a version of the tester for the 1-uniform
n
-block string from

Lemma

7.16
with success probability
5/6
, repeat it with
all symbols negated, and accept if either execution accepts.
If the input
x=u^{(1)}
, the first execution accepts with probability at least
5/6
, and
if
x=u^{(0)}
, the second execution accepts with probability at least
5/6
.
If
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)>\epsilon
, then
x
is far from both
u^{(1)}
and
u^{(0)}
,
so each execution only accepts with probability at most
1/6
, and by the union bound the
probability that
x
is accepted is at most
1/3
.
∎

Toward establishing the upper bound portion of
Theorem

1.7
, we introduce
the following definition. We say that
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
is a
type-1
n
-block string
if

x=1^{t_{1}}0^{t_{2}}1^{t_{3}}\dotsm\mathsf{par}(n)^{t_{n}}
for some choice of non-negative integers

t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}
. We say that
x
is a
type-0
n
-block string
if

x=0^{t_{1}}1^{t_{2}}0^{t_{3}}\dotsm(1-\mathsf{par}(n))^{t_{n}}
for some choice of non-negative
integers
t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}
.

Remark 7.17
.

A string
x
may be
both
a type-1
n
-block string and a type-0
n
-block string.
Moreover,
x
is an
n
-block string if and only if it is a type-1
n
-block string or
a type-0
n
-block string.

If
x
is a type-1
n
-block string, then
\psi^{-1}(x)
is supported within
[n]
, and if

x
is a type-0
n
-block string, then
\psi^{-1}(x)
is supported within
\{2,3,\dotsc,n+1\}
.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
is even and divides
N
, and let
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\Psi_{1}
contain only the 1-uniform
n
-block string
u^{(1)}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,
and let
\Psi_{2}
contain all type-1
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
that are
\epsilon
-far from

\Psi_{1}
in (relative) edit distance.
There is a
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace of expected size
\rho N=\widetilde{O}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi_{1}
contain only the uniform distribution
\pi^{*}=\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)})
over
[n]
,
and let
\Pi_{2}
contain the distributions over
[n]
that are
\epsilon/2
-far from uniform in
edit distance. By
Theorem

4.1
together with
6.29
,
there is a
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},2/3)
-distribution tester
under the parity trace, which we may assume is Poissonized by
Proposition

A.1
,
with sample complexity
\widetilde{O}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.

We claim that
\Pi(\Psi_{1})\subseteq\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi(\Psi_{2})\subseteq\Pi_{2}
. First, let

\pi\in\Pi(\Psi_{1})
, so that necessarily
\pi=\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)})
. Then indeed

\pi
is uniform over
[n]
, so
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
. Now, suppose
\pi\in\Pi(\Psi_{2})
, so that

\pi=\psi^{-1}(x)
for some
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
such that
x
is a type-1
n
-block string and

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(1)})>\epsilon
. It follows that
\pi
is supported within
[n]

by
Remark

7.17
and, by
Lemma

7.6
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{*})\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),\psi(\pi^{*}))=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(1)})>\epsilon/2
, so
\pi\in\Pi_{2}
.

Therefore we obtain a
(\Pi(\Psi_{1}),\Pi(\Psi_{2}),2/3)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace with sample complexity
m=\widetilde{O}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.
Then
Lemma

7.9
yields a

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace for
\rho=1-e^{-m/N}
,
i. e. expected trace size

\rho N=\widetilde{O}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.
∎

Now, we obtain the single-trace upper bound portion of
Theorem

1.7
:

Theorem 7.19
.

Let
N,n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
is even and divides
N
, and let
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\Psi_{1}
contain only the uniform
n
-block strings
u^{(1)},u^{(0)}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,
and let
\Psi_{2}
contain all
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
that are
\epsilon
-far from
\Psi_{1}

in (relative) edit distance.
There is a
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using one trace of expected size
\rho N=\widetilde{O}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.

Proof.

The key observation is that the algorithm
A
obtained in
Lemma

7.18

is invariant to negation of all the symbols in the input: it is a combination of the
distribution tester from
Theorem

4.1
, which only depends on run lengths, and the

Poissonize
algorithm from
Proposition

7.7
, which transforms the input
in the same way regardless of the values of the symbols. Formally, for
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and
letting
\overline{x}
denote the string obtained by negating every symbol in
x
, the
outputs
A(x)
and
A(\overline{x})
are identically distributed.

Therefore, we claim that
A
is also a
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester. Indeed if
the input
x\in\Psi_{1}
, then either
x=u^{(1)}
and
A
accepts with probability at least

2/3
by
Lemma

7.18
, or
x=u^{(0)}
and therefore

\overline{x}=u^{(1)}
, so again
A
accepts. On the other hand, if
x\in\Psi_{2}
then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(1)})>\epsilon
and

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\overline{x},u^{(1)})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(0)})>\epsilon
. Moreover,
either
x
or
\overline{x}
is a type-1
n
-block string, so
A
rejects with probability
at least
2/3
.
∎

We remark that the probability of success
2/3
in the results above could be replaced with any
higher constant without affecting the asymptotic bounds, by using the distribution tester under the
parity trace with correspondingly better constant probability of success. Alternatively,
multiple independent traces may be used to amplify the result into the high probability regime.

7.2
Multiple-Trace Upper Bound

The ability to make inferences from multiple independent traces is a central component of the
trace reconstruction model. Accordingly, we would like to test the class of uniform
n
-block
strings with smaller traces than afforded by our single-trace results, at the cost of taking more
traces. The main idea is to
reduce
to the single-trace case by concatenating the
k
traces
together, and thinking of the result as one trace from the input string copied
k
times.

For any strings
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{*}
and integer
k>0
, denote by
x\circ y
the concatenation
of
x
and
y
, and by
x^{\circ k}
the concatenation
x\circ\dotsm\circ x
with
k

terms in total.

Proposition 7.20
.

There exists a universal constant
c>0
such the following holds.
Let
N,n,k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
is even and divides
N
.
Let
u=u^{(1)}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
be the 1-uniform
n
-block string and let
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
be a type-1

n
-block string. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(u^{\circ k},x^{\circ k})\geq c\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(u,x)\,.

Proof.

Let
\pi_{u}\vcentcolon=\psi^{-1}(u)
and
\pi_{x}\vcentcolon=\psi^{-1}(x)
, so that
\pi_{u}
and
\pi_{x}

are supported within
[n]
and
\psi(\pi_{u})=u,\psi(\pi_{x})=x
.
Define
\pi_{u}^{k}
as the following distribution on
\mathbb{N}
: for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

and
i\in[n]
,

\pi_{u}^{k}(tn+i)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\pi_{u}(i)/k,&\text{if $t\leq k-1$}\\
0,&\text{if $t\geq k$.}\end{cases}

Define
\pi_{x}^{k}
analogously. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{u}^{k},\pi_{x}^{k})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{u},\pi_{x})
, since the entries of the former
are aligned in each of the
k
rescaled copies of the latter.
Also, the entries of
\pi_{u}^{k},\pi_{x}^{k}
are integer multiples of
1/kN
, and their
corresponding strings over
\{0,1\}^{kN}
satisfy
\psi(\pi_{u}^{k})=u^{\circ k}
and

\psi(\pi_{x}^{k})=x^{\circ k}
.

By
Lemma

7.6
, we have

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(u^{\circ k},x^{\circ k})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi_{u}^{k}),\psi(\pi_{x}^{k}))\geq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{u}^{k},\pi_{x}^{k})
.
Since
n
is even,
u^{\circ k}
is the 1-uniform
kn
-block string, and therefore

\pi_{u}^{k}
is uniformly distributed on
[kn]
. It is also clear that

\pi_{x}^{k}
is supported within
[kn]
. It follows from
Lemma

6.31
that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{u}^{k},\pi_{x}^{k})\geq c^{\prime}\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{u}^{k},\pi_{x}^{k})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{u},\pi_{x})
for some universal constant
c^{\prime}>0
.
It is easy to see from the definition of edit distance that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{u},\pi_{x})\geq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{u},\pi_{x})
. Finally, applying

Lemma

7.6
again yields

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{u},\pi_{x})\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi_{u}),\psi(\pi_{x}))
.
Recalling that
\psi(\pi_{u})=u
and
\psi(\psi_{x})=x
, this concludes the proof.
∎

We first use the result above to show a multiple-trace upper bound for testing the 1-uniform strings
among the type-1
n
-block strings, and then generalize this result to both types of (uniform)
strings to obtain the upper bound portion of
Theorem

1.7
.

Lemma 7.21
.

Let
N,n,k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
is even and divides
N
, and let
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\Psi_{1}
contain only the 1-uniform
n
-block string
u^{(1)}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,
and let
\Psi_{2}
be the set of type-1
n
-block strings that are
\epsilon
-far from
\Psi_{1}

in (relative) edit distance. Then there is a
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using

k
traces of expected size
\rho N=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n^{4/5}}{k^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{k}\epsilon^{2}}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
u\vcentcolon=u^{(1)}
for convenience of notation.
Let
\Psi^{\prime}_{1}
contain only the 1-uniform
kn
-block string
u^{\circ k}\in\{0,1\}^{kN}
, and let

\Psi^{\prime}_{2}
be the set of type-1
kn
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{kN}
that are
c\epsilon
-far from

\Psi^{\prime}_{1}
in relative edit distance, where
c
is the constant from
Proposition

7.20
.

By
Lemma

7.18
we obtain algorithm
A
, a

(\Psi^{\prime}_{1},\Psi^{\prime}_{2},\rho^{\prime},2/3)
-trace tester using one trace of expected size

\rho^{\prime}(kN)=\widetilde{O}((kn)^{4/5}/\epsilon^{4/5}+\sqrt{kn}/\epsilon^{2})
.
Our algorithm
B
, which will be a
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using
k

traces, works as follows:

1.

Obtain
k
independent traces
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}
of expected size

\rho N=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n^{4/5}}{k^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{k}\epsilon^{2}}\right)
.

2.

Return the output of
A
on
x_{1}\circ\dotsm\circ x_{k}
.

Let
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
be the unknown input to
B
. If
x\in\Psi_{1}
, then
x=u
and hence

x^{\circ k}\in\Psi^{\prime}_{1}
. On the other hand, if
x\in\Psi_{2}
, then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u)>\epsilon
and we use
Proposition

7.20
to conclude that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x^{\circ k},u^{\circ k})>c\epsilon
, so

x^{\circ k}\in\Psi^{\prime}_{2}
. Moreover, the input
\bm{x}_{1}\circ\dotsm\circ\bm{x}_{k}
to
A

is distributed as a trace from
x^{\circ k}
of expected size
k(\rho N)
, i. e. deletion rate

\rho
. Therefore
A
will produce the correct output (and hence so will
B
) with probability
at least
2/3
as long as we satisfy

k\rho N\geq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{(kn)^{4/5}}{\epsilon^{4/5}}+\frac{\sqrt{kn}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)\,,

which holds when

\rho N\geq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n^{4/5}}{k^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{k}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\,.\qed

We now obtain the upper bound portion of
Theorem

1.7
.

Theorem 7.22
.

Let
N,n,k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n
is even and divides
N
, and let
\epsilon>0
. Let

\Psi_{1}
contain only the uniform
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
, and let
\Psi_{2}
be the set of

n
-block strings that are
\epsilon
-far from
\Psi_{1}
in (relative) edit distance. Then there is
a
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using
k
traces of expected size
\rho N=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{n^{4/5}}{k^{1/5}\epsilon^{4/5}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{k}\epsilon^{2}}\right)
.

Proof.

The argument is identical to the proof of
Theorem

7.19
. Letting
A

be the algorithm from
Lemma

7.21
, we observe that
the output
A(x)
is distributed identically to
A(\overline{x})
. Therefore
A
is also a

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using
k
traces: if
x\in\Psi_{1}
, then either

x=u^{(1)}
or
\overline{x}=u^{(1)}
, so
A
accepts,
and if
x\in\Psi_{2}
, then both
x
and
\overline{x}
are far from
u^{(1)}
and moreover
either
x
or
\overline{x}
is a type-1
n
-block string, so
A
rejects.
∎

7.3
Lower Bounds

We wish to reduce from distribution testing under the parity trace to testing properties of
strings in the trace reconstruction model. We define a “trace splitting” procedure,
which takes a parity trace from distribution
\pi
and produces
k
strings that look like
independent traces from string
\psi(\pi)
.

Proposition 7.23

(Poissonized trace splitting)
.

There exists a randomized algorithm
Split
that satisfies the following.
Let
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\rho>0
satisfy

\rho<\frac{1}{20\cdot\sqrt{kN}}
. Let
\pi
be any probability distribution over
\mathbb{N}

whose densities are integer multiples of
1/N
. Then on inputs
N,k\text{ and }\rho
,

Split
draws a parity trace of size

\mathsf{Poi}\left(k\cdot\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\cdot N\right)
from
\pi
and outputs
a sequence of
k
binary strings satisfying the following. Let
\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k}

be the random variables denoting the output of
Split
(over the randomness of the
parity trace and internal randomness of the algorithm), and let

\bm{y}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{y}_{k}
be such that each
\bm{y}_{i}
is an independent trace from
\psi(\pi)

with expected size
\rho N
. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}((\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k}),(\bm{y}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{y}_{k}))<1/100\,.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\vcentcolon=\rho/(1-\rho)
. The algorithm
Split
proceeds as follows:

1.

Draw a parity trace
T
of size
\mathsf{Poi}(k\lambda N)
from
\pi
;

2.

Initialize empty strings
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}
;

3.

For each symbol
b
in
T
from left to right, append
b
to
x_{i}
where

i
is drawn uniformly at random from
[k]
;

4.

Return the strings
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k})
.

For each
i\in[k]
and
j\in[N]
, let
\bm{X}_{i,j}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(\lambda)
independently.
Define random variables
\bm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}^{\prime}_{k}
as follows: for each

i\in[k]
,

\bm{x}^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\pi)_{1}^{\bm{X}_{i,1}}\psi(\pi)_{2}^{\bm{X}_{i,2}}\dotsm\psi(\pi)_{N}^{\bm{X}_{i,N}}\,.

We claim that
(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k})
is distributed identically to

(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}^{\prime}_{k})
. Indeed, first recall that the parity trace
\bm{T}
from
Step 1 is distributed as follows:

\bm{T}=\psi(\pi)_{1}^{\bm{A}_{1}}\psi(\pi)_{2}^{\bm{A}_{2}}\dotsm\psi(\pi)_{N}^{\bm{A}_{N}}\,,

where
\bm{A}_{j}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(k\lambda)
independently for each
j\in[N]
.
Then, Step 3 is equivalent to splitting each
\bm{A}_{j}
into random variables

(\bm{A}_{1,j},\dotsc,\bm{A}_{k,j})\sim\mathsf{Multinomial}(\bm{A}_{j},(1/k,\dotsc,1/k))
,
and producing each
\bm{x}_{i}
by concatenation:

\bm{x}_{i}=\psi(\pi)_{1}^{\bm{A}_{i,1}}\psi(\pi)_{2}^{\bm{A}_{i,2}}\dotsm\psi(\pi)_{N}^{\bm{A}_{i,N}}\,.

It follows from standard arguments that
\bm{A}_{1,j},\dotsc,\bm{A}_{k,j}
are i.i.d.
random variables distributed as
\bm{A}_{i,j}\sim\mathsf{Poi}(\lambda)
for each
i\in[k]
.
Therefore the
\bm{A}_{i,j}
are distributed identically to the
\bm{X}_{i,j}
, and so

(\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k})
is distributed identically to
(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x^{\prime}}_{k})
.

Now, for each
i\in[k]
and
j\in[N]
, let
\bm{Y}_{i,j}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\rho)
independently.
By definition of trace, we have

\bm{y}_{i}=\psi(\pi)_{1}^{\bm{Y}_{i,1}}\psi(\pi)_{2}^{\bm{Y}_{i,2}}\dotsm\psi(\pi)_{N}^{\bm{Y}_{i,N}}\,.

Therefore, we will be done if we can show that the
\bm{X}_{i,j}
are sufficiently similar to
the
\bm{Y}_{i,j}
.

Concretely, fix some
i\in[k]
and
j\in[N]
, and let
\bm{X}=\bm{X}_{i,j}
and

\bm{Y}=\bm{Y}_{i,j}
for convenience.
We claim that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})\leq\lambda^{2}
.
Indeed, first, note that the distribution of
\bm{X}
conditional on
\bm{X}\leq 1
is identical
to that of
\bm{Y}
:

\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bm{X}=1\;\;\mid\;\;\bm{X}\leq 1\right]=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}=1\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}=0\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}=1\right]}=\frac{\lambda e^{-\lambda}}{e^{-\lambda}+\lambda e^{-\lambda}}=\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}=\rho=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{Y}=1\right]\,,

and therefore

\displaystyle\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}\geq 2\right]=1-\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}=0\right]-\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{X}=1\right]=1-e^{-\lambda}-\lambda e^{-\lambda}=1-e^{-\lambda}(1+\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq 1-(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)=\lambda^{2}\,.

Finally, since the
\bm{X}_{i,j}
and
\bm{Y}_{i,j}
are all mutually independent, we obtain

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}((\bm{X}_{i,j})_{i\in[k],j\in[N]},(\bm{Y}_{i,j})_{i\in[k],j\in[N]})\leq kN\cdot\lambda^{2}<kN(2\rho)^{2}<1/100\,,

and thus
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}((\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k}),(\bm{y}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{y}_{k}))<1/100

by the data processing inequality.
∎

We use this procedure to give our general lower bound for trace testing:

Lemma 7.24
.

Let
\alpha>0
.
Let
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
be properties of probability distributions over
\mathbb{N}

whose densities are integer multiples of
1/N
, and such that

(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\alpha)
-testing under the parity trace requires sample complexity at
least
m
. Then any
(\Psi(\Pi_{1}),\Psi(\Pi_{2}),\rho,\alpha+1/100)
-trace tester using
k

traces of expected size
\rho N
must satisfy

k\rho N=\Omega\left(\min\left(m,\sqrt{kN}\right)\right)
.

Proof.

Suppose
B
is a
(\Psi(\Pi_{1}),\Psi(\Pi_{2}),\alpha+1/100)
-trace tester using
k
traces
of expected size
\rho N
, and suppose
k\rho N<\sqrt{kN}/20
. Then

\rho<\frac{1}{20\sqrt{kN}}
and our goal is to show that
k\rho N=\Omega(m)
. We do
so by constructing an algorithm
A
in the parity trace model and showing that
A
is a

(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\alpha)
-tester under the parity trace with sample complexity
O(k\rho N)
.
The algorithm works as follows:

1.

Take a parity trace of size
\mathsf{Poi}\left(k\cdot\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\cdot N\right)
.

2.

Apply
Split
to obtain
k
binary strings
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}
.

3.

Return the output of
B
on inputs
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}
.

Note that
A
has sample complexity
O(k\rho N)
. Let
\pi
be the input distribution;
recall that if
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
then
\psi(\pi)\in\Psi(\Pi_{1})
, and if
\pi\in\Pi_{2}
then

\psi(\pi)\in\Psi(\Pi_{2})
.
Let
\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k}
be the inputs fed to
B
, and let

\bm{y}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{y}_{k}
be mutually independent traces from
\psi(\pi)
, each with
expected size
\rho N
. By
Proposition

7.23
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}((\bm{x}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{x}_{k}),(\bm{y}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{y}_{k}))<1/100
, and we know
that
B
would succeed (i. e. accept if
\psi(\pi)\in\Psi(\Pi_{1})
, reject if

\psi(\pi)\in\Psi(\Pi_{2})
) with probability at least
\alpha+1/100
if it were given inputs

\bm{y}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{y}_{k}
. Therefore
A
succeeds with probability at least
\alpha
,
and
k\rho N=\Omega(m)
.
∎

We now obtain the lower bounds stated in

Theorems

1.7
and
1.9
.

Theorem 7.25
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following holds.
Let
n,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho,\epsilon>0
be such that
n
is even and divides
N
,

\epsilon
is smaller than some sufficiently small universal constant, and

N\geq C\cdot\max\left\{(n/\epsilon)^{8/5},n/\epsilon^{4}\right\}
.
Let
\Psi_{1}
contain only the uniform
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
, and let
\Psi_{2}
be the set
of all
n
-block strings that are
\epsilon
-far from
\Psi_{1}
in (relative) edit distance.
Then any
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using
k
traces of expected size
\rho N

must satisfy
k\rho N=\widetilde{\Omega}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi_{1}
contain only the uniform distribution

\pi^{*}=\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)})
on
[n]
. Let
\epsilon^{*}\vcentcolon=8\epsilon
for convenience.
Using the value
\epsilon^{\prime}\in(\epsilon^{*},2\epsilon^{*}]
defined below, let

\Pi_{2}
be the set of distributions over
[n]
that 1) are
(\epsilon^{\prime}/c)
-far from
uniform in total variation distance (where
c
is the constant from

Lemma

6.31
); and 2) have all densities in the set

\left\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{1-4\epsilon^{\prime}/c}{n},\frac{1+4\epsilon^{\prime}/c}{n}\right\}
.
Note that, by
Lemma

6.31
, every distribution in
\Pi_{2}
is

\epsilon^{\prime}
-far from uniform in
edit
distance.

We define
\epsilon^{\prime}
as the smallest
\epsilon^{\prime}>\epsilon^{*}
such that
\frac{4\epsilon^{\prime}/c}{n}

is an integer multiple of
1/N
, and claim that
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 2\epsilon^{*}
. Indeed, we have

\frac{4\epsilon^{*}/c}{n}\geq\frac{1}{N}\iff N\geq\frac{cn}{4\epsilon^{*}}\,,

which holds by assumption for sufficiently large
C
, so there exists an integer multiple of

1/N
between
\frac{4\epsilon^{*}/c}{n}
and
\frac{8\epsilon^{*}/c}{n}
.

Since the proof of
Theorem

5.1
only uses distributions of the form of
\Pi_{2}
,
it follows that
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},51/100)
-distribution testing under the parity trace requires

\widetilde{\Omega}\left((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2}\right)
samples.
Therefore, noticing that by assumption we have

\sqrt{kN}=\Omega\left((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2}\right)
,

Lemma

7.24
gives that any

(\Psi(\Pi_{1}),\Psi(\Pi_{2}),52/100)
-trace tester using
k
traces of expected size
\rho N

must satisfy
k\rho N=\widetilde{\Omega}((n/\epsilon)^{4/5}+\sqrt{n}/\epsilon^{2})
.
The result will follow if we show that
\Psi(\Pi_{1})\subseteq\Psi_{1}
and

\Psi(\Pi_{2})\subseteq\Psi_{2}
.

First, suppose
x\in\Psi(\Pi_{1})
. Then
x=\psi(\pi^{*})=\psi(\psi^{-1}(u^{(1)}))=u^{(1)}

and hence
x\in\Psi_{1}
.

Second, suppose
x\in\Psi(\Pi_{2})
, so
x=\psi(\pi)
for some
\pi\in\Pi_{2}

We claim that
x\in\Psi_{2}
. It is clear that
x
is an
n
-block string, so it remains to
show that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi_{1})>\epsilon
.
Since
\pi\in\Pi_{2}
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{*})\geq\epsilon^{\prime}/c
and thus,
as was observed,
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{*})\geq\epsilon^{\prime}>\epsilon^{*}
.
Then by
Lemma

7.6
,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(1)})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),\psi(\pi^{*}))\geq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi,\pi^{*})>\epsilon^{*}>\epsilon
. We also need to show that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,u^{(0)})>\epsilon
. We consider two cases.

First, suppose
\epsilon^{*}\geq 4/n
. By the triangle inequality,

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),u^{(1)})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),u^{(0)})+\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(u^{(0)},u^{(1)})=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),u^{(0)})+2/n
.
Thus
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),u^{(0)})>\epsilon^{*}-2/n>\epsilon
.

Second, suppose
\epsilon^{*}<4/n
, so
1/2n>\epsilon^{*}/8
. The first block of
\psi(\pi)

has length at least
N\cdot\frac{1-4\epsilon^{\prime}/c}{n}
by construction, and for sufficiently
small
\epsilon
, this is at least

N\cdot\frac{1}{2n}
. Moreover this first block of
\psi(\pi)
is a block of 1s, whereas
the first block of
u^{(0)}
is a block of 0s of length
N\cdot\frac{1}{n}
. Therefore

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi),u^{(0)})\geq 1/2n>\epsilon^{*}/8=\epsilon
, as desired.
Therefore
x\in\Psi_{2}
, completing the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.26
.

The following holds for all sufficiently small constant
\epsilon>0
.
There exists a function
N(n)=\Theta(n^{2})
such that, for all
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
and

N\geq N(n)
, the following is true. Let
\Psi
be the set of
n
-block strings in
\{0,1\}^{N}
.
Then any
(\Psi,\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi),\rho,2/3)
-trace tester using
k
traces of
expected size
\rho N
must satisfy
k\rho N=\Omega(n/\log n)
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi_{1}
be the set of probability distributions over
\mathbb{N}
with support size
at most
n
. By
Theorem

6.38
and the lower bound on testing
n
-alternating
functions from
[
BFH21
]
, any

(\Pi_{1},\allowbreak\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{2\epsilon}(\Pi_{1}),\allowbreak 51/100)
-distribution
tester under the parity trace must have sample complexity
\Omega(n/\log n)
. In fact, a stronger
statement holds: for some sufficiently large universal constant
C>0
, let
\Pi_{2}
be the
restriction of
\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{edit}}_{2\epsilon}(\Pi_{1})
to those distributions with support size at most

Cn
. Then any
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},51/100)
-distribution tester under the parity trace must have sample
complexity
\Omega(n/\log n)
. This is because the lower bound on testing
n
-alternating
functions from
[
BFH21
]
is proved via a reduction from the
support size distinction

problem
[
VV11
,
WY19
]
, and the hard examples for that problem have support size linear in
n
.

To apply
Lemma

7.24
, we need the all densities to be integer
multiples of
1/N
. Let
\Pi^{\prime}_{1}
be a property obtained by taking each distribution

\pi\in\Pi_{1}
and rounding all the densities of
\pi
to a multiple of
1/N
in such a way
that we obtain another probability distribution
\pi^{\prime}
.
Let
\Pi^{\prime}_{2}
be a property obtained from
\Pi_{2}
in the same way. Then every

\pi\in\Pi_{1}
satisfies
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\Pi^{\prime}_{1})\leq\tfrac{1}{N}\cdot O(n)
and every

\pi\in\Pi_{2}
satisfies
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\Pi^{\prime}_{2})\leq\tfrac{1}{N}\cdot O(n)
.

We claim that any
(\Pi^{\prime}_{1},\Pi^{\prime}_{2},60/100)
-distribution tester under the parity trace must
have sample complexity
\Omega(n/\log n)
. Suppose
A
is a

(\Pi^{\prime}_{1},\Pi^{\prime}_{2},60/100)
-distribution tester under the parity trace with sample complexity

m\leq n/\log n
. Then
A
is a
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},55/100)
-distribution tester under the parity
trace, as we now prove. For any input
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
, there exists
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\prime}_{1}
such that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=O(n/N)
. Then the random variables

\bm{S}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi,m)
and
\bm{S^{\prime}}\sim\mathsf{samp}(\pi^{\prime},m)
satisfy

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\bm{S},\bm{S^{\prime}})\leq m\cdot\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=O(\frac{n^{2}}{N\log n})=o(1)
.
Since
A
accepts
\pi^{\prime}
with probability at least
60/100
,
it accepts
\pi
with probability at least
60/100-o(1)\geq 55/100
.
The same argument holds for
\Pi_{2}
and

\Pi^{\prime}_{2}
, and therefore
A
is a
(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},55/100)
-distribution tester under the
parity trace. Hence
m=\Omega(n/\log n)
, proving the claim.

Now, noticing that by assumption we have
\sqrt{Nk}=\Omega(n)
,

Lemma

7.24
gives that any

(\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{1}),\allowbreak\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{2}),\allowbreak 61/100)
-trace
tester using
k
traces of expected size

\rho N
must satisfy
k\rho N=\Omega(n/\log n)
. The result will follow if we show
that
\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{1})\subseteq\Psi
and
\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{2})\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi)
.

First, let
x\in\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{1})
, so
x=\psi(\pi^{\prime}_{1})
for some
\pi^{\prime}_{1}\in\Pi^{\prime}_{1}
.
Since the process to obtain
\Pi^{\prime}_{1}
from
\Pi_{1}
does not add any new elements to the support
of the distributions, every
\pi^{\prime}_{1}\in\Pi^{\prime}_{1}
has support size at most
n
, and thus

x
is an
n
-block string. Hence
\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{1})\subseteq\Psi
.

Second, let
x\in\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{2})
, so
x=\psi(\pi^{\prime}_{2})
for some
\pi^{\prime}_{2}\in\Pi^{\prime}_{2}
.
We need to show that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)>\epsilon
. First, we claim that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi^{\prime}_{2},\Pi_{1})>\epsilon
, i. e.
\pi^{\prime}_{2}
is
\epsilon
-far in edit distance
from any distribution (not necessarily rounded) with support size at most
n
.
Suppose for a contradiction there exists
\pi_{1}\in\Pi_{1}
such that

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})\leq\epsilon
. Let
\pi_{2}\in\Pi_{2}
be some distribution
satisfying
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\pi_{2},\pi^{\prime}_{2})=O(n/N)
, which exists by construction of
\Pi^{\prime}_{2}
.
Then
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{2},\pi^{\prime}_{2})=O(n/N)
and, since
\epsilon
is a constant, we
may assume that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{2},\pi^{\prime}_{2})<\epsilon/2
. Then

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})+\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi^{\prime}_{2},\pi_{2})<\epsilon+\epsilon/2<2\epsilon\,,

contradicting the definition of
\Pi_{2}
. Therefore
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi^{\prime}_{2},\Pi_{1})>\epsilon
.
Now, let
y\in\Psi
. We claim that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,y)>\epsilon
.
Since
y
is an
n
-block string, we have
\psi^{-1}(y)\in\Pi_{1}
, and hence

\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi^{\prime}_{2},\psi^{-1}(y))>\epsilon
. Then
Lemma

7.6
gives
that
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,y)=\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(\psi(\pi^{\prime}_{2}),\psi(\psi^{-1}(y)))\geq\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{edit}}(\pi^{\prime}_{2},\psi^{-1}(y))>\epsilon
. Hence
\mathsf{dist}_{\mathsf{rel-edit}}(x,\Psi)>\epsilon

and therefore
\Psi(\Pi^{\prime}_{2})\subseteq\textsc{far}^{\mathsf{rel-edit}}_{\epsilon}(\Psi)
, concluding the proof.
∎
